### [26] [A computable representation of the physical laboratory enables verifiable workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03621)
*Xiaobo Li,Luyao Ge,Xiaohui Li,Lulu Guo,Ming Mao,Jiwang Zheng,Wenting Guan,Xin Yang,Yi Luo,Jun Jiang,Linjiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A computable representation of physical laboratories using typed research objects, capability-bound operations, and a workflow algebra enables autonomous scientific discovery through agentic robotics and simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To make science computable by establishing representations of both scientific knowledge and the physical world where scientific claims are tested, enabling autonomous scientific discovery.

Method: Created a computable representation using typed research objects, capability-bound operations, and a compositional workflow algebra. Implemented in a modular agentic robotic laboratory by binding formal operations to executable Function Skills. Used stateful simulation to propagate object transformations and verify operation preconditions before dispatch.

Result: Successfully implemented the representation in a modular agentic robotic laboratory, enabling generation of capability-relative workflows for diverse scientific intents. Stateful simulation verified operation preconditions and laboratory constraints before dispatch.

Conclusion: The proposed representation and engineering framework establish a general computational interface between agent reasoning and capability-bound physical transformations, providing a foundation for end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery.

Abstract: Making science computable requires representations of both scientific knowledge and the physical world in which scientific claims are tested. A computable representation of the physical laboratory is established through typed research objects, capability-bound operations and a compositional workflow algebra. It provides the physical-world counterpart to machine-readable knowledge, expressing workflows as programs over evolving laboratory states with explicit dependencies, decisions, iteration and concurrency. The representation was implemented in a modular agentic robotic laboratory by binding formal operations to executable Function Skills. For diverse scientific intents, capability-relative workflows were generated, while stateful simulation propagated object transformations and verified operation preconditions and laboratory constraints before dispatch. The proposed representation and its engineering framework jointly establish a general computational interface between agent reasoning and capability-bound physical transformations, providing a foundation for end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery.

</details>


### [27] [Analysis of Prompt Engineering for Drug Toxicity Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03635)
*Mia MacGregor,Aakash Welgamage Don,Mark Bartlett*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how prompt engineering affects LLM performance in drug toxicity prediction, finding that LLM natural variance outweighs prompt fine-tuning, but chemoinformatic feature extraction yields substantial performance improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical drug trials are expensive (up to £1.3M in UK) with 90% failure rate, and toxicity is a major cause. Current testing is time and cost intensive. LLMs show promise for toxicity prediction but are sensitive to prompt phrasing variations, raising concerns about reliability and reproducibility.

Method: Proposed method analyzes prompt engineering for drug toxicity prediction by: 1) Using LLMs to identify significant chemical properties for toxicity prediction, 2) Investigating prompt variations (job role, prompt structuring, rule interpretation), 3) Generating datasets with LLM-identified features, 4) Passing these to machine learning algorithms, and 5) Comparing with chemoinformatic code-extracted features.

Result: Experiments show that natural variance in LLM responses outweighs any benefits from prompt fine-tuning. However, substantial performance improvements occur when using chemoinformatic code to extract features instead of LLM-generated values. The variance from prompt engineering had minimal impact compared to inherent LLM variability.

Conclusion: The proposed prompt engineering analysis methodology is broadly applicable across bioinformatics domains. While prompt phrasing has limited impact due to LLM natural variance, using domain-specific tools (like chemoinformatic code) for feature extraction significantly improves model performance for drug toxicity prediction tasks.

Abstract: Clinical trials in the UK can cost up to £1.3 million, with approximately 90% drug failure rate. Toxicity is a major contributing factor in drug failure. Testing is time and cost intensive. In recent years, the use of artificial intelligence has been increasingly explored to aid in the prediction of drug toxicity, with extensive use of large language models (LLMs). However, LLMs can show considerable variation when minor changes are made to prompts, which raises concerns about their sensitivity to prompt engineering. Prompt engineering is used to optimise a prompt given to an LLM to generate the desired output. This paper proposes a method to analyse prompt engineering for drug toxicity prediction. The aim of the paper is to investigate the importance of prompt phrasing for drug toxicity prediction. LLMs were prompted to identify chemical properties of significance when predicting drug toxicity. Prompts were constructed to investigate; job role, prompt structuring, and rule interpretation. LLMs were then used to generate datasets, using the identified features from initial prompting, which were then passed to machine learning algorithms. The experiments show that the natural variance which occurs in LLMs outweighs any fine-tuning of prompts. There were, however, substantial improvements in model performance when using chemoinformatic code to extract features instead of using LLM-generated values. The proposed analysis methodology is applicable to a wide range of prompt types across different areas of bioinformatics.

</details>


### [28] [Synthetic Semantic Supervision for Contrastive Code Representation Learning in Small Transformers: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03702)
*Kenneth Paulsen,Florian Tambon,Mike Papadakis,Shin Yoo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small code encoders pretrained with synthetic natural-language descriptions (discarded at inference) outperform pretraining baselines on multiple code understanding tasks and match larger LLMs on classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current code embedding methods rely on either expensive human-written docstrings (inconsistent and labor-intensive) or hard-to-collect execution traces (setting-specific). Need a scalable alternative for functional code understanding.

Method: Contrastive pretraining of small transformer encoders using synthetically generated natural-language descriptions emphasizing code functionality/intent, paired with code in a dual-encoder framework (descriptions discarded at inference time).

Result: Synthetic semantic supervision yields significant gains over pretraining baselines on 5/8 tasks (parity on 2 more); fine-tuned models match/exceed zero-shot models 100x larger on classification; matches execution-aware supervision at same pretraining data scale.

Conclusion: Synthetically supervised contrastive pretraining offers a scalable, effective alternative to existing code representation paradigms, achieving strong performance without expensive human annotations or execution traces.

Abstract: General-purpose code embeddings power tools for code search, classification, and retrieval. Compact transformer encoders for code typically rely on either human-written docstrings (labor-intensive and inconsistent) or mined structural signals such as execution traces (setting-specific and costly to collect). We empirically study an alternative: contrastive pretraining of small encoders with synthetically generated natural-language descriptions emphasizing code functionality and intent, paired with code in a dual-encoder framework at training and discarded at inference. We benchmark this approach against pretraining-based baselines, generalist LLMs, and embedding-specific models on eight retrieval, classification, and generation tasks across C, C++, and Java. Synthetic semantic supervision yields statistically significant gains over pretraining baselines of the same inference-time size on five of eight tasks, with parity on two more; once fine-tuned, it matches or exceeds zero-shot models two orders of magnitude larger on classification, and it stays on par with execution-aware supervision at matched pretraining data, suggesting a scalable, effective alternative to existing code-representation paradigms.

</details>


### [29] [Counterfactual Routing Using Integer Programming with Constraint Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03707)
*Daniël Vos,Sterre Lutz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A competition entry that models counterfactual route explanations as an integer program, achieving 4th in solution quality and fastest runtime (9s avg) among submissions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The competition aimed to generate counterfactual explanations for shortest path problems, enabling explanations like "Your route would have been optimal if road X weren't a bicycle path." This helps make routing decisions more transparent and understandable to users.

Method: The solution models the counterfactual routing problem as an integer programming formulation, then iteratively adds constraints until an exact solution is found. The iterative approach helps find minimal changes to make a user's route optimal.

Result: In the final evaluation, the method ranked 4th in solution quality and achieved the fastest runtime on every test instance with an average of 9.0 seconds, compared to 118.8 seconds for the next-fastest submission.

Conclusion: The integer programming approach provides an efficient solution for counterfactual route explanations, balancing solution quality with computational speed, making it practical for real-world applications where fast explanations are needed.

Abstract: We present our submission to the IJCAI 2025 'Counterfactual Routing Competition' (CRC 25). The goal of the competition is to find counterfactual explanations for the shortest path problem. This requires deciding what the minimal changes to a road network would make a route chosen by the user the optimal route. This enables explanations such as "Your suggested route would indeed have been optimal, if road X were not a bicycle path." Our solution models the problem as an integer program, iteratively incorporating constraints until an exact solution is found. In the final evaluation on held-out test instances, our method ranked fourth in solution quality and obtained its solution fastest on every instance, with an average runtime of 9.0 seconds compared to 118.8 seconds for the next-fastest submission.

</details>


### [30] [Artificial Intelligence for Energy Optimization in Data Centers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03716)
*Mohammed Basharath Ullah,Summaiya Unnisa Begum,Mohammed Nadeem Ullah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies critical gaps in AI optimization research for data centers, where control studies ignore environmental impacts and sustainability studies ignore workload dynamics, leading to unrankable methods and proposes CLEAR-DC, a coupled framework for comprehensive evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI optimization research for data centers suffers from a fundamental disconnect: control studies model workload as external without considering environmental impacts, while sustainability studies treat infrastructure as static without considering workload dynamics, making methods uncomparable and incomplete.

Method: Conducted systematic literature review of 194 papers, coded 63 of them, analyzed 28 primary control-oriented studies. Identified validation gaps, measurement limitations, and environmental oversights. Developed CLEAR-DC framework that couples control policies with workload demands through explicit elasticity, measuring net benefits including energy, carbon, water, embodied carbon with standardized reporting.

Result: Found that 18 of 28 control studies validated only in simulation, only 5 reached physical hardware. No studies accounted for water withdrawal or embodied carbon. Savings intervals across four technique families overlap completely, making method ranking impossible. Identified 10 recurring gaps and proposed CLEAR-DC framework with standardized reporting schema.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates critical methodological gaps in AI data center optimization research, showing current approaches cannot rank effectiveness due to incomplete metrics and validation. CLEAR-DC provides a comprehensive framework for future research that accounts for both control policies and workload demands while measuring full environmental impacts with standardized reporting.

Abstract: Data centers are increasingly optimized by artificial intelligence and, at the same time, increasingly loaded by it. The literature treats these as two unrelated problems: control studies model workload as an exogenous arrival process, while sustainability studies model infrastructure as a fixed multiplier. We screen roughly 194 papers retrieved through a documented protocol, code 63 of them, and report what the coding shows. Of 28 primary control-oriented studies, 18 are validated in simulation alone and 5 reach physical hardware or a production facility; none account for water withdrawal, and none account for embodied carbon. Reported savings intervals across four technique families overlap almost completely, which means the field cannot presently rank its own methods. Ten recurring gaps are scored for consequence and tractability, and we set out CLEAR-DC, a framework coupling a control-policy branch to a workload-demand branch through an explicit elasticity term, reads out net rather than direct benefit, and emits a schema-conformant record covering energy, carbon, water, embodied share and validation venue. The framework is an architectural and methodological proposal, not a trained system; the contribution we defend empirically is the corpus analysis and the reporting schema derived from it. Coding sheet, derived statistics and all result artifacts: https://github.com/Kimalice/AI-for-Energy-Optimization-in-Data-Centers-Closing-the-Optimizer-Load-Loop

</details>


### [31] [Proactive Service Agents: A Unified Decision Framework, Methods, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03727)
*Yan Tang,Tingyu Cao,Yuanbo Tang,Huaze Tang,Keer Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proactive service in LLM agents involves inferring service opportunities from environmental signals rather than waiting for explicit instructions, requiring decision-making about when and how to intervene while balancing risks like interruption and privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents primarily react to explicit user instructions, missing opportunities for proactive assistance. There's a need for agents that can infer service needs from incomplete environmental and user signals, but this requires careful consideration of intervention timing, authorization, and risk management.

Method: Formulates proactive service as a partially observable sequential decision process with authorization and risk constraints. Organizes methods along a decision pipeline (state/need estimation, intervention gating, action construction, feedback adaptation) and describes policy-construction components (prescribed, predictive, model-based, return optimizing). Normalizes decision units across different resource types and formalizes metrics for triggering, timing, calibration, and safety.

Result: Provides a comprehensive framework showing why offline classification performance doesn't predict deployment benefit and why long-term memory isn't defining for proactivity. Identifies that reliable proactive service requires calibrated incremental intervention value, verifiable authorization, recoverable execution, and counterfactual evidence.

Conclusion: Proactive service requires a formal decision-making framework that accounts for initiative timing, risk management, and authorization constraints. The paper provides a systematic approach to designing proactive agents that can effectively infer service opportunities while minimizing negative impacts like interruption or privacy violations.

Abstract: Large language model agents can plan, invoke tools, and modify external states, yet most systems still take an explicit user instruction as a fixed starting point. Proactive service moves the decision upstream: an agent must infer service opportunities from incomplete environmental and user signals, choose among remaining silent, asking, assisting, and acting, and account for interruption, misunderstanding, overreach, and privacy costs. This survey gives an operational definition centered on initiative and formulates the problem as a partially observable sequential decision process constrained by authorization and risk. The formulation represents timing, content, and delivery within one structured action, while making explicit the option value of waiting, the decision value of questions, and feedback-induced state changes. On this basis, we organize existing methods along one decision pipeline (state and need estimation, intervention gating, action construction, and feedback adaptation) and describe prescribed, predictive, model based, and return optimizing mechanisms as nonexclusive policy-construction components. We further normalize decision units and three-axis evidence descriptors across streaming dialogue, screen, video, software-engineering, and human-agent collaboration resources, and formalize metrics for triggering, timing, calibration, user burden, safety, and policy value. The synthesis shows why offline classification performance alone does not predict deployment benefit and why long-term memory is not a defining condition of proactivity. Reliable proactive service instead requires calibrated incremental intervention value, verifiable authorization, recoverable execution, and counterfactual evidence.

</details>


### [32] [Rethinking World Models for Safety-Critical Embodied Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03774)
*Kailang Ma,Heye Huang,Inhi Kim,Kitae Jang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: World models need to evolve from focusing on prediction accuracy to supporting safe decision-making by incorporating risk assessment, counterfactual reasoning, and epistemic uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current world models focus on predictive likelihood and visual fidelity but fail to preserve evidence needed for safe decision-making in safety-critical embodied systems. There's a structural mismatch between prediction accuracy and actual safety requirements.

Method: Proposes Risk-Informed World Model (RIWM) that organizes modeling around consequences, intervention, epistemic uncertainty, and recoverability. It integrates four capabilities: decision-relevant representation, counterfactual reasoning, safety-critical episodic memory, and runtime safety assurance.

Result: A conceptual framework for decision-centric world modeling that distinguishes physical, social, and operational consequences while using epistemic uncertainty to qualify evidence supporting actions. Identifies structural mismatches between current approaches and safety needs.

Conclusion: Future world models should shift focus from predicting likely futures to identifying consequential futures, revising judgments through experience, and determining appropriate actions (act, revise, sense, defer, or abstain) based on sufficient evidence.

Abstract: World models have progressed from compact latent dynamics to generative, controllable, and interactive simulators of embodied environments. However, high predictive likelihood and visual fidelity do not necessarily ensure that a model preserves the evidence required for safe decision-making. This perspective identifies three structural mismatches in current world modeling: likelihood versus risk, prediction versus intervention, and finite-horizon prediction versus accumulated consequences. We propose the Risk-Informed World Model (RIWM) as a decision-centric research direction for safety-critical embodied systems. RIWM organizes world modeling around consequences, intervention, epistemic uncertainty, and recoverability, and integrates four interdependent capabilities: decision-relevant representation, counterfactual reasoning, safety-critical episodic memory, and runtime safety assurance. It distinguishes physical, social, and operational consequences while using epistemic uncertainty to qualify the evidence supporting action. We further discuss open challenges in identifying consequential futures, validating counterfactual reasoning, maintaining revisable safety memories, translating learned consequences into executable constraints, and determining when evidence is sufficient to act. This perspective argues that future world models should move beyond predicting likely futures toward identifying which futures matter, revising judgments through experience, and recognizing when to act, revise, sense, defer, or abstain.

</details>


### [33] [DNative-Twin: Decision Graphs and Digital Twins for Reconstructable Agentic Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03787)
*Junjie Pang,Zhenzhen Xie,Haoke Han,Ying He,Jing Wang,Gang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DNative-Twin is a graph-native digital twin system that captures and replays AI agent decisions to audit decision-making mechanisms by linking evidence, tools, rules, and actions in a traceable graph structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents increasingly make decisions that lead to real-world actions, there's a critical need to understand how these decisions are made. Current systems only provide final outputs without showing the evidence, tool states, rules, authorizations, or decision paths that produced them, making it impossible to audit or verify decision mechanisms.

Method: The paper introduces DNative-Twin, which records committed agentic decisions as typed trajectories in a graph structure. The system re-executes decision mechanisms under declared conditions, linking observed states, decision paths, and authority information. It synchronizes this information, replays mechanisms in isolation, and compares them under controlled changes using three public process logs and controlled replay suites.

Result: Experiments identified a specific failure: graph structure can localize represented changes but cannot determine consequences of unobserved tool states. In controlled experiments with 300 injected instances, unresolved-divergence recall increased from 0 to 0.667 when replay-contract state was added, and to 1.0 with verification results. Across 500-5,000 BPI 2020 cases, median end-to-end time increased from 0.794 to 8.889 seconds. The system successfully separated roles of graph structure, replay context, and verification evidence in decision review.

Conclusion: DNative-Twin provides an effective framework for auditing AI agent decisions by capturing decision mechanisms in graph-native digital twins. While it introduces computational overhead, the system enables systematic review of decision paths and identifies limitations in handling unobserved tool states. The approach represents significant progress toward accountable AI decision-making in enterprise contexts.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly gather evidence, invoke tools, apply constraints, and produce decisions that people or software may commit to action. A final output alone cannot show which evidence, tool state, rule, authorization, or action path produced it. We present DNative-Twin, a graph-native digital twin that records a committed agentic decision as a typed trajectory and re-executes its decision mechanism under declared conditions. The graph links the state observed by the agent, the path it followed, and the authority behind the resulting action. The twin synchronizes this information, replays the mechanism in isolation, and compares it under controlled changes. We instantiate the framework in enterprise decision processes using three public process logs and controlled replay suites. The experiments identify a specific failure: graph structure localizes represented changes but cannot determine the consequence of an unobserved tool state. In a three-condition controlled experiment with 300 injected instances, unresolved-divergence recall increased from 0 to 0.667 when replay-contract state was added and to 1.0 when verification results were also available; the held-out set contained no critical-class instance. Across 500--5,000 BPI 2020 cases, median end-to-end time increased from 0.794 to 8.889 seconds on the reported platform. These results separate the roles of graph structure, replay context, and verification evidence in reviewing a decision mechanism.

</details>


### [34] [Transfiver: Human-AI Co-Inference through a Shared Editable State](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03797)
*Minji Park,Seunghyun Yoon,Hyuk Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Transfiver is a framework for transparent human-AI interaction where both parties co-infer through a shared, editable persistent state that evolves via implicit model updates and explicit human edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term human-AI interaction is challenging because AI models update their internal information implicitly, making it difficult for users to inspect or control what the model knows. This lack of transparency hinders effective collaboration and trust in AI systems.

Method: Transfiver introduces a shared editable state ($S_t$) that both humans and AI models can update. It features two state evolution modes: 1) implicit stream updates where the model interprets interactions and decides whether to revise existing items or create new ones, and 2) explicit directed edits where humans inspect and modify addressed items. The architecture separates learned parameters ($θ$) from the persistent state ($S_t$).

Result: The framework enables human-AI co-inference through transparent state management, allowing human corrections to directly modify the state that subsequent computations read, rather than adding separate instructions. The persistent state evolves during deployment without requiring parameter retraining.

Conclusion: Transfiver addresses transparency in human-AI interaction but faces open challenges in extending to rich natural-language, relational, and large-scale shared states. The approach shows promise for making AI inference processes more inspectable and controllable by users.

Abstract: Long-term human-AI interaction is difficult because the information that guides inference is updated implicitly by the model and is not directly inspectable or controllable by the user. We introduce the TRANSparent Framework for Interactive, Verifiable, Editable Representation (Transfiver), an architecture for human-AI co-inference through a shared editable state. Its central idea is that interaction-specific information is maintained in a single persistent state $(S_t)$ that both the model and the human update.
  Transfiver distinguishes two modes of state evolution. In an implicit stream update, the model interprets ongoing interaction and decides whether new information revises an existing state item or creates a new one. In an explicit directed edit, a human inspects and modifies an addressed item. Both act on the same underlying state, so a human correction changes the state that subsequent computation reads, rather than adding another instruction or separate record.
  The architecture separates shared parameters $(θ)$, learned before ordinary use, from the persistent state $(S_t)$, which evolves during deployment without parameter retraining. Extending Transfiver to rich natural-language, relational, and large-scale shared states remains open.

</details>


### [35] [Govern the Model, Not Only the Data: Storage, Circulation, and Learning in Creative AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03800)
*Phoenix Perry,George Simms,Elizabeth Wilson,Yasmine Boudiaf,Nick Bryan-Kinns,Tega Brain,R. Luke DuBois,Alix Rule,Rachel Meade Smith,Kelani Nichole,Atharva Pravin Pawar,Rebecca Fiebrink*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Federated learning fails as a privacy solution for creative communities because it allows model owners to extract value while contributors lack control over models and federation. True protection requires governance at the model level, not just data storage and circulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated learning is marketed as privacy-preserving but actually enables extractive AI by allowing model owners to control outputs while contributors have no say. The paper aims to address this power imbalance in creative communities where artists lack governance over models trained on their work.

Method: The paper analyzes three layers of governance (storage, circulation, learning) through examination of artist-governed trusts, cooperatives, and consent infrastructures. It maps the research space by pairing technical open problems with human questions and proposes four design principles for a creative data commons.

Result: The analysis reveals that current creator governance stops at learning layer: contributors can consent to training but have little say over resulting models or their federation. The paper identifies critical governance gaps and proposes principles for model-level governance in federated systems.

Conclusion: Federation alone doesn't prevent extractive AI. To protect creative communities, governance must extend to models and their federation, not just datasets. The proposed principles offer a path toward systems where contributors have meaningful agency over AI systems built with their work.

Abstract: Federated learning is increasingly presented as a privacy-preserving advance: personal data remain on the device, and only model updates are shared. It borrows the vocabulary of the federated social web, yet inverts its logic, distributing computation while the resulting model stays with whoever convened the training. We argue that federation is not in itself a remedy for extractive AI, because outcomes depend on who governs the data and the model and who has agency over the practices that shape them. We describe three layers at which a creative community can hold its work: storage, circulation, and learning. Examining artist-governed trusts, cooperatives, and consent infrastructures, we show that creator governance is established at storage and circulation but stops at learning: contributors can consent to training, yet have little say over the resulting model or its federation. We map the research space this opens, pairing technical open problems with the human questions from which they unfold. We propose four design principles for a creative data commons that governs models and their federation, not only datasets: govern the model, not only the corpus; make the terms legible at the moment of contribution; design for refusal as a first-class state; and decide stewardship in the open and account for it.

</details>


### [36] [SVG-Score: Human-Aligned Evaluation of Text-to-SVG Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03806)
*Marco Cipriano,Leonardo Zini,Alexandra Schild,Valentin Teutschbein,Afsana Mimi,Marcella Cornia,Lorenzo Baraldi,Gerard de Melo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces SVGEval, a human-aligned evaluation framework for text-to-SVG generation that addresses limitations of current CLIP-based metrics by developing specialized evaluators aligned with human judgment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current SVG generation evaluation relies on natural image metrics like CLIPScore, which are poorly suited for vector graphics since they weren't trained on SVG data and don't adequately capture SVG-specific errors like wrong colors, counts, and spatial relations.

Method: 1) First analyzed CLIP-based scores' limitations through controlled caption/image perturbations, 2) Created a human-annotated dataset for Semantic Alignment evaluation, 3) Developed two evaluators: CLIP scorers adapted to vector graphics and aligned to human preferences for fast evaluation, and a VLM judge trained with supervised fine-tuning and reward-shaped RL for interpretable assessment.

Result: Developed SVGEval framework with two complementary evaluators that better align with human judgment, and used it to benchmark major open-source, commercial, and optimization-based SVG generators on an independent caption set.

Conclusion: SVGEval addresses the critical gap in SVG generation evaluation by providing human-aligned, domain-specific evaluation protocols that are more sensitive to SVG-specific errors and better reflect human judgment compared to existing natural image metrics.

Abstract: Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) generation is attracting increasing attention as generative models improve in expressiveness and controllability. Progress, however, is held back by the lack of domain-specific evaluation protocols: current practice relies on metrics designed for natural images, most notably CLIPScore, which was never trained on vector graphics and aligns only partially with human judgment. We introduce \textbf{\ours}, a human-aligned evaluation framework for text-to-SVG generation. Through controlled caption and image perturbations, we first show that CLIP-based scores barely react to the errors SVG generators actually make, such as wrong colors, counts, and spatial relations, and that off-the-shelf Vision-Language Model (VLM) judges, while more sensitive, respond unevenly across error types and SVG styles. We then introduce a human-annotated dataset for \textit{Semantic Alignment}, measuring how faithfully a generated SVG reflects its caption. Building on it, we develop two complementary evaluators: CLIP scorers adapted to vector graphics and then aligned to human preferences, for fast large-scale evaluation, and a VLM judge trained with supervised fine-tuning and reward-shaped reinforcement learning, for more expressive and interpretable assessment. Using both, we benchmark major open-source, commercial, and optimization-based SVG generators on an independent caption set.

</details>


### [37] [CauseCollab: Causal Unified and Modality-Agnostic Network for Heterogeneous Collaborative Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03818)
*Weize Li,Yang Li,Quan Yuan,Xiaoyuan Fu,Guiyang Luo,Jinglin Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CauseCollab is a causal unified network that addresses modality heterogeneity in collaborative perception by disentangling semantic factors from modality-specific confounders using causal metric learning, achieving state-of-the-art performance especially in large modality gap scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world collaborative perception faces performance constraints due to heterogeneous sensor modalities and model architectures. Existing two-stage methods with modality-specific converters create modality-specific pseudo-protocol distributions, leading to semantic inconsistency and error accumulation, particularly problematic with large modality discrepancies.

Method: Proposes CauseCollab: (1) Formulates representation learning from a causal perspective to explicitly disentangle semantic factors from modality-specific statistical confounders via causal metric learning; (2) Uses context-guided Unified Converter for heterogeneous modalities to ensure cross-modal semantic consistency; (3) Enables integration of new modalities with minimal parameter training through adapters.

Result: Extensive experiments on OPV2V and DAIR-V2X datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, with particularly significant performance gains in scenarios involving large modality gaps compared to previous approaches.

Conclusion: CauseCollab effectively addresses modality heterogeneity in collaborative perception by taking a causal perspective to achieve semantic consistency across modalities, offering a more robust and extensible solution that requires minimal parameters for integrating new modalities while outperforming existing methods.

Abstract: Collaborative perception enhances environment understanding through multi-agent information sharing, but its performance in real-world scenarios is constrained by heterogeneous sensor modalities and model architectures. Recent protocol-based two-stage methods alleviate this problem by mapping heterogeneous features into a shared protocol space; however, independently trained modality-specific converters often generate modality-specific pseudo-protocol distributions, leading to semantic inconsistency and error accumulation, which is particularly pronounced in scenarios with large modality discrepancies. To address this issue, we propose CauseCollab, a causal unified and modality-agnostic network. CauseCollab formulates representation learning in the protocol space from a causal perspective, explicitly disentangling semantic factors from modality-specific statistical confounders via causal metric learning. Meanwhile, CauseCollab adopts context-guided Unified Converter for heterogeneous modalities to ensure cross-modal semantic consistency. In addition, integrating new modalities only requires training adapters with minimal parameters. Extensive experiments on the OPV2V and DAIR-V2X datasets demonstrate that CauseCollab achieves state-of-the-art performance, with more significant gains in scenarios involving large modality gaps.

</details>


### [38] [Semantic Bayesian World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03834)
*Tommaso Soru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Semantic Bayesian World Models (SBWMs) as a unified probabilistic reasoning framework that bridges knowledge graphs with foundation models, enabling belief representation, Bayesian updates, and agent collaboration over shared probabilistic knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a fundamental mismatch between knowledge graphs (which use crisp assertions) and modern AI systems like foundation models and autonomous agents (which reason natively in probabilities). This prevents true integration and limits reasoning capabilities.

Method: Propose Semantic Bayesian World Models (SBWMs) - a Web architecture where knowledge graphs are transformed into shared, evolving fabrics of beliefs. The approach uses ontological axioms to constrain priors, Bayesian conditioning for belief updates, and intervention mechanisms for actions.

Result: Demonstrate applications including: a home-security agent distinguishing between couriers and burglars, actuarial estimates via entailment rather than frequency, solving planning tasks that language models fail, and estimating quantities never explicitly stated in documents.

Conclusion: The community needs to develop: belief annotation standards for RDF, probabilistic entailment regimes, semantic calibration layers, and protocols for agents to exchange and disagree over calibrated beliefs to realize SBWMs as a unified reasoning architecture.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs describe reality in crisp assertions, while the systems now consuming them, foundation models and autonomous agents, reason natively in probabilities. We argue that this mismatch is why the integration of language models and knowledge graphs remains a data-feeding pipeline rather than a unified reasoning architecture. We envision Semantic Bayesian World Models (SBWMs): a Web that describes the world not as a database of facts but as a shared, evolving fabric of beliefs over knowledge graphs, where ontological axioms constrain priors, observations update beliefs by Bayesian conditioning, and actions intervene upon the world. We work through what an agent gains from such a model: a home-security agent deciding whether the figure at the gate is a courier or a burglar, an actuarial estimate aggregated by entailment rather than by string frequency, a planning task that language models reliably fail, and the estimation of quantities that no document has ever stated. We then set out what the community must build to make them possible: belief annotation over RDF~1.2, probabilistic entailment regimes, semantic calibration layers, and protocols by which agents that have never met can exchange, and disagree over, calibrated beliefs.

</details>


### [39] [Adapting to Evolving Requirements: Agentic AI for Retail Supply Chain Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03860)
*Lei Zheng,Liping Yang,Zihao Li,Guodong Lyu,Chaik Ming Koh,Chung-Piaw Teo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A graph-constrained agentic framework that uses LLMs to adapt retail supply chain decision pipelines by selecting optimal intervention paths and module reformulations, validated through downstream KPI evaluation and achieving 79-83% success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail supply chains rely on coupled decision modules that need adaptation as requirements evolve. While LLMs offer natural language interfaces for adaptation, existing methods focus on individual optimization models and struggle with heterogeneous decision pipelines where a single requirement may have multiple intervention paths with different downstream effects.

Method: Formulates requirement-driven adaptation as joint selection of intervention route and module-level change. Uses a graph-constrained agentic framework where domain agents expose admissible reformulation interfaces, and a central processor searches over bounded intervention paths. Candidates are validated and compared using downstream KPIs.

Result: Evaluated on 100 warehouse requirements from practitioner interviews using GPT, Qwen, and DeepSeek as base LLMs. The framework improved correctness and end-to-end success across all models, raising success rates from 72-76% to 79-83% compared to direct LLM reformulation.

Conclusion: The proposed graph-constrained agentic framework effectively addresses the challenge of adapting heterogeneous decision pipelines in retail supply chains, demonstrating significant improvements over direct LLM approaches and providing a structured way to handle multiple possible intervention paths with different downstream effects.

Abstract: Retail supply chain operations rely on coupled decision modules that must adapt as requirements evolve. LLMs offer a natural-language interface for this task, but existing methods primarily focus on individual optimization models. Extending them to heterogeneous decision pipelines is challenging because a requirement may admit multiple intervention paths with different downstream effects. We formulate requirement-driven adaptation as the joint selection of an intervention route and an admissible module-level change, and propose a graph-constrained agentic framework in which domain agents expose admissible reformulation interfaces and a central processor searches over bounded intervention paths. Candidates are validated and compared using downstream KPIs. In collaboration with a large retail partner, we evaluate 100 warehouse requirements elicited from practitioner interviews, with GPT, Qwen, and DeepSeek as base LLMs. Relative to direct LLM reformulation, our framework improves correctness and end-to-end success across all three models, raising end-to-end success from 72--76% to 79--83%.

</details>


### [40] [STAIR (STructure Aware Information Retriever): A novel dataset and LLM based retriever for document structure augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03874)
*Vineet Kumar,Meghanadh Pulivarthi,vishwajeet kumar,Jaydeep Sen,Riyaz Ahmad Bhat,Sachindra Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STAIR is a novel retrieval system that uses Table of Contents (ToC) information to improve LLM-based retrieval by exploiting global semantic structure in documents, achieving significantly higher recall with low hallucination rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RAG systems chunk documents into length-based segments, losing rich semantic global structure like Table of Contents, while LLMs still struggle with "lost in the middle" problems despite improvements in long-context handling.

Method: STAIR uses a finetuned Differentiable Search Index (DSI) system enhanced with Table of Contents information, enabling LLMs to store and retrieve information efficiently by exploiting global corpus structure.

Result: STAIR achieves 82.6% Recall@1 on SearchTome benchmark, significantly outperforming DSI (76.9%), BM25 (59.5%), DPR (68.7%) and Mistral (13.8%), with low hallucination rates (<0.05%) and good few-shot generalization.

Conclusion: Leveraging global structural information like Table of Contents is crucial for building accurate, low-hallucination retrieval systems, and the SearchTome benchmark enables further research in this promising direction.

Abstract: Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) is a key component for generating accurate and hallucination free answers using Large Language Models (LLMs). LLMs are improving at handling long context, but still suffer from "lost in the middle" problem. Thus, precise and accurate retrieval is important. Current retrievers chunk long context into length-based manageable chunks - in the process throwing away rich and informative semantic global structure in the corpus. We introduce a novel retrieval system STAIR that empowers an LLM to exploit global structure in a corpus such as a Table of Contents (ToC) to efficiently store and retrieve information from its model parameters. Our thorough and careful ablation studies with a finetuned Differentiable Search Index (DSI) system show that ToC helps build a low hallucination (less than 0.05%) generative Information Retrieval (IR) system and can generalize to examples where very few training samples are available. To further research in this novel direction of ToC based retrieval we release SearchTome - a diverse benchmark created from 18 books across 6 diverse domains to further research in this novel direction. STAIR achieves a high Recall@1 score of 82.6% on SearchTome as compared to DSI (76.9%), where the difference is found to be statistically significant. STAIR easily beats other strong baselines such as BM25 (59.5%), DPR (68.7%) and out-of-the-box Mistral (13.8%).

</details>


### [41] [Xiaomi-TabLDM: A Tabular Foundation Model Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03880)
*Xiaomi-TabLDM Team,:,Penghui Wang,Wei Liu,Hong Wang,Chengyue Huang,Yuxi Sun,Zirui Wang,Hongming Huang,Quan Wang,Chunxiao Liu,Erli Meng,Bin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Xiaomi-TabLDM is a tabular foundation model for classification/regression using in-context learning, achieving top performance on benchmarks with synthetic pretraining and test-time compute scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to develop a tabular large data foundation model that achieves superior prediction accuracy without task-specific fine-tuning, overcoming limitations of traditional tabular models that require extensive tuning and lack generalization.

Method: The model is pretrained exclusively on synthetic data from structural causal models (SCMs). It employs a three-stage training strategy with dual-stream feature grouping, lightweight Attention Residual, and sparse Mixture-of-Experts to learn feature interactions. It uses in-context learning for predictions without fine-tuning.

Result: Xiaomi-TabLDM ranks 1st on OpenML-CTR23 and 2nd on regression across TALENT, TabArena, and BCCO benchmarks. It achieves favorable performance-efficiency trade-off with 82% less training time and 68% less prediction time than top-ranked TabFM while maintaining strong Elo scores. Test-time compute scaling further improves performance.

Conclusion: Xiaomi-TabLDM establishes a new standard for tabular foundation models, demonstrating that synthetic pretraining and in-context learning can deliver superior predictive performance with substantially lower computational costs than existing approaches.

Abstract: We introduce Xiaomi-TabLDM, a tabular large data foundation model for classification and regression via in-context learning, which delivers superior prediction accuracy without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. Pretrained exclusively on synthetic data generated from structural causal models (SCMs), our model enables more flexible context utilization and more efficient capacity scaling.
  i) A new performance standard. Strong regression performance across benchmarks: Xiaomi-TabLDM ranks 1st on OpenML-CTR23 and 2nd on regression across TALENT, TabArena, and BCCO, demonstrating consistently strong regression performance across four complementary benchmark suites. Favorable performance--efficiency trade-off: Xiaomi-TabLDM combines strong predictive performance with substantially lower computational cost. For example, on TabArena regression, it achieves the second-highest Elo while using 82% less training time and 68% less prediction time than the top-ranked TabFM.
  ii) Large-scale synthetic pretraining. Xiaomi-TabLDM expands the coverage and diversity of synthetic tabular data used for pretraining. We also adopt a three-stage training strategy together with dual-stream feature grouping, lightweight Attention Residual, and sparse Mixture-of-Experts, enabling Xiaomi-TabLDM to learn richer feature interactions and expert specialization across diverse tabular tasks.
  iii) Test-time scaling. Xiaomi-TabLDM further extends tabular prediction through test-time compute scaling, where allocating additional computation at inference time consistently improves predictive performance over the base model.

</details>


### [42] [Inferring Affective Consciousness in an Artificial Agent: A Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03883)
*Mark Solms,St John Grimbly,Bruce Bassett,Evert Boonstra,Rowan Hodson,Nicolas Kuske,Kival Mahadew,Benjamin Rosman,Charel van Hoof,Jonathan Shock*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Artificial agents with affective systems can display hedonic place preference behavior similar to biological creatures, despite being entirely deterministic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To challenge assumptions about consciousness and feelings by showing that behaviors typically attributed to subjective experience (like hedonic place preference) can be produced by deterministic artificial systems.

Method: Developed a simple artificial agent with an affective system that experiences "felt uncertainty" about its intrinsic needs in relation to environmental resources, creating subjective-like information processing.

Result: The artificial agent successfully displayed hedonic place preference behavior similar to biological creatures that seek pleasure-producing substances, despite the agent being entirely deterministic and lacking subjective feelings.

Conclusion: This finding challenges our understanding of the physical basis of consciousness and free will, suggesting that behaviors we associate with subjective experience might arise from deterministic processes.

Abstract: Creatures that display 'hedonic place preference behaviour' are thought by many scientists to experience feelings, on the assumption that their attraction to pleasure-producing substances which lack nutritional value (e.g. cocaine, morphine) cannot easily be attributed to unconscious instinctual behaviour. In this paper, we discuss how a simple artificial agent that instantiates attributes of an affective system engaging in felt uncertainty about its intrinsic needs in relation to environmental resources can similarly display hedonic place preference behaviour -- through an apparently subjective form of information processing -- while simultaneously being entirely deter-ministic. We outline some implications of this artificially engineered behaviour for our understanding of the physical basis of consciousness and the experience of free will.

</details>


### [43] [Lose the Order, Keep the Hierarchy: Deordering HTN Plans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03912)
*Takudzwa Togarepi,Gaspard Quenard,Damien Pellier,Humbert Fiorino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Extending classical planning's plan deordering techniques to HTN planning to reduce unnecessary ordering constraints in partially ordered plans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HTN planning has focused more on plan generation than post-plan optimization, and plan deordering (removing unnecessary ordering constraints while keeping plans valid) has been well-studied in classical planning but remains under-researched in HTN settings.

Method: Adapted two established plan deordering techniques from classical planning by extending them to account for hierarchical decomposition constraints specific to HTN planning.

Result: Evaluated on IPC 2023 Partial-Order HTN benchmarks, showing substantial reduction in number of ordering constraints compared to Optiplan (an HTN planner that generates partially ordered plans directly). Also observed reduction in critical path length, though improvements were less pronounced.

Conclusion: Successfully extended classical plan deordering techniques to HTN planning, demonstrating effectiveness in reducing ordering constraints in partially ordered HTN plans, with promising results for post-plan optimization in hierarchical planning.

Abstract: Hierarchical Task Network (HTN) planning is a powerful planning formalism based on task decomposition. Although most of the literature studied plan generation, comparatively less attention has been paid to post-plan optimization. In particular, plan deordering has been extensively studied in classical planning but remains under-researched in the HTN setting. Plan deordering removes unnecessary ordering constraints between actions in a plan whilst keeping the plan valid. In this paper, we adapt two established plan deordering techniques from classical planning by extending the techniques to account for hierarchical decomposition constraints. We evaluate our proposed approaches on the IPC 2023 Partial-Order HTN benchmarks and we compare them against Optiplan, an HTN planner that generates partially ordered plans directly. Our results show a substantial reduction in number of ordering constraints in both our implementations. Although we also observe a reduction in critical path length, the improvements are less pronounced.

</details>


### [44] [Value-Preserving Architectures for Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03920)
*Alessandro Pesare,Tommaso Dolci,Katja Hose,Emanuel Sallinger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Architectural design in LLM-based multi-agent systems influences human-centered values like privacy, fairness, and safety; this paper identifies value-preserving patterns including federated privacy-aware, distributed pluralistic, and guard-agent fairness architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The adoption of LLMs and AI agents in socio-technical systems intensifies the need for responsible software engineering and value alignment, beyond just functional correctness. Architectural decisions in multi-agent systems shape system behavior and outcomes, raising critical concerns about preserving human-centered values like privacy, fairness, and safety.

Method: The paper investigates how different architectural designs support different human-centered values by discussing three value-preserving architectural patterns: (1) privacy-aware architecture with federated topology, (2) distributed architecture to promote pluralism and diversity, and (3) guard-agent architecture to detect and mitigate unfairness. Representative use cases illustrate these architectures in real-world scenarios.

Result: The paper identifies and analyzes specific architectural patterns that can preserve human-centered values in multi-agent systems: federated topology for privacy, distributed design for pluralism/diversity, and guard-agent architecture for fairness. It demonstrates these patterns through use cases and establishes connections between architectural design and value preservation.

Conclusion: Architectural choices significantly influence value-oriented system behavior in multi-agent systems. By linking architectural design with human-centered values, this work provides foundational patterns and guidelines for designing trustworthy multi-agent systems, advancing responsible software engineering in the age of agentic AI.

Abstract: The emergence of agentic AI and LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) presents unprecedented opportunities for automating complex tasks, while simultaneously raising critical concerns about the preservation of fundamental human-centered values, such as privacy, fairness, and safety. Although software engineering has traditionally focused on functional correctness, the adoption of LLMs and AI agents into complex socio-technical systems has intensified the need for responsible software engineering and robust value alignment. In MAS, architectural design decisions, such as coordination mechanisms, communication protocols, and system topologies, play a central role in shaping system behavior and the outcomes they produce. This paper argues that architectural choices influence not only the functionality and performance of MAS but can also promote value-oriented system behavior. Therefore, we investigate how different architectural designs support different human-centered values, discussing the following value-preserving architectural patterns: (i) a privacy-aware architecture with a federated topology, (ii) a distributed architecture to promote pluralism and diversity, and (iii) a guard-agent architecture to detect and mitigate unfairness. Finally, we introduce representative use cases to illustrate the proposed architectures in real-world scenarios. By linking architectural design with human-centered values, this work lays the foundation for a unified set of architectural patterns and guidelines towards the design of trustworthy MAS.

</details>


### [45] [Speak for Me: Giving LLMs the Situational Awareness to Participate in a Meeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03923)
*Muneeb Khan,Frederic Kirstein,Terry Ruas,Bela Gipp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAPA is an architecture for LLM-based meeting delegation that reduces silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5% by using predictive modules, judges, and a recalibration system to better recognize when to speak in online meetings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents in online meeting delegation fail to recognize appropriate speaking moments due to lack of structured tracking for stances, coverage, and floor dynamics, resulting in 51.4% silence rate on speaking opportunities.

Method: CAPA (Collaborative Agent Predictive Architecture) uses: 1) Perceiver updates meeting state from observed turns; 2) Predictor forecasts conversation continuation; 3) Controller decides whether/what to speak; 4) Generator phrases contributions; 5) Two judges score forecasts and actions; 6) Recalibrator updates meeting state based on verdicts.

Result: On 137 AMI meetings: Reduced silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5%; Doubled credited recovery (26.1 → 52.2); Kept hallucination at 0.6%; Schema-constrained LLM judges achieved Cohen's kappa = 0.71 alignment with human annotations.

Conclusion: CAPA effectively addresses the recognition gap in meeting delegation, shifting failure mode from omission to selection. The meeting state module is crucial for closing the gap, where raw context scaling alone is insufficient, enabling near-human-level participation awareness.

Abstract: In online meeting delegation, LLM agents fail to recognize when to speak. With no structured way to track stances, coverage, and floor, they miss the moments where they should contribute. Prompt-only delegates stay silent on 51.4% of the absent participant's talking opportunities on the AMI corpus. We present CAPA (Collaborative Agent Predictive Architecture), an architecture for online meeting delegation. A Perceiver updates the meeting state from each observed turn. A Predictor forecasts how the conversation will continue. A Controller decides whether to speak and which proposition to surface. A Generator phrases the chosen contribution in the participant's style. Two judges score the forecast and the action against the next observed turn. A Recalibrator updates the meeting state from those verdicts for future decisions. To evaluate online delegation, we introduce an episode-level protocol that scores whether, when, and what a delegate contributes around the participant's actual idea units. The protocol's schema-constrained LLM judges align with human annotations at Cohen's kappa = 0.71. On 137 AMI meetings, CAPA reduces the silence rate from 51.4% to 2.5%, doubles credited recovery (26.1 --> 52.2), and keeps hallucination at 0.6%. The failure mode shifts from omission to selection, with each residual near-miss attributable to a specific module of the architecture. Mechanism ablations identify the meeting state as the lever that closes the recognition gap, where raw-context scaling alone does not.

</details>


### [46] [Towards Numerical TOHTN Planning with SMT-based HTN-SAT Encoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03938)
*Gaspard Quenard,Takudzwa Togarepi,Damien Pellier,Humbert Fiorino*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper extends SAT-based HTN planning with SMT for numerical reasoning, creating a competitive baseline for numerical TOHTN planning with new benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HTN planning lacks comprehensive numerical reasoning support, despite significant recent attention. The paper addresses this gap by investigating numerical Totally-Ordered HTN planning.

Method: The paper extends standard SAT-based encodings with SMT (Satisfiability Modulo Theories) to handle numeric fluents in TOHTN planning. It also introduces a new benchmark suite for numerical TOHTN planning.

Result: Experimental results show that this simple SMT-based encoding already constitutes a competitive baseline for numerical TOHTN planning.

Conclusion: This work establishes a foundation for numerical HTN planning and opens the way to more expressive approaches in this domain.

Abstract: While HTN planning has received significant attention in recent years, support for numerical reasoning remains very limited. In this paper, we investigate numerical Totally-Ordered HTN (TOHTN) planning and show how standard SAT-based encodings can be naturally extended with SMT to handle numeric fluents. In addition, we introduce a benchmark suite for numerical TOHTN planning, providing a first common basis for evaluation in this setting. Experimental results show that this simple encoding already constitutes a competitive baseline. This work opens the way to more expressive approaches to HTN planning.

</details>


### [47] [More Criticism Does Not Make a Better Review: EquiReview-R](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03943)
*Zexing Zhang,Jichao Li,Tianyang Lei,Yude Fu,Yang Kewei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EquiReview-R: An AI-assisted review system that treats omission and overcritique as separate risks, using evidence-guided refinement to improve review quality by resolving concerns against evidence, searching for missing issues, and providing stop/continue/defer recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI review systems generate many criticisms but obscure the distinction between missing consequential weaknesses (omission) and retaining unsupported allegations (overcritique). These opposite failure modes require different corrections, but generation-oriented approaches don't handle them separately.

Method: Recast AI-assisted review as evidence-guided refinement of structured concern sets. EquiReview-R resolves existing concerns against localized evidence, searches for missing issues from independent and review-conditioned perspectives, and returns stop/continue/defer decisions. Uses evidence-linked trajectory corpus (ReviewTrace) for analysis and training.

Result: EquiReview-R satisfies non-inferiority criterion for major omission, reduces major overcritique from 15.5% to 8.1%, attains one-sided omission upper bound of 9.9%, and stops on 52.4% of papers. Gains come from revision rather than extra inference or shorter output, as shown by computation-matched controls and ablations.

Conclusion: Treating omission and overcritique as separate risks with evidence-guided refinement significantly improves AI-assisted review quality. The evidence-linked approach enables better concern resolution and missing issue detection. Revision should precede further search in review pipelines, and the ReviewTrace corpus provides valuable resources for studying review processes.

Abstract: AI reviewers can now produce many specific criticisms, but more criticism is not necessarily a better review. A review may miss a consequential weakness or retain an allegation that available evidence does not support. These failures require opposite corrections, yet generation-oriented systems and aggregate measures obscure the distinction. We therefore recast AI-assisted review as evidence-guided refinement of a structured concern set, with omission and overcritique treated as separate risks. Building on this formulation, we introduce EquiReview-R, which resolves existing concerns against localized evidence, searches for missing issues from independent and review-conditioned perspectives, and returns stop, continue, or defer. To expose the failure mode that motivates this design, we construct an evidence-linked trajectory corpus. Its retrospective analysis shows why revision must precede further search: nearly all concerns in a high-recall review lack a definitive evidential disposition, while an earlier refinement mechanism cannot revise them. On a frozen cohort of previously unseen papers, EquiReview-R satisfies the prespecified non-inferiority criterion for major omission, reduces major overcritique from 15.5% to 8.1%, and attains a one-sided omission upper bound of 9.9% while stopping on 52.4% of papers. Computation-matched controls, controlled pairs, and ablations show that the gain comes from revision rather than extra inference or shorter output. We release the corpus as ReviewTrace, an evidence-linked resource for studying review revision, disagreement, and provenance.

</details>


### [48] [FiMI Banking: A Sovereign Model for Indian Retail Banking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03960)
*NPCI AI Research Team,Aman Kumar,Asit Desai,Chandra Bhushan,Harsh Sharma,Harshit Bhushan,Hrithik Kadam,Keyur Doshi,Kolisetty Sai Kapardheeswar,Krishanu Adhikary,Nadeem Shaik,Navya Prakash,Nitin Kukreja,Prashant Devadiga,Shamanth MH,Shantanu Pandey,Suvradip Paul,Yatharth Dedhia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces FiMI Banking, a controlled Indian retail-banking setting, and shows that post-training approaches (preference optimization and RL with verifiable rewards) significantly improve conversational systems for banking, addressing safety, tool use, and regulatory constraints better than general-purpose language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Banks need conversational systems that can answer product questions, assist with account-related requests, and operate safely within strict operational and regulatory constraints. General-purpose language models fail to reliably meet these requirements, especially for tasks requiring grounded information, correct tool use, or cautious handling of bank-specific sensitive situations.

Method: 1. Create FiMI Banking - a controlled Indian retail-banking setting built from vetted banking documents, structured ground truth, synthetic customer backgrounds, and banking tools. 2. Evaluate two post-training approaches: preference optimization for response-level behavior, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for multi-turn tool-use tasks.

Result: Preference optimization improved safe behavior substantially: out-of-scope refusal rose from 52% to 80%. Reinforcement learning improved edge-case performance from 0.509 to 0.718 and order-sensitive task performance from 0.590 to 0.679, while using 29% fewer generated tokens.

Conclusion: Preference optimization and verifiable-reward reinforcement learning address complementary requirements for reliable banking agents, showing that specialized post-training approaches are effective for creating banking conversational systems that meet strict operational and regulatory needs.

Abstract: Banks need conversational systems that can answer product questions, assist customers with account-related requests, and operate safely within strict operational and regulatory constraints. General-purpose language models do not reliably meet these requirements. They fall short when a task requires grounded information, correct tool use, or cautious handling of bank-specific sensitive situations. We introduce FiMI Banking, a controlled Indian retail-banking setting. We build it from vetted banking documents, structured ground truth, synthetic customer backgrounds, and banking tools. We evaluate two post-training approaches: preference optimization for response-level behavior, and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for multi-turn tool-use tasks. Preference optimization improves safe behavior substantially: out-of-scope refusal rises from 52% to 80%. Reinforcement learning improves edge-case performance from 0.509 to 0.718 and order-sensitive task performance from 0.590 to 0.679, while using 29% fewer generated tokens. These results show that preference optimization and verifiable-reward reinforcement learning address complementary requirements for reliable banking agents.

</details>


### [49] [Interface-Induced Trajectory Censoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03966)
*Wenbo Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Server-reported tool-call rates can be zero while models actually emit valid calls - the interface censors them, creating silent failures that dramatically affect evaluation metrics across different model sizes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent evaluations often report tool-call rates from the serving stack, but these metrics can be misleadingly zero even when models are actually producing well-formed tool calls due to interface censorship. This creates a fundamental measurement problem where what's reported as model capability (zero tool calls) may actually be an interface issue.

Method: Used controlled experiments holding model weights, test cases, decoding parameters, and random seeds fixed while only changing the serving adapter. Conducted 2x2 factorial analysis over chat templates and parsers to isolate effects. Applied methods across different benchmarks (BFCL v4, tau-bench), model families (Qwen2.5-Coder, Llama-3.1-8B), and scales (21x scale range). Developed a 98-line preflight check to detect these silent failures.

Result: Found that changing only the serving adapter can swing scores from 0.00 to 0.96/0.19 on BFCL v4. On tau-bench's 115 tasks, the swap moved server-parsed calls from 0 to 636 and tasks reaching execution from 0 to 103. Qwen2.5-Coder showed server parsing 0/100 at every size while actual emitted calls rose to 80/100 at 32B. Llama-3.1-8B's 23% call rate fell to 0 with one flag. At evaluation time, repairing the adapter restored parsing (0→84) but didn't significantly change pass rates (53→62, n.s.).

Conclusion: The observed tool-call rate is not an intrinsic property of the model alone but a property of the model-interface stack that measures it. Silent failures in the interface can completely hide model capability, and these issues become scale-dependent (more apparent at larger model sizes). The problem reaches into training loops and has measurement consequences that standard evaluations miss.

Abstract: Agent evaluations report a tool-call rate read off the serving stack. That number can be zero while the model is emitting well-formed calls: the interface censors the trajectory before anything downstream sees it.
  On BFCL v4's own data, executor and scorer, holding weights, cases, decoding and seeds fixed and changing only the serving adapter, the same model scores 0.00 or 0.96 / 0.19. A 2x2 over chat template and parser locates the effect exactly: both main effects are exactly zero and all of it sits in the interaction -- no component is defective, and repairing one side of the contract buys precisely nothing. On tau-bench's 115 interactive retail tasks the same swap moves server-parsed calls from 0 to 636 and tasks reaching any tool execution from 0 to 103. Our probe reproduces the funnel across a 21x scale range of Qwen2.5-Coder: the server parses 0/100 at every size while well-formed emitted calls rise to 80/100 at 32B (~72 after calibration against an adjudicated gold standard). Under a matched envelope, across a comparable scale span, the silent fraction stays at 0-2, a prediction committed to the repository before the run. Llama-3.1-8B's 23% rate of calling the task function itself as a tool falls to 0 under one strict:true flag.
  The mismatch reaches inside the training loop, and its consequence is scale-dependent: in verl's AgentLoop at 7B, 45 of 115 generations carry a complete call; 0 are accepted, 0 execute, 0 return an observation. At 1.5B the same zero is over-determined, so we report the two scales separately. At evaluation time, repairing the adapter restores the mechanism but not a significant outcome gain: parsing 0->84, rescues 0->9, pass rate 53->62 (n.s.). We release a 98-line preflight check that catches every silent failure here. The observed tool-call rate is not a property of the model alone; it is a property of the model-interface stack that measures it.

</details>


### [50] [Common-Witness Certificates and Sharp Feature Bounds for Counterfactual Image Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03973)
*Usef Faghihi,Amir Saki*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for auditing image editors that formalizes local-to-global plausibility failures using witness nerves, separates auditing from causal identification, provides incompatibility certificates via Helly-type arguments, and gives finite-sample outer coverage of identified intervals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image editors can satisfy regional plausibility constraints separately while failing to produce a globally coherent image with a single latent explanation. There's a need to formalize this local-to-global failure and separate auditing from causal identification to assess whether declared feature relations are valid.

Method: Uses witness grades and witness nerves to formalize local-to-global failures. Separates auditing from causal identification: shared exogeneity allows coupling of regime marginals, while externally justified witness relations yield partial-identification bounds. Employs Helly-type arguments for incompatibility certificates for quasiconvex losses, heterogeneous action strata, and finite witness atlases. Uses blocker-hypergraph formula for exact repair counts and simultaneous confidence regions for finite-sample outer coverage.

Result: The framework successfully demonstrates predicted local-global separation in controlled MNIST, Morpho-MNIST, and smallNORB studies. Synthetic experiments validate sharp bounds, certificate recovery, and structured computation. The method provides finite-sample outer coverage of complete identified intervals and can audit declared feature relations (but not identify unrestricted pixel-level counterfactuals).

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a principled approach to auditing image editors by formalizing local-to-global plausibility failures, separating auditing from causal identification, and offering practical tools for verification. It successfully audits declared feature relations but has limitations in identifying unrestricted pixel-level counterfactuals.

Abstract: An image editor may satisfy every regional plausibility constraint separately even when no single latent explanation fits the complete output. We formalize this local-to-global failure using a common witness grade and witness nerve. The framework separates auditing from causal identification: shared exogeneity alone allows every coupling of the regime marginals, whereas an externally justified witness relation yields sharp partial-identification bounds for prespecified image features. Helly-type arguments provide short incompatibility certificates for quasiconvex losses, heterogeneous action strata, and finite witness atlases; a blocker-hypergraph formula gives exact repair counts. Simultaneous confidence regions for the regime marginals give finite-sample outer coverage of the complete identified interval. Controlled MNIST, Morpho-MNIST, and smallNORB studies demonstrate the predicted local-global separation, while synthetic experiments test sharp bounds, certificate recovery, and structured computation. The method audits a declared feature relation and does not identify unrestricted pixel-level counterfactuals.

</details>


### [51] [The Dually Flat Geometry of Planning as Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04005)
*Nikola Milosevic,Asaki Kataoka,Nicolas Hinrichs,Kenji Doya,Nico Scherf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a new geometric framework for reinforcement learning by defining visitation measures and showing they form a dually flat manifold, enabling natural-gradient optimization and connecting planning to inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to provide a more natural geometric foundation for reinforcement learning decision making, moving beyond traditional occupancy measures to better capture the information geometry of planning processes.

Method: Introduces visitation measures as stationary measures of a resetting planning process embedded into dynamics, showing they form a dually flat statistical manifold with affine charts in visitation probabilities and log-policies.

Result: The dual flatness enables planning-as-inference to generalize from linear rewards to nonlinear functionals, solvable by natural-gradient steps, and gives temporal-difference errors interpretation as marginal-utility estimates.

Conclusion: The paper develops a geometric framework with implications for both reinforcement learning algorithms and theoretical neuroscience, providing a unified perspective on planning, inference, and optimization.

Abstract: We present an alternative characterization of the occupancy measure of reinforcement learning, obtained by embedding the planning criterion into the dynamics through a resetting planning process. Its stationary measure, which we term visitation measure, is the object on which the information geometry of decision making is most naturally expressed. The achievable visitation measures form a dually flat statistical manifold whose two affine charts are the visitation probabilities and the log-policies, dual under the conditional entropy.
