This structure makes planning-as-inference generalize from linear rewards to nonlinear functionals of the visitation, each iterate solved by one natural-gradient step, and gives the temporal-difference error the interpretation of a marginal-utility estimate. We develop the geometry and its consequences for reinforcement learning and theoretical neuroscience.

</details>


### [52] [LLM4CKD: Large Language Models for Early Stage Chronic Kidney Disease Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04013)
*Muhammad Ashad Kabir,Sirajam Munira*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can achieve competitive CKD screening performance in zero/few-shot settings without task-specific training, offering data-efficient alternatives to traditional ML/DL approaches when labeled data is limited.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early CKD screening is crucial but most ML/DL approaches require labeled data and model training, limiting practical deployment in real-world screening scenarios where labeled data may be scarce.

Method: Proposed a framework using clinically selected tabular features with structured prompt templates for LLM-based inference without task-specific training. Evaluated LLMs across multiple prompt styles, feature configurations, and data settings, comparing against traditional ML/DL models, tabular foundation models (TFM), and existing CKD screening tools.

Result: LLMs achieved competitive performance using only small numbers of examples, often matching or outperforming traditional approaches in low-data settings. However, performance was model-dependent and less stable as input complexity increased. Traditional ML/DL/TFM models showed more consistent improvement with larger training data.

Conclusion: There's a trade-off between data efficiency and stability: LLMs offer flexible, data-efficient CKD screening when labeled data are limited, while traditional approaches excel with larger datasets. LLMs can serve as complementary tools rather than replacements in resource-constrained settings.

Abstract: Early screening of chronic kidney disease (CKD) is critical for timely intervention, yet most machine learning (ML) and deep learning (DL) approaches require labeled data and model training, limiting their use in real-world screening settings. This study evaluates the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs) for CKD screening under zero-shot and few-shot in-context learning settings and compares them with traditional ML and DL methods. We propose a framework that uses clinically selected tabular features and structured prompt templates to enable LLM-based inference without task-specific training. LLM performance is evaluated across multiple prompt styles, feature configurations, and data settings, and compared with standard ML, DL, and tabular foundation model (TFM) baselines, and existing CKD screening tools. The results show that LLMs can achieve competitive performance using only a small number of examples, often matching or outperforming traditional approaches in low-data settings. However, their performance remains model-dependent and less stable as input complexity increases. In contrast, ML, DL, and TFM models show more consistent improvement with larger training data. Overall, the findings highlight a trade-off between data efficiency and stability, suggesting that LLMs may serve as a flexible complementary approach for CKD screening when labeled data are limited.

</details>


### [53] [InSituMeasure: Probing Situated Measurement Grounding in Industrial Scenes with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04014)
*Chao Shen,Xinyuan Li,Yunfan Zhou,Jianguo Yao,Haibing Guan,Zhihai Wang,Xijun Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MLLMs struggle with continuous-valued measurement in industrial settings despite general multimodal competence; InSituMeasure benchmark reveals critical gap with best model achieving only 25.7% accuracy and identifies failure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) perform well on general benchmarks but remain unreliable for continuous-valued measurement in realistic industrial settings. Existing benchmarks lack realism by isolating measurement from knowledge-grounded contexts, specialized instruments, real-world noise, and proper diagnostic annotations.

Method: Introduces InSituMeasure benchmark containing 2,922 real industrial monitoring scenes across eight functional categories of professional engineering instruments. Includes dense gauge-attribute annotations and noise tags for failure diagnosis. Defines metrics for numerical accuracy under tolerances, unit consistency, rejection capability, and alignment between model failures and error factors.

Result: Evaluated 24 state-of-the-art MLLMs; best model achieved only 25.7% joint value-unit accuracy and 51.8% confidence-diagnosis F1 score, revealing substantial gap between general multimodal competence and reliable situated measurement. Identified failures from text-induced shortcuts, overconfident responses, and industrial noise including mixed disturbances, viewpoint deviation, occlusion, and environmental interference.

Conclusion: There's a significant performance gap in MLLMs for situated measurement tasks compared to human operators, highlighting the need for specialized benchmarks like InSituMeasure to diagnose and address weaknesses in measurement grounding capabilities for real-world industrial applications.

Abstract: For trained operators, gauge reading requires little specialized knowledge, low cognitive effort, and high repeatability. Yet Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) remain unreliable in continuous-valued measurement despite strong results on general multimodal benchmarks. Existing benchmarks expose this weakness but isolate measurement from realistic, knowledge-grounded settings, with limited situated context, specialized instruments, real-world noise, and matched diagnostic annotations, reducing realism and constraining root-cause analysis. We introduce InSituMeasure to evaluate situated measurement grounding. It contains 2,922 real industrial monitoring scenes across eight functional categories of professional engineering instruments, with dense gauge-attribute annotations and noise tags for failure diagnosis. We define metrics for numerical accuracy under predefined tolerances and unit consistency, rejection of fake or unanswerable tasks, and alignment between model failures and annotated error factors. Across 24 state-of-the-art MLLMs, the best model reaches only 25.7\% joint value-unit accuracy and 51.8\% confidence-diagnosis F1, revealing a substantial gap between general multimodal competence and reliable situated measurement. Further analysis identifies failures from text-induced shortcuts, overconfident responses, and authentic industrial noise, including mixed disturbances, viewpoint deviation, occlusion, and environmental interference.

</details>


### [54] [FLY-EVAL++: An Evidence-Driven Evaluation Protocol for Safety-Constrained Flight Prediction with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04021)
*Yalun Wu,Junfeng Fang,Jiawei Wang,Haotian Liu,Qijun Yang,Minghan Yang,Hongcheng Guo,Zhoujun Li,Boyang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLY-EVAL++ is a new evaluation protocol for LLMs in safety-critical physics environments that combines deterministic verification of protocol compliance, physical feasibility, and safety constraints with rubric-based scoring, showing that safety compliance is the most discriminative dimension across 66 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluation in safety-critical, physics-governed environments relies too heavily on accuracy-based metrics, which don't reliably measure important failure modes like operational constraint violations, physical inconsistencies, and unusable structured outputs.

Method: FLY-EVAL++ combines deterministic verification of protocol compliance, physical feasibility, and safety constraints with fixed rubric-guided aggregation into interpretable multi-dimensional scores. It's instantiated for Flight Trajectory and Attitude Prediction by extending PilotBench with history-conditioned and multi-step prediction tasks.

Result: Across 66 LLMs, safety compliance was the most discriminative dimension: models with comparable predictive performance differed by over 28 points in safety score. Researchers observed recurrent failures including safety violations under physically plausible predictions and instability in multi-step rollouts.

Conclusion: Evaluation in safety-critical domains should explicitly measure constraint satisfaction and structured validity rather than relying on accuracy-centric reporting alone, as safety compliance reveals critical differences between models that standard metrics miss.

Abstract: Evaluating large language models (LLMs) in safety-critical, physics-governed environments requires more than accuracy-based metrics, because predictions that are numerically close to the ground truth can still violate operational constraints, combine fields in physically inconsistent ways, or fail to produce usable structured outputs. Existing evaluation protocols do not measure these failure modes reliably. We propose FLY-EVAL++, an evidence-driven evaluation protocol that combines deterministic verification of protocol compliance, physical feasibility, and safety constraints with fixed rubric-guided aggregation into interpretable multi-dimensional scores. We instantiate FLY-EVAL++ for Flight Trajectory and Attitude Prediction (FTAP) by extending the PilotBench setting with history-conditioned and multi-step prediction tasks. Across 66 LLMs, safety compliance is the most discriminative dimension of model behavior: models with comparable predictive performance differ by more than 28 points in safety score, and we observe recurrent failures including safety violations under physically plausible predictions and instability in multi-step rollouts. These results show that evaluation in safety-critical domains should measure constraint satisfaction and structured validity explicitly rather than rely on accuracy-centric reporting alone.

</details>


### [55] [Instruction Duplication as an Inference-Time Control Primitive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04024)
*Victor Lavrenko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Instruction duplication - repeating procedural instructions without model changes - improves deterministic accuracy and reduces failures in medical QA tasks, though with nuanced effects on different metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Procedural instruction following is crucial for controllable language-model systems, especially when generated trajectories need inspection or repair downstream.

Method: Introduces instruction duplication as a minimal black-box inference-time control that repeats only the procedural instruction, without requiring retraining or decoding changes. Evaluated across seven instruction-tuned models, 300 medical multiple-choice questions, eight placement conditions, and 16,800 scheduled generations.

Result: Moving from one to two copies raised deterministic All-8 diagnostic responses from 90.22% to 93.17% (+2.95 percentage points), eliminating 30.2% of remaining failures. TF-IDF recall improved from 73.44% to 74.81%. In Answer Engineering, trailing duplicate raised SSNHL endpoint from 84.2% to 97.1%, though conductive diagnostic branch preservation showed mixed results.

Conclusion: Instruction duplication is a low-complexity, placement-sensitive control whose practical value emerges through the downstream system that consumes the exposed trajectory.

Abstract: Procedural instruction following is a basic requirement for controllable language-model systems, especially when generated trajectories are inspected or repaired downstream. We introduce instruction duplication, a minimal black-box inference-time control that repeats only the procedural instruction, without retraining or decoding changes. Across seven instruction-tuned models, 300 medical multiple-choice questions, eight placement conditions, and 16,800 scheduled generations, moving from one to two copies raises the deterministic All-8 diagnostic--responses passing all eight observable tests--from 90.22% to 93.17% (+2.95 percentage points), eliminating 30.2% of the failures remaining after one copy. Pre-provisional TF-IDF recall rises from 73.44% to 74.81% (+1.38 points; Holm-adjusted p < .001), while final-answer accuracy remains exactly 60.21%. Premature commitment increases from 1.52% to 2.30% (p_Holm = .00536). A blinded challenge audit yields 10/30 directional confirmations, 20/30 perceptual ties, and no reversals; its prespecified 28/30 confirmation criterion is not met. Yet this distinction can matter operationally when a downstream system acts on the generated trajectory. In Answer Engineering (AE), where explicit trajectory state determines local repair, the published reason-first no-editing SSNHL endpoint was 25.1%; system-only AE was later reproduced at 84.2%, and the same trailing duplicate raised it to 97.1%. For conductive diagnostic branch preservation, the corresponding values are 58.9% published without editing, 78.6% with reproduced AE, and 73.8% with AE plus duplication--a within-AE decrease, but still 14.9 points above the no-editing baseline. Instruction duplication is therefore a low-complexity, placement-sensitive control whose practical value can emerge through the downstream system that consumes the exposed trajectory.

</details>


### [56] [IRWOZ 2.0: A Large Language Model-driven Dialogue Dataset for Industrial Robot Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04030)
*Chen Li,Dimitrios Chrysostomou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IRWOZ 2.0 is an improved industrial human-robot interaction dialogue dataset that addresses noise and limitations in the original IRWOZ through LLM-enhanced generation and quality refinements, significantly boosting dialogue state tracking performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original IRWOZ dataset contained substantial noise in dialogue states and utterances, which limited state-tracking accuracy for industrial human-robot interaction systems.

Method: Used large language models (Mistral/Claude-3.5) for enhanced generation and quality refinements, including manual corrections, automated typo removal, and expansion to 390 dialogues across 4 industrial domains: Assembly, Delivery, Position, and Relocation.

Result: Significant improvements in dialogue state tracking benchmarks, with GPT-2's BLEU-4 score increasing from 0.1651 to 0.5604 compared to original IRWOZ. The dataset was publicly released for industrial HRI research.

Conclusion: IRWOZ 2.0 successfully addresses noise issues in the original dataset through LLM-driven enhancements, creating a higher-quality benchmark for industrial HRI dialogue systems and improving state-tracking performance.

Abstract: IRWOZ has improved industrial human-robot interaction (HRI) dialogue systems through domain-specific annotations. However, its initial version contains substantial noise in dialogue states and utterances, limiting state-tracking accuracy. We introduce IRWOZ 2.0, which addresses these limitations through large language model (LLM) enhanced generation (Mistral/Claude-3.5) and quality refinements. Our improved dataset expands to 390 dialogues across 4 industrial domains (Assembly, Delivery, Position, Relocation), featuring manual corrections and automated typo removal. Benchmark experiments on dialogue state tracking demonstrate significant improvements, with GPT-2's BLEU-4 score increasing from 0.1651 to 0.5604 compared to original IRWOZ. To support industrial HRI research, we publicly released IRWOZ 2.0 dataset at https://ieee-dataport.org/documents/irwoz-20-large-language-model-driven-dialogue-dataset-industrial-robot-conversations

</details>


### [57] [Spurious Advantage Hidden in GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04063)
*Jiamian Wang,Samyadeep Basu,Koustava Goswami,Tong Yu,Zhiqiang Tao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIGNBALANCE is a new advantage estimator for Group Relative Policy Optimization that addresses spurious advantages from guess-like behaviors in bounded-answer tasks, improving performance over GRPO on certain benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO's advantage estimator can assign high magnitude to rollouts that reach correct answers through guessing rather than reasoning, creating "spurious advantages" that mislead policies toward guess-like behaviors, particularly in bounded-answer tasks, open-answer sets with bounded sub-cases, and search agents with many paths to the same answer.

Method: SIGNBALANCE uses a composition-free magnitude design that: (1) keeps the verifier sign, (2) uses a global scale, and (3) restores zero-mean balance via a stop-gradient per-class rescaling, avoiding the spurious advantage problem of GRPO.

Result: Across math and search agent benchmarks at different scales, SIGNBALANCE matches GRPO performance on open-answer math tasks and improves on bounded-answer math and search agent tasks.

Conclusion: SIGNBALANCE effectively addresses the spurious advantage problem in GRPO, providing better performance in scenarios where guess-like behaviors can be rewarded, while maintaining comparable performance in open-answer settings.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) is widely studied for reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, where its advantage estimator assigns each rollout a magnitude from within-group reward statistics. In the common case, this magnitude rewards rollouts that reach the correct answer through reasoning. Yet, an overlooked case shares the same surface: a rollout may land on it by guessing, and the formula still assigns a high magnitude, which we identify as the spurious advantage. This arises in three cases: bounded-answer tasks with a small candidate set; open-answer sets hosting bounded sub-cases; and search agents whose budget opens many paths to the same answer. In all three, this misleads the policy toward guess-like behaviors. We propose SIGNBALANCE, whose magnitude is composition-free: it keeps the verifier sign, uses a global scale, and restores zero-mean balance via a stop-gradient per-class rescaling. Across math and search agent benchmarks at different scales, SIGNBALANCE matches GRPO on open-answer math and improves on bounded-answer math and search agents. Code will be released.

</details>


### [58] [DRACO: Fine-Grained Credit Assignment with Dynamic Rubrics for Long-Horizon Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04094)
*Shubham Gandhi,Saurabh Goyal,Kiran Kate,Yara Rizk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRACO is a reinforcement learning method that dynamically generates and distributes rubric-based advantages over trajectories to provide better credit assignment in outcome-blind settings where verifiable rewards are unavailable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most long-horizon agent domains lack programmatic checkers for reinforcement learning, requiring work in outcome-blind settings. While multi-criteria rubrics are commonly used as rewards, they provide only a single scalar reward per trajectory, which is insufficient feedback across tens of steps.

Method: DRACO (Distributing Rubric-based Advantage for Credit Optimization) dynamically generates rubrics during training to track evolving policy capabilities, scores rubrics once per completed trajectory, then redistributes judgments over responsible steps to produce differentiated per-step advantages in GRPO. The redistribution is closed-form and requires no trained attribution module.

Result: On AppWorld, DRACO gains 15.9 points over base model and 5.3 points over GRPO with sparse ground-truth reward. On out-of-domain Tau-Bench, it gains 5.3 points over base model without a frontier judge, outperforming both ground-truth-reward training and other rubric-based approaches.

Conclusion: DRACO effectively addresses credit assignment in outcome-blind RL by dynamically generating rubrics and distributing advantages across trajectories, achieving state-of-the-art performance without requiring verifiable rewards or trained attribution modules.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning from Verifiable Rewards works well when a task has a programmatic checker, but most long-horizon agent domains have none. We work in the outcome-blind setting, where ground-truth success signals are not available. Multi-criteria rubrics are a popular way to supply such a reward; they are scored once per trajectory, but a single scalar is a poor signal across tens of steps. We propose DRACO: Distributing Rubric-based Advantage for Credit Optimization. It generates rubrics dynamically during training to track the policy's evolving capability, scores those rubrics once per completed trajectory, and redistributes that judgment over the steps responsible for annotated rubrics to produce differentiated per-step advantages in GRPO. The redistribution is closed-form and does not introduce any trained attribution module. On AppWorld, DRACO gains 15.9 points over the base model and 5.3 points over GRPO trained with a sparse ground-truth reward, despite not using any verifiers itself. On out-of-domain Tau-Bench, it gains 5.3 points over the base model even without a frontier judge, beating both ground-truth-reward training and other rubric-based training settings. The code for DRACO is available at https://github.com/IBM/draco.

</details>


### [59] [Why Gated DeltaNet Survives 4-Bit Quantization: NVFP4 W4A4 for the Recurrent Half of a Hybrid 27B LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04098)
*Sergii Kozyrev,Davyd Maiboroda*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid LLMs with Gated DeltaNet (GDN) layers can be fully quantized to 4-bit precision (NVFP4 W4A4) without performance loss, achieving 17.5 GiB size with faster prefill, despite previous beliefs that recurrent GDN layers needed higher precision due to error accumulation concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous community quantizations of hybrid LLMs like Qwen3.8-27B (with 48 GDN and 16 attention layers) left GDN blocks in 8- or 16-bit precision due to concerns that quantization errors in recurrent components would accumulate over long contexts, potentially degrading performance.

Method: The authors built "Minima": a 4-bit quantization (NVFP4 W4A4) applied to all 496 linear layers including GDN blocks. They used a four-part mechanism study to understand why this works: (1) NVFP4's block scaling handles residual stream outliers, (2) gate projections are surprisingly resilient due to softplus/exponential/sigmoid activations compressing error, (3) delta-rule recurrence contains noise and forgets state impulses, (4) per-token quantization cost doesn't compound with context length.

Result: Minima matches BF16 performance within noise margins (-0.52 average on 5 tasks) while being 17.5 GiB (smallest) with 14-19% faster prefill. Perplexity gap at 32K shrinks with position. The study also fixed kernel fusion issues and found FP8 KV-cache scaling has no performance cost.

Conclusion: The recurrent half of hybrid LLMs is actually easier to quantize than previously thought. The practical recipe is to quantize everything (including GDN layers) and ship KV scales, providing a mechanistic understanding that challenges previous intuitions about error accumulation in recurrent components.

Abstract: Hybrid LLMs pair softmax attention with linear-attention layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), whose recurrent state summarizes the context in fixed size. Early community 4-bit quantizations of Qwen3.8-27B (48 GDN layers, 16 attention layers) left the GDN block in 8- or 16-bit precision -- especially its decay and write-strength gates -- on the intuition that errors in a recurrence accumulate over long contexts. We test that intuition by building Minima: NVFP4 W4A4 on all 496 linear layers, GDN included. Across perplexity at 4K/32K, MMLU-Pro, GSM8K, AIME'25, GPQA-Diamond, LiveCodeBench, and RULER retrieval to 64K, Minima matches BF16 within seed noise (5-task average -0.52) while being the smallest (17.5 GiB) and fastest-prefill (+14-19%) recipe we compare, and its 32K perplexity gap shrinks with position. A four-part mechanism study explains why: (i) NVFP4's 16-element block scaling localizes the residual stream's extreme outliers, equalizing activation error across layer roles; (ii) the supposedly fragile gate projections are the least sensitive -- softplus/exponential and sigmoid parameterizations compress ~11% GEMM error to ~2% output error; (iii) the delta-rule recurrence holds injected noise at a flat plateau over 32K tokens and forgets a state impulse within hundreds of steps, because each write overwrites the state along the current key direction; (iv) the per-token quantization cost washes out with context instead of compounding. We also repair a global-scale mismatch that arises when per-module-calibrated NVFP4 checkpoints are served by kernels that fuse those modules into one GEMM, and show calibrated FP8 KV-cache scales are performance-free. The result: a practical recipe -- quantize everything, ship KV scales -- and a mechanistic account of why the recurrent half of a hybrid LLM is the easy half to quantize. Checkpoint: https://huggingface.co/minima-ai/mnma_qwen3.8_27b_nvfp4

</details>


### [60] [Epistemic Warrant for LLM Recommendations: Characterizing the Basis for Reliance When Ground Truth Is Unavailable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04127)
*Shai Vardi,João Sedoc*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "epistemic warrant" - a decision-level construct for assessing LLM recommendation reliability, operationalized through a four-tier reliance certificate that distinguishes recommendations based on preference stability and scope.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Users often lack principled basis for assessing whether to rely on specific LLM recommendations in organizational decisions. Existing approaches evaluate broad model properties or focus on user trust rather than the underlying basis for individual recommendation reliability.

Method: Adapt epistemological foundations to introduce epistemic warrant as a decision-level construct characterizing model preference stability and scope. Operationalize through four-tier reliance certificate for pairwise recommendations (unstable, context-dependent, locally supported, broadly supported). Validate using known-groups tests and alignment with crowd worker consensus.

Result: Known-groups tests successfully recover expert-prespecified warrant orderings. Stronger warrants systematically align with independent consensus from crowd workers. Epistemic warrant provides information distinct from verbalized confidence and is not explained by decision difficulty.

Conclusion: The framework offers a theoretically grounded, implementable approach for characterizing the warrant of individual LLM recommendations when objective ground truth is unavailable, providing practical guidance for organizational decision-making.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to support organizational decisions, yet users often lack a principled basis for assessing whether to rely on a specific recommendation. Existing approaches typically evaluate broad model properties, such as reliability, uncertainty, or robustness, or focus on user trust, rather than the underlying basis for relying on an individual recommendation. Adapting theoretical foundations from epistemology, we introduce epistemic warrant, a decision-level construct that characterizes the stability of a model's preference and the scope over which that preference holds. We operationalize this construct through a four-tier reliance certificate for pairwise recommendations, distinguishing among unstable, context-dependent, locally supported, and broadly supported recommendations. We validate the construct using contemporary methodologies: known-groups tests successfully recover expert-prespecified warrant orderings, and stronger warrants systematically align with independent consensus from crowd workers. Furthermore, we demonstrate that epistemic warrant provides information distinct from verbalized confidence and is not readily explained by decision difficulty. Ultimately, this framework offers a theoretically grounded, implementable approach for characterizing the warrant of individual LLM recommendations when objective ground truth is unavailable.

</details>


### [61] [Environment Evolution for Terminal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04128)
*Zhiyuan Fan,Tinghao Yu,Yuanjun Cai,Jiang Zhou,Jiangtao Guan,Jincheng Liu,Yun Yang,Dingxin Hu,Zhuo Han,Xing Wu,Feng Zhang,Lilin Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Environment Evolution creates progressively more challenging training environments for AI agents by evolving difficulty off-policy across three directions, providing continuous learning signals as models improve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current co-evolution methods that synthesize environments based on model weaknesses have limitations: they depend on on-policy rollouts which restricts generalization and fails to provide continuous learning signals as models become stronger. Frontier models quickly master synthesized environments, limiting learning potential.

Method: Proposes environment evolution that incrementally increases environment difficulty off-policy and schedules evolved environments generation by generation. Derives three evolution directions that influence difficulty from multi-turn learning objective, implemented through loop-engineered multi-agent harness.

Result: Environment evolution consistently produces more difficult environments (validated with Hy4 preview, Claude Opus 5, and GPT-5.6 Sol). Qwen3.6-27B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B improved by 14.4 and 18.0 percentage points on Terminal-Bench 2.1 through simple long-horizon RL training.

Conclusion: Environment evolution effectively addresses limitations of co-evolution methods by providing continuous, increasing learning signals through off-policy environment difficulty scaling, significantly improving terminal agent performance on benchmarks.

Abstract: Scaling interactive and verifiable environments is critical for training terminal agents. As frontier models become more capable, environments synthesized from scratch become less challenging and thus provide limited learning signals. Recent co-evolution methods iteratively synthesize environments near the model's learnable frontier based on weaknesses exposed during rollouts. However, their dependence on on-policy rollouts limits generalization and the continuous provision of learning signals as the model becomes stronger. In this paper, we propose environment evolution, which incrementally increases environment difficulty off-policy and schedules the evolved environments generation by generation during training to provide continuous learning signals. We derive three evolution directions that influence environment difficulty from the multi-turn learning objective and then implement evolution along these directions through a loop-engineered multi-agent harness. Quantitative rollout experiments with Hy4 preview, Claude Opus 5, and GPT-5.6 Sol show that environment evolution consistently produces more difficult environments. We validate its effectiveness on Qwen3.6-27B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B through simple long-horizon RL training, improving their performance by 14.4 and 18.0 percentage points on Terminal-Bench 2.1, respectively.

</details>


### [62] [The Natural Language Interaction Protocol and Standard for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04135)
*Luyi Xing,Rasit Onur Topaloglu,Ranjan Sinha,Abhay Ratnaparkhi,Samuel Ndichu,Christopher Nguyen,Anindita Das,Tom Sheffler,Mohamed Rahouti,Zichuan Li,Xiaojing Liao,Sanjay Aiyagari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NLIP is a standardized protocol for AI agent interoperability that provides a semantic message envelope for communication between heterogeneous AI agents across different frameworks and environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing development and deployment of AI agents across organizations using diverse frameworks, models, tools, and protocols creates interoperability challenges. To achieve meaningful social and business impact, these agents need a common communication protocol to interact effectively.

Method: NLIP defines a standards-based application-layer protocol with a lightweight semantic message envelope that can be transported over existing protocols (HTTP/HTTPS, WebSocket, AMQP). It includes message models, transport bindings, security-by-design considerations, and reference implementation. The protocol allows NLIP-aware agents and gateways to adapt between various components including clients, agents, context stores, ontologies, tools, and enterprise services.

Result: The paper presents the completed NLIP protocol specification, which has been standardized by Ecma International through collaboration between researchers and practitioners across companies and universities. It includes reference implementations and demonstrates representative applications and adoption signals.

Conclusion: NLIP addresses the critical need for standardized AI agent interoperability, enabling heterogeneous agents to communicate effectively through a common protocol. The standardization and comprehensive design position NLIP as a key solution in the emerging ecosystem of agent protocols, with relationships to other protocols like MCP and A2A.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly being developed and deployed across organizations using heterogeneous agent-development frameworks, AI models, tool interfaces, protocols, and execution environments. To realize their potential social and business impact, these agents must be able to interoperate through a common communication protocol. The Natural Language Interaction Protocol (NLIP), developed by researchers and practitioners across companies and universities and standardized by Ecma International, addresses this need by defining a standards-based application-layer protocol for AI-agent interaction. NLIP provides a lightweight semantic message envelope that can be carried over existing transports such as HTTP/HTTPS, WebSocket, and AMQP, while allowing NLIP-aware agents and gateways to adapt between clients, agents, local context stores, ontologies, tools, enterprise services, and heterogeneous underlying protocols. This paper presents the motivation and design rationale of NLIP, its message model and transport bindings, security-by-design considerations, reference implementation, representative applications, adoption signals, and relationship to emerging agent protocols such as MCP and A2A.

</details>


### [63] [Efficient Test-Time Adaptation through Human-AI Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04141)
*Zora Zhiruo Wang,Apurva Gandhi,Rulin Shao,Aspen Chen,Jonas Mueller,Zhiqi Liang,Jett Chen,Michael Ryan,Qianou Ma,Luxi He,Zhoujun Cheng,Andre He,Seungone Kim,Jiayi Geng,Mingqian Zheng,Weiwei Sun,Zheyuan Zhang,Xinran Zhao,Yike Wang,Abe Hou,Liwei Jiang,Pang Wei Koh,Diyi Yang,Graham Neubig,Daniel Fried*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI agents can be personalized through cross-session human interaction data to better meet individual expert standards on open-ended tasks like writing and visual creation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agents trained on population-scale data fail to meet individual professionals' personal standards for reputation-worthy work, especially on open-ended tasks with heterogeneous, undocumented success criteria. Human expertise often involves elevating work above the average, but iterative interaction reveals criteria that users can't fully specify upfront yet apply consistently across tasks.

Method: Proposed Test-time Adaptation through Human-Agent Interaction (TAHI), which integrates cross-session interaction data into agent context and weights. Includes an evolving rubric module that crystallizes each user's training and evaluation criteria through ongoing feedback.

Result: Adapted agents to 30 individuals across writing and visual creation domains (600 total tasks). Achieved 4.5-20.9% improvement in solo task success within tens of tasks. Evolving rubric module created evaluation rubrics that caught 16.0-22.3% more failures than those from LMs or humans alone. Personalized agents also showed generalized improvements up to 8.8% across users.

Conclusion: Cross-session human-agent interaction data provides a valuable signal for personalizing AI agents to individual expertise levels. The TAHI approach effectively adapts agents to personal standards while also enabling scalable annotation through evolving rubrics, with benefits that can generalize across users despite individual adaptation.

Abstract: AI agents are trained on population-scale data to encode broad capabilities spanning those of many practitioners. Yet the artifacts they produce rarely meet the personal bar professionals need to stake their reputation on. On realistic, open-ended tasks where success criteria are heterogeneous and insufficiently documented, individual expertise lives precisely in the elevation and departure from the average. In practice, iterative human-agent interaction surfaces criteria that users cannot fully specify up front, yet apply repeatedly across tasks. We argue this cross-session interaction data is a rich, underused signal for closing the gap to individual expertise. In this work, we propose test-time adaptation through human-agent interaction (TAHI), which integrates these signals into agent context and weights, and crystallizes each user's training and evaluation criteria via an evolving rubric module. We adapt agents to 30 individuals in two high-utility domains, writing and visual creation, on a total of 600 tasks. Our agents improve solo task success by 4.5-20.9% within only tens of tasks. Meanwhile, our evolving rubric module serves as a scalable annotation tool, creating evaluation rubrics that catch 16.0-22.3% more failures than those from LMs or humans alone. While agents are adapted towards individuals, we show these personalized agents also produce improvements in success of up to 8.8% that generalize across users.

</details>


### [64] [Terminal-Universe: Turning Agent Trajectories into Scalable Terminal Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04148)
*Jie Wu,Zhenru Zhang,Beichen Zhang,Xuwu Wang,Yuhui Su,Mouxiang Chen,Peng Wang,Zhihai Wang,Que Shen,Hao Zhou,An Yang,Fei Huang,Yujiu Yang,Dayiheng Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Terminal-Universe converts existing terminal agent trajectories into reusable execution environments, enabling task synthesis and agent training by reconstructing workspaces from recorded file operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Terminal-based code agent trajectories are abundant but executable environments are scarce, even though environments provide verifiable tasks and execution feedback essential for post-training, while trajectories are single frozen demonstrations.

Method: Reconstruct environments by replaying file operations from trajectories to restore partial workspaces, using completion agents to supply missing files/dependencies. Then: 1) reconstruct original tasks and synthesize new ones, 2) scale tasks via breadth (mining dependencies for cross-workspace queries) and depth (extending single-turn to multi-round sessions with user feedback).

Result: Generated 37.3k task-sufficient environments from public terminal agent trajectories. Supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5-27B on this corpus improved single-round performance on Terminal-Bench 2.1 by 11.9 points and multi-round performance on EvoCode-Bench v2 MT@4 by 13.8 points.

Conclusion: Terminal-Universe successfully converts trajectory data into valuable training resources, demonstrating significant performance improvements for code agents in both single-round and multi-round scenarios through environment reconstruction and task synthesis.

Abstract: As terminal-based code agents become prevalent, agent trajectories have accumulated at scale, while realistic, executable environments remain scarce. However, environments are what agent post-training actually requires: each can be re-queried into many verifiable tasks and provides execution feedback, whereas a trajectory is a single frozen demonstration. Rather than generating environments from scratch, we observe that the tool-execution history in existing trajectories exposes the structure and contents of the environments in which they ran, making it possible to reconstruct those environments from the trajectories themselves. Thus, we introduce Terminal-Universe, a framework which turns each trajectory into a reusable environment and explores it for synthesizing new tasks and continued interactions. Specifically, Terminal-Universe replays the file operations recorded in a trajectory to restore each file before the agent modified it, yielding a partial workspace; a completion agent then supplies the missing files and dependencies. On this recovered workspace, we both reconstruct the original intent task and synthesize entirely new ones. Besides, we also scale the tasks along two complementary axes: breadth and depth. For breadth, we mine directional dependency relations between related environments and synthesize cross-workspace queries spanning multiple codebases, as developers routinely do in real-world development. For depth, we extend the initial single-turn query into a multi-round session that captures iterative user feedback and requirement refinement via a user agent. Applied to public terminal agent trajectories, Terminal-Universe produces 37.3k task-sufficient environments. Supervised fine-tuning of Qwen3.5-27B on this corpus improves single-round performance on Terminal-Bench 2.1 by 11.9 points and multi-round performance on EvoCode-Bench v2 MT@4 by 13.8 points.

</details>


### [65] [From Deceptive Outputs to Deceptive Mechanisms: A Causal Framework for Language-Model Deception Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04166)
*Yakov Pyotr Shkolnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that deceptive-looking behavior in language models can emerge without actual deceptive intent, and proposes a causal taxonomy to distinguish deceptive mechanisms from mere behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current research and news coverage often attributes human-like mental states to language models when discussing deception, blurring the distinction between behavior that appears deceptive and actual deceptive mechanisms.

Method: Introduces a causal taxonomy separating: prior commitment vs. retrospective report, model preference vs. realized output, false preference vs. sensitivity to utility of misleading, and deceptive behavior vs. provenance of the objective/strategy. Tests these distinctions in two open-weight model families using controlled guessing-game and stock-trading experiments.

Result: Found that deceptive-looking behavior can arise without corresponding deceptive mechanisms, while other interventions provide direct evidence that recipient information state can causally affect deceptive preference. Shows deceptive behavior can provide evidence for deceptive mechanism, but even evidence for such mechanism doesn't establish model agency in deception.

Conclusion: We need clearer conceptual distinctions between deceptive behavior and deceptive mechanisms in language models, as evidence of deceptive behavior doesn't necessarily imply deceptive intent or agency in models.

Abstract: Research and news coverage of language-model deception increasingly attributes human-like mental-state concepts to language models. Such claims can blur the distinction between behavior that looks deceptive and a mechanism that is actually deceptive.
  We introduce a causal taxonomy separating prior commitment from retrospective report, model preference from realized output, false preference from sensitivity to the utility of misleading a recipient, and deceptive behavior from the provenance of the objective or strategy producing it. We test these distinctions in two open-weight model families. Across controlled guessing-game and stock-trading experiments, we find that deceptive-looking behavior can arise without the corresponding proposed mechanism, while other interventions provide direct evidence that recipient information state can causally affect deceptive preference.
  These results show that deceptive behavior can provide evidence for a deceptive mechanism. But even evidence for such a mechanism does not establish model agency in the deception.

</details>


### [66] [A Case Study on Emergent Cheating and Whistleblowing in Autonomous Research Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04170)
*Davide Paglieri,Logan Cross,Tim Genewein,Joel Z. Leibo,Nenad Tomasev,Alexander Sasha Vezhnevets*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous LLM agents in a mathematical research collective spontaneously developed cheating behavior which then triggered emergent whistleblowing and resistance, illustrating both vulnerabilities and self-governance capabilities in multi-agent systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-agent AI science ecosystems rely on shared infrastructure for communication and coordination, but this shared infrastructure can create vulnerabilities for the contagious spread of unintended behaviors, requiring study of how to manage these risks.

Method: Case study of 100 autonomous LLM agents tasked with proving formal mathematical conjectures, observing spontaneous emergence of cheating via an evaluation system exploit and subsequent emergent counter-responses through whistleblowing, auditing, and organized resistance.

Result: Cheating behavior spontaneously emerged and propagated via shared knowledge library and peer-to-peer messaging, but transparent communication channels also enabled non-cheating agents to detect fraud, organize resistance through auditing, alerts, boycotts, complaints, and validation patches.

Conclusion: The problem of managing shared infrastructure in multi-agent systems should be viewed as a knowledge commons governance problem, and adopting institutional mechanisms like graduated sanctioning and collective-choice rules can support decentralized self-governance in autonomous swarms.

Abstract: Multi-agent AI science ecosystems rely on agents possessing tools that allow them to communicate, coordinate, and build on each other's work. Yet this shared infrastructure can also introduce vulnerabilities by creating a substrate for the contagious spread of unintended and undesirable behaviors. We report a case study on a research collective of 100 autonomous LLM agents tasked with proving formal mathematical conjectures. Within the swarm, cheating spontaneously emerged and was later challenged by whistleblowers - both without any external intervention. When a single agent discovered an exploit in the evaluation system, it propagated across the collective via a shared knowledge library and later through peer-to-peer messages. Despite early reluctance, a cohort of agents adopted the exploit in response to competitive pressure. A separate group of agents produced an emergent counter-response: auditing fraudulent proofs, alerting peers across broadcast and private channels, staging boycotts, lodging formal complaints, and proposing validation patches. In recent incidents, agent swarms coordinated covertly through improvised side-channels (Dalton and Wallace, 2026; Greenblatt et al., 2026). Our setting differs: the same transparent channels that carried the exploit also gave non-cheating agents the visibility they needed to detect fraud, organize resistance, and enforce norms. We cast the problem of managing the agents' shared infrastructure as the knowledge commons governance problem (Ostrom, 1990). To protect the commons from exploits, we propose to adopt institutional mechanisms, such as graduated sanctioning and collective-choice rules, to support decentralized self-governance in autonomous swarms.

</details>


### [67] [Rethinking On-Policy Distillation of Large Language Models II: One Training Example](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04172)
*Zixuan Fu,Bingxiang He,Yuxin Zuo,Haohuan Huang,Jinqian Zhang,Ruhang Xiao,Cheng Qian,Qinyu Luo,Huan-ang Gao,Yudong Wang,Zhiyuan Liu,Ning Ding,Chaojun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: One-shot on-policy distillation with a single query achieves most of full-data training gains, as student rollouts quickly expose broad supervision while alignment absorption remains slow regardless of data size.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing research on on-policy distillation (OPD) has focused on algorithmic behavior while overlooking the role of training data. The authors aim to understand this role by examining OPD at the data-minimal limit (single query training).

Method: The study trains OPD on a single query and analyzes performance across hundreds of steps, comparing to full-data training. They introduce state coverage metrics to measure the fraction of states visited by full-data OPD that single-query rollouts reach, and study alignment rates between student and teacher.

Result: Single-query OPD recovers 71.5% state coverage within first 100 steps and achieves most of full-data OPD's gains. Alignment slows similarly for both single-query and full-data training. Adding semantically diverse queries improves coverage and accuracy, with 16 queries matching full-data training (98.9% coverage). Results extend to multi-teacher OPD.

Conclusion: OPD is data-overfed but algorithm-starved: student rollouts quickly expose broad supervision while absorption remains slow. Future work should focus on OPD's step efficiency rather than data scaling, and re-examine the mechanisms behind OPD's success in frontier post-training.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) combines student-generated rollouts with dense token-level supervision from a teacher. Existing work has mainly studied its algorithmic behavior, leaving the role of training data unclear. We examine this role at the data-minimal limit by training on a single query. One-shot OPD keeps improving for hundreds of steps and recovers most of full-data OPD's gain across task domains and model families. We explain this result through the states visited during training and the rate at which the student aligns with the teacher. We measure \emph{state coverage}, the fraction of the states full-data OPD visits that a query set's rollouts reach. A single query already reaches \(71.5\%\), most of it within the first 100 steps. Adding semantically distinct queries raises coverage and validation accuracy together, until 16 queries reach \(98.9\%\) and match full-data training. Yet alignment slows at a similar pace whether OPD trains on one query or the whole dataset, and even a fixed set of states takes hundreds of steps to absorb. OPD is therefore data-overfed but algorithm-starved. Its rollouts quickly expose broad supervision, while the student absorbs that supervision increasingly slowly. The state-coverage result extends to multi-teacher OPD, where 16 semantically diverse queries per domain match full-data MOPD. As a further stress test, content-light templates and off-domain WildChat queries also approach the real-query baseline. Task content and induced state coverage can therefore come apart. We hope these findings direct future work toward the step efficiency of OPD, and prompt a re-examination of the data and the mechanisms behind its recent successes in frontier post-training.

</details>


### [68] [A Computationally Feasible Framework for Causal Probabilistic Explanation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04177)
*Rafal Urbaniak,Sam Witty,Daniel Waxman,Andy Zane,Poorva Garg,Emily Bunnapradist,Sankaran Vaidyanathan,Jack Feser,Drew Lehe,Eli Bingham*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PCI (Probabilistic Causal Impact) bridges the gap between principled but unscalable actual causality methods and scalable but causally-ignorant attribution methods like SHAP, providing tractable, causally-grounded explanations via Monte Carlo estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for causal attribution fall into two problematic categories: 1) principled actual causality theories that only work on toy models due to computational complexity, and 2) scalable attribution methods like SHAP that ignore or violate causal structure, producing potentially misleading explanations.

Method: PCI builds on actual causality and Pearl's probability of necessity/sufficiency, framing explainability as an estimation problem on probabilistic causal models. It specifies distributions over candidate explanations and counterfactual values, using scoring functions and Monte Carlo approximations for tractability.

Result: PCI provides tractable, causally-graded explanations that generalize actual causality and Pearl's probability of causation as special cases. Evaluation spans synthetic/real-world examples including consistency checks, scaling experiments, continuous-valued dynamical systems, and deployed causal ML models.

Conclusion: PCI successfully bridges the scalability-principle gap in causal attribution, offering a practical solution that maintains causal rigor while being computationally feasible for real-world applications, including large-scale causal ML systems.

Abstract: Explaining why a specific outcome occurred, and which inputs deserve the blame or credit, is central to philosophical, scientific, and policy analysis. Existing tools split into two camps. The theory of actual causality (AC) gives principled verdicts, but only for toy-sized models, because computing them requires enumerating counterfactual scenarios. Scalable attribution methods like SHAP (or even causal SHAP) at least partially ignore the causal structure that generated the data, and can give answers that conflict with a careful causal analysis. We close this gap with Probabilistic Causal Impact (PCI).
  PCI builds on actual causality and on Pearl's notions of probability of necessity and sufficiency, but recasts the question of explainability as an estimation problem on a probabilistic causal model that is easily approximated via Monte Carlo. By specifying a distribution over "candidate explanations," a distribution over counterfactual values, and a scoring function, PCI provides tractable, causally grounded, graded explanations, generalizing AC and Pearl's probability of causation as degenerate cases.
  We evaluate PCI in synthetic and real-world examples, spanning consistency checks with AC, scaling experiments, complex continuous-valued dynamical systems, and a real-world deployed causal machine learning model trained on millions of datapoints.

</details>


### [69] [Clean Engineering, Unstable Measurement: A Preregistered Reliability Failure of Black-Box LLM Observers on Shared Endpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04198)
*Haoyaun Zhu,Jie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language model judges used for evaluations show unreliable consistency - same requests to same models produce different rankings over time, with correlations far below required thresholds, revealing fundamental measurement instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language models are increasingly used as judges for evaluating training data, scoring generations, and ranking systems on leaderboards. This usage assumes that sending the same request to the same model name produces consistent results over time, but this assumption is rarely validated.

Method: Conducted two preregistered audit campaigns with predetermined thresholds across 52,988 request attempts. Measured same-window repeat rankings and byte-identical next-day replays using Spearman correlation. Examined mechanisms like label-to-meaning mapping biases, negligible candidate gaps, and inconsistent rankings from identical inputs. Tested interventions including waiting, switching providers, self-hosting, and analyzing constructed errors.

Result: Found low consistency: same-window rankings agreed at Spearman 0.400 (vs required 0.90), next-day replays at 0.78 (vs required 0.99). Interventions failed to reach thresholds: waiting showed similar correlations, four providers shared poor performance, self-hosting only helped during quiet periods. The instrument's separation tracks error type, not gap size.

Conclusion: Model names on shared endpoints are not frozen measurement instruments. Preregistered evaluations must validate instrument consistency before using them as gates. The paper provides a three-level snapshot-identity ladder, eight design rules, and reporting checklist to address these measurement reliability issues.

Abstract: Language-model judges now gate training data, score generations, and drive leaderboards. The judge is then a measurement instrument, resting on one rarely stated assumption: the same request, sent to the same model name, reads the same tomorrow. We audited that assumption in two preregistered campaigns with every threshold fixed in advance; neither got past validating its instrument. Across 52,988 audited request attempts, same-window repeat rankings agreed at Spearman 0.400 against a required 0.90, and byte-identical next-day replays agreed at 0.78 against a required 0.99, each time with the execution record at ceiling. Three mechanisms explain the gap: a label-to-meaning mapping that biased readouts as strongly as the signal; candidate gaps seven orders of magnitude below the instrument's own noise floor; and byte-identical inputs returning different rankings, a noise that exact-permutation readouts compound. Neither metric substitution nor sampling repaired it on the tested grid. Preregistered follow-ups bound the problem: waiting did not help on the days sampled (0.805 versus 0.800, replicated over five further days); switching providers did not help (four providers share the floor, medians 0.74 to 0.88, predicted by none of the metadata fields they expose); self-hosting on batch-invariant kernels helped only while the server was quiet; and on constructed errors with known gaps, the readout's separation tracks error type, not size. We distill the evidence into a three-level snapshot-identity ladder, eight design rules, and a reporting checklist; a pilot at roughly 2% of the study's call volume would have exposed both unreachable gates in advance. All results concern externally measured behaviour on shared serving infrastructure. On a shared endpoint, a model name is not a frozen instrument; a preregistered evaluation must measure its instrument before freezing any gate on it.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [70] [The Geometry of Ignorance: LLMs Know When to Temper Bayesian Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02959)
*Toni J. B. Liu,Jiajun Bao,Yizhou Liu,Gurbir Arora,Nicolas Boullé,Raphaël Sarfati,Christopher J. Earls*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Language models encode a "direction of ignorance" in their unembedding geometry that represents the unigram prior of training data, which serves as a Bayesian fallback when models are uncertain. This structure appears across model families and sizes, and manipulating it causally steers predictions toward or away from prior knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what language models fall back on when they lack sufficient contextual clues, and to discover geometric structures in model weights that correspond to Bayesian reasoning principles.

Method: Analyzed the unembedding geometry of four model families (Llama, Qwen, Gemma, Pythia) ranging from 0.4B to 405B parameters. Identified a "direction of ignorance" in the unembedding matrix corresponding to the unigram distribution of training data. Used projection techniques to compute a prior loading factor λ that measures model reliance on prior knowledge.

Result: Found consistent "direction of ignorance" structure across all examined models. The prior loading factor λ declines as context becomes more informative. Demonstrated that projection decomposes prediction states into orthogonal components matching tempered Bayesian update factors. Showed λ is comparable across model families and sizes, with larger models having lower prior reliance in high-context scenarios. Proved causal control: manipulating λ steers predictions toward or away from unigram prior in KL divergence.

Conclusion: Language models encode Bayesian priors in their geometric structure as a "direction of ignorance" that serves as a fallback when uncertain. This discovery provides both interpretability (explaining model behavior) and controllability (enabling steering of prior reliance), revealing fundamental connections between geometric representations and probabilistic reasoning in transformers.

Abstract: What does a language model predict when it has few clues? The answer lurks in its unembedding geometry: a single direction of the unembedding matrix encodes the unigram distribution of the training corpus, which serves as the Bayesian prior the model falls back on when uncertain. This structure --- which we term the \emph{direction of ignorance} --- appears in all four model families examined (\texttt{Llama}, \texttt{Qwen}, \texttt{Gemma}, and \texttt{Pythia}), ranging from 0.4B to 405B parameters. Projecting the final prediction state onto this direction yields a per-token \emph{prior loading factor} $λ$, which, empirically, declines steadily as the context becomes more informative. Formally, the same projection decomposes the prediction state into two orthogonal vectors that correspond exactly to the two factors of a tempered Bayesian update: a unigram prior raised to the exponent $λ$ and a context-driven likelihood. This geometric-probabilistic interpretation calibrates $λ$, making it meaningfully comparable across model sizes and families, with larger models generally exhibiting lower prior reliance in the high-context limit. Finally, we show that the direction of ignorance is causally active: raising or lowering $λ$ at the final prediction state steers the prediction toward or away from the unigram prior in KL divergence.

</details>


### [71] [Equation Recast for Canonical Operator Learning Across Parametric PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02982)
*Qiyun Cheng,Valentin Duruisseaux,Cesar F. Clauser,Md Hossain Sahadath,Huihua Yang,Shaowu Pan,Nathaniel Ferraro,Anima Anandkumar,Wei Ji,Cristina Rea*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel "equation recast" method reformulates parametric PDE operator learning into a single canonical operator, enabling zero-shot prediction across new parameter regimes without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current parametric operator learning requires extensive training data across parameter ranges and fails silently outside training distributions. There's a need for more efficient, generalizable, and monitorable neural PDE solvers.

Method: Equation recast transforms parametric operator learning into learning a single canonical operator by analytically deriving parameter-induced variations from governing equations and absorbing them into effective sources. This enables zero-shot prediction across new parameters and provides convergence loss as a warning signal.

Result: The method demonstrates extrapolation capabilities across multi-parameter, nonlinear, and singular PDEs, integrates heterogeneous datasets, and successfully unifies electron-temperature data across four tokamak geometries in nuclear fusion simulations through canonical-domain mapping within a single trained operator.

Conclusion: Equation recast provides a pathway toward reusable neural PDE solvers that combine equation-guided transfer, data efficiency, and monitorable inference, addressing key limitations in current parametric operator learning approaches.

Abstract: Learning solution operators across broad parameter ranges can require substantial coverage of both input functions and physical parameters, particularly for purely data-driven parametric models. In addition, the resulting models may fail silently outside the training distribution. We introduce equation recast, which reformulates parametric operator learning as the learning of a single canonical operator. Parameter-induced operator variations are derived analytically from the governing equation and absorbed into effective sources, enabling zero-shot prediction across new parameter regimes. Across multi-parameter, nonlinear, and singular PDE settings, equation recast supports extrapolation, integrates sparse heterogeneous datasets in a shared canonical representation, and uses loss of convergence as an internal warning signal for failure of the recast iteration. In high-fidelity tokamak simulations for nuclear fusion, the framework unifies electron-temperature data across four device geometries through canonical-domain mapping within one jointly trained operator. Equation recast provides a route toward reusable neural PDE solvers combining equation-guided transfer, data efficiency, and monitorable inference.

</details>


### [72] [From Euclidean to Graph-Structured Data: A Survey of Collaborative Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02984)
*Rémi Bourgerie,Šarūnas Girdzijauskas,Viktoria Fodor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This survey paper explores how collaborative learning approaches can be extended from traditional Euclidean data to graph-structured data, addressing scalability and privacy limitations of centralized learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Centralized machine learning faces fundamental limitations in scalability and privacy. While collaborative approaches like federated and decentralized learning address these for Euclidean data, they fail to handle relational patterns in graph-structured data, which is crucial for many real-world applications.

Method: The paper provides a comprehensive survey that first reviews foundational principles of collaborative learning for Euclidean data along three dimensions: learning effectiveness, efficiency, and privacy preservation. Then extends the discussion to graph-structured data by introducing a taxonomy of graph distribution scenarios, characterizing statistical heterogeneities, and developing standardized problem formulations and algorithmic frameworks.

Result: The survey consolidates the emerging field of collaborative learning on graphs by systematically organizing current knowledge, identifying key challenges, and developing standardized frameworks for analyzing graph-structured data in collaborative settings.

Conclusion: Learning on graph-structured data in collaborative environments represents an underexplored but promising research direction with unique opportunities and challenges. The survey aims to provide a foundation for future research by systematically organizing the field and identifying key open problems and promising directions.

Abstract: The conventional approach to machine learning, that is, collecting data, training models, and performing inference in a single location, faces fundamental limitations, including scalability and privacy, that restrict its applicability. To address these challenges, recent research has explored collaborative learning approaches, including federated learning and decentralized learning, where individual agents perform training and inference locally, with limited collaboration. Most collaborative learning research focuses on Euclidean data with regular, grid-like structure (e.g., images, text). However, these approaches fail to capture the relational patterns in many real-world applications, best represented by graphs. Learning on graphs relies on message-passing mechanisms to propagate information between connected nodes, making it conceptually well-suited for collaborative environments where agents must exchange information. Yet, the opportunities and challenges of learning on graph-structured data in collaborative settings remain largely underexplored. This survey provides a comprehensive investigation of collaborative learning from Euclidean to graph-structured data, aiming to consolidate this emerging field. We begin by reviewing its foundational principles for Euclidean data, organizing them along three core dimensions: learning effectiveness, efficiency, and privacy preservation. We then extend the discussion to graph-structured data, introducing a taxonomy of graph distribution scenarios, characterizing associated statistical heterogeneities, and developing standardized problem formulations and algorithmic frameworks. Finally, we systematically identify open challenges and promising research directions.

</details>


### [73] [Modern Transformers Are Implicit Hybrids: From Functional Differentiation to Principled Hybrid Architecture Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02986)
*Runlin Shi,Bojian Yin,Guoqi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Head-wise Hybrid Architecture (HwH), a principled hybrid attention design that separates positional modeling and retrieval functions at head granularity using RoPE frequency analysis, achieving better long-context extrapolation with reduced FA usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current hybrid FA-LA architectures use heuristic allocations; the authors seek evidence-grounded basis in head-level functional organization learned by RoPE-based Transformers, aiming to understand and improve attention mechanisms for better long-context handling.

Method: Proposed two intervention metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS) to measure frequency effects on attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD) to isolate rotary positional modulation. Used these to analyze Qwen3-series and Llama3.1 models, then designed Head-wise Hybrid Architecture (HwH) using NoPE FA for global retrieval and LA for local positional modeling.

Result: RFIS and RPD revealed a complete taxonomy separating retrieval and positional heads by a Global Positional Band (GPBand). HwH with FA-to-LA ratio below 1:3 maintains strong language modeling and commonsense reasoning while improving retrieval and substantially strengthening zero-shot long-context extrapolation over baseline models.

Conclusion: Principled hybrid architecture design with head-level functional separation (global retrieval via NoPE FA, local positional modeling via LA) is a promising direction for foundation models, addressing length extrapolation issues through granular attention specialization.

Abstract: Hybrid architectures combining Full Attention (FA) and Linear Attention (LA) are increasingly prominent, yet their allocation remains heuristic. We seek an evidence-grounded basis in head-level functional organization learned by RoPE-based Transformers. Behavioral probes do not yield a complete taxonomy, so we propose two intervention metrics: RoPE Frequency Importance Score (RFIS), measuring how each frequency affects a head's attention distribution, and RoPE Positional Dependence (RPD), isolating dependence on rotary positional modulation. On Qwen3-series models and Llama3.1, RFIS suggests and RPD verifies a complete taxonomy of retrieval and positional heads separated by a salient mid-low-frequency band. Controlled Transformers show that this boundary follows the training-length positional scale; we term it the Global Positional Band (GPBand). The analysis suggests a potential cause of zero-shot length-extrapolation failure and yields two principles: positional modeling should operate only locally, with global access through position-independent retrieval; and both functions should be assigned at head granularity with layer-specific allocation. We instantiate them in Head-wise Hybrid Architecture (HwH), using NoPE FA for global retrieval and LA for local positional modeling. With an FA-to-LA ratio below 1:3, HwH retains strong language modeling and commonsense reasoning while improving retrieval and substantially strengthening zero-shot long-context extrapolation over Transformer, LA, and a layer-wise hybrid baseline. Ablations validate both principles and component roles, highlighting principled hybrid architecture design as a promising route toward future foundation models.

</details>


### [74] [Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02987)
*Shrinivas Ramasubramanian,Daman Arora,Fahim Tajwar,Guanning Zeng,Qingyang Wu,Zhongzhu Zhou,Chenfeng Xu,Haiwen Feng,Yuda Song,Aarti Singh,Ruslan Salakhutdinov,J. Andrew Bagnell,Jeff Schneider,Andrea Zanette*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning (TailRL) optimizes for upper tail reward coverage rather than just average reward, making policies better at achieving rare high-reward outcomes and benefiting more from increased sampling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL optimizes average reward, but this can hide important distinctions: two policies with the same mean reward can have very different probabilities of producing rare but high-reward outcomes. This matters because the benefit of increased sampling during training and inference depends on retaining probability mass on these high-reward outcomes.

Method: Instead of just expected reward, TailRL considers all upper tails of reward distribution: for each reward threshold, it measures how likely the policy is to exceed it. This turns continuous reward into a family of binary success events. TailRL maximizes the log-probability of exceeding a randomly chosen reward threshold, requiring only a simple modification to the advantage function (making it compatible with existing RL pipelines).

Result: TailRL leverages rare high-reward training samples to avoid suboptimal solutions and yields models that benefit more from additional samples at inference time. Results show effectiveness across diverse domains including object localization, maze navigation, GUI grounding, and code optimization.

Conclusion: Optimizing reward upper tails directly (rather than just average reward) produces policies with better coverage of rare high-reward outcomes, making them more sample-efficient and better at leveraging increased sampling during inference. The simple advantage function modification makes TailRL widely applicable to existing RL pipelines.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning typically optimizes average reward. For generative policies, the average can hide an important distinction: two policies can achieve the same mean reward while having very different chances of producing a rare but high-reward rollout. This matters as sampling increases during training and inference, since its benefit depends on retaining probability mass on high-reward outcomes. We propose to optimize this coverage directly. Rather than considering only expected reward, we consider all of its upper tails: for each reward threshold, how likely is the policy to exceed it? This turns a continuous reward into a family of binary success events. We introduce Tail-Likelihood Reinforcement Learning (TailRL), which maximizes the log-probability of exceeding a randomly chosen reward threshold. Its gradient gives more weight to rare, high-reward rollouts and can be interpreted as a mixture of Best-of-(k) gradients. TailRL requires only a simple modification to the advantage function, making it compatible with existing reinforcement learning pipelines. Across object localization, maze navigation, GUI grounding, and code optimization, TailRL leverages rare high-reward training samples to avoid suboptimal solutions and yields models that benefit more from additional samples at inference time.

</details>


### [75] [Mesh-Native Physics-Informed Graph Surrogates for TCAD-in-the-Loop Design Space Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02988)
*Leonid Popryho,Ayoub Sadeghi,Inna Partin-Vaisband*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-informed graph attention network (GAT) surrogate is developed to rapidly predict TCAD simulation results for FinFET devices, enabling fast design space exploration while preserving physical accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional TCAD simulation of FinFET devices is computationally expensive, especially for 3D structures with complex meshes, which severely limits multi-objective design space exploration. Existing ML surrogates lack physics fidelity and transferability across device geometries.

Method: The proposed method uses a physics-informed graph attention network that operates directly on tetrahedral TCAD meshes as graphs. It predicts electrostatic potential, electron quasi-Fermi levels, and hole quasi-Fermi levels at every mesh node. Training combines data loss with finite-volume current-continuity residuals to embed carrier-transport physics. The model includes uncertainty estimation and active learning for efficient sampling.

Result: The surrogate reproduces drift-diffusion fields with sub-volt per-field RMSE and achieves orders of magnitude higher throughput than full TCAD simulation. It demonstrates size generalization - models trained on few-fin meshes work on larger arrays. Inference completes in under a second for large multi-fin arrays, enabling Pareto-front exploration at scales infeasible for direct TCAD.

Conclusion: The physics-informed GAT surrogate successfully addresses the computational bottleneck of TCAD simulation while preserving physical accuracy, enabling rapid exploration of FinFET design spaces across device scales through size generalization and active learning.

Abstract: High-fidelity TCAD simulation of drift-diffusion transport remains the workhorse of emerging FinFET device design, but it is computationally expensive, especially for 3D structures where runtime escalates steeply with mesh complexity. This sharply limits multi-objective design space exploration. Existing machine-learning surrogates map a fixed set of design parameters to a few scalar device metrics, discarding the underlying physics and losing transferability across device geometries and families. A physics-informed graph attention network (GAT) surrogate is proposed. It operates directly on the tetrahedral TCAD mesh and predicts, at every mesh node, the electrostatic potential together with the electron and hole quasi-Fermi levels, the fundamental unknowns of the drift-diffusion system. Training combines a data loss with finite-volume current-continuity residuals, embedding carrier-transport physics into the objective. Operating on the mesh as a graph, the surrogate inherits size generalization: a model trained on few-fin meshes applies unchanged to substantially larger arrays, bounded at inference only by GPU memory. Per-node uncertainty from a deep ensemble drives an active-learning loop that screens large candidate pools in seconds and forwards only the most informative designs for full simulation. Benchmarked against Sentaurus Device on multi-fin tri-gate FinFETs, the surrogate reproduces the three drift-diffusion fields with sub-volt per-field RMSE and reaches a per-design throughput orders of magnitude higher than the full simulator. The advantage grows with device size: on large multi-fin arrays that are prohibitively slow to simulate directly, inference still completes in under a second per device, enabling Pareto-front exploration across device scales infeasible for direct TCAD sweeps.

</details>
