### [76] [TRACE: Spatiotemporal Contact Memory Graph Network Simulator for Granular Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02991)
*Changjian Zhou,Negin Yousefpour,Jie Qi,Junfeng Fang,Guillermo A. Narsilio,Hans Petter Jostad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE is a graph-network simulator for granular dynamics that stores interaction history on contact edges using persistent memory and attention-based message passing, achieving stable long-horizon predictions with improved accuracy and speed over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Granular motion depends strongly on inter-granular contact history, which is difficult to preserve when particle contacts dynamically form, break, and rearrange. Existing simulators mainly store temporal information at the node level, but preserving interaction history across changing contact graphs remains challenging.

Method: TRACE stores interaction history directly on contact edges using persistent memory updated by attention-based message passing and a gated recurrent unit (GRU). An edge-identity dictionary preserves memory as the contact graph changes. A physics-structured decoder predicts contact forces, enforces Coulomb friction limits, and applies equal-and-opposite internal forces. Training involves single-step pretraining followed by autoregressive rollout fine-tuning.

Result: TRACE produces stable, physically consistent long-horizon rollouts that closely reproduce final deposit geometry and kinetic energy release during collapse. It reduces long-rollout position error by 31-62% and final-deposit error by 58-89% compared to GNS and NMGNS, while using fewer parameters and maintaining near-zero particle interpenetration. It also achieves 12.2× and 8.9× speedups over MPM reference solver in 2D and 3D respectively.

Conclusion: TRACE successfully addresses the challenge of preserving contact history in granular dynamics simulations by storing memory on edges rather than nodes, leading to more accurate, stable, and efficient predictions than existing graph-based simulators and reference solvers.

Abstract: Learned graph simulators provide an efficient alternative to high-fidelity solvers for granular dynamics. However, granular motion depends strongly on inter-granular contact history, which is difficult to preserve when particle contacts form, break, and rearrange. Existing simulators mainly store temporal information in node features or node-level memory. Here we introduce TRACE, a graph-network simulator that stores interaction history directly on contact edges. Each edge maintains a persistent memory updated by attention-based message passing and a gated recurrent unit, while an edge-identity dictionary preserves this memory as the contact graph changes. A physics-structured decoder predicts inter-granular normal and tangential contact forces, enforces the Coulomb friction limit, and applies equal-and-opposite internal forces. The model is trained with single-step pretraining followed by autoregressive rollout fine-tuning. We evaluate TRACE on 2D and 3D granular column-collapse benchmarks. In both cases, TRACE produces stable, physically consistent long-horizon rollouts, closely reproducing the final deposit geometry and the kinetic energy released during collapse. Compared with graph network simulator (GNS) and node-memory graph neural simulator (NMGNS), TRACE reduces long-rollout position error by 31-62% and final-deposit error by 58-89% across the two benchmarks, while using fewer parameters and maintaining near-zero particle interpenetration. TRACE also achieves 12.2$\times$ and 8.9$\times$ speedups over the material point method (MPM) reference solver in 2D and 3D, respectively. Our code is available at https://github.com/Data-Driven-Computational-Geotechnics/TRACE.

</details>


### [77] [No-Regret Bayesian Optimization with Finite-Library Input-Warped Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02993)
*Edvin Ketabati Augustinsson,Robert A. Bridges*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Finite-Library Input-Warped Bayesian Optimization (FLIWBO) improves GP-BO efficiency by adaptively selecting input warping from a finite library, maintaining convergence guarantees even when raw coordinate geometry is misspecified.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard GP-BO methods like GP-UCB have convergence guarantees but require fixed kernels. However, kernel geometry often mismatches real problems (e.g., log-scaled hyperparameters, localized peaks), where input warping could improve sample efficiency. Existing GP-UCB proofs can't handle adaptive warping.

Method: Proposes FLIWBO which selects input warps from a finite library of smooth input maps using any history-dependent rule. This adapts input geometry to accelerate learning while maintaining convergence guarantees with explicit library-size cost bounds.

Result: FLIWBO-UCB outperforms raw-coordinate GP-UCB under misspecified geometry, escapes traps that defeat even oracle-warp expected improvement, recovers most gains from manual log scaling, and leads methods with matching regret guarantees. Validated across synthetic objectives, confidence-fence traps, Fashion-MNIST HPO, and 20D MAS design with noisy evaluations.

Conclusion: FLIWBO provides a principled way to incorporate input warping into Bayesian optimization while maintaining theoretical convergence guarantees, bridging the gap between practical performance improvements and theoretical robustness in GP-BO methods.

Abstract: Gaussian-process Bayesian optimization (GP-BO) excels at black-box optimization of costly functions, e.g., hyperparameter optimization (HPO) and multi-agent system (MAS) design. Convergence-rate guarantees exist for select methods, notably GP upper confidence bound (GP-UCB), but require a fixed kernel. Critically, the kernel encodes how input proximity affects objective value similarity. When raw coordinates poorly match this geometry - as with log-scaled hyperparameters or localized peaks - input warping can greatly improve sample efficiency, yet known GP-UCB proofs require a fixed kernel. We propose Finite-Library Input-Warped Bayesian Optimization (FLIWBO), which selects warps from a finite library of smooth input maps by any history-dependent rule. It adapts the input geometry to accelerate learning while retaining high-probability convergence guarantees under mild hypotheses, with an explicit $\sqrt(N_\varepsilon)$ library-size cost. Controlled diagnostics show that finite-library warping repairs planted geometry mismatches and identify FLIWBO failure cases. Across four repeated benchmarks - warped synthetic objectives, a confidence-fence trap, and Fashion-MNIST HPO - FLIWBO-UCB beats raw-coordinate GP-UCB under misspecified geometry, escapes traps that defeat even oracle-warp expected improvement, and recovers much of the gain from manual log scaling, while leading the tested methods that admit a matching regret guarantee. A 20-dimensional MAS design study further shows feasibility under costly noisy evaluations. Code for experiments is available: https://github.com/edvin-ketabati/bogp-paper-experiments.

</details>


### [78] [Evaluating Graph Neural Networks for Change-Criticality Classification in Maritime Navigation Charts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02996)
*Abhishek Potnis,Jacob Arndt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GNNs applied to graph-structured ENC data effectively classify navigational safety risks from chart changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maintaining electronic navigational charts (ENCs) is challenging, and automatically classifying changes based on navigational safety significance is important for improving maintenance workflows.

Method: Represent ENC vector datasets as graphs with spatial objects as nodes and relationships as edges; encode old and new ENC datasets into graph pairs; frame task as graph-pair classification; investigate various GNN architectures and configurations.

Result: Graph-based representations improve classification of ENC updates; several GNN architectures trained on expert-reviewed ENC changes demonstrate effectiveness for risk classification.

Conclusion: Graph-based approaches using GNNs provide a scalable solution for automating ENC maintenance by effectively classifying navigational safety risks from chart changes.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are a class of neural networks suitable for learning on graph-structured data. Their application to spatial data is a natural extension, however its relatively unclear which message-passing operations, architectural configurations, and graph representation is best suited for classifying changes to objects in electronic navigational charts (ENCs)--geospatial vector datasets used for marine navigation. Maintaining these datasets is a challenge, and categorizing changes to objects in the ENC based on their significance to navigational safety is of particular importance. Here, we propose to represent these vector navigation datasets as a graph structure where the spatial objects serve as nodes and their spatial and semantic relationships form edges. We encode both the old ENC dataset and new ENC dataset into a pair of graphs and frame the task as a graph-pair classification problem. Building on this representation, we investigate the use of GNN architectures to classify whether the encoded graphs constitutes a critical or non-critical risk to navigational safety. We train and evaluate several GNN architectures and model configurations on ENC changes reviewed by maritime experts. Our results demonstrate that graph-based representations improve the classification of ENC updates, providing a scalable approach for automating or improving ENC maintenance workflows.

</details>


### [79] [Verify Before You Distill: Prompt-Level Teacher Gating for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02998)
*Zhiwei Zhang,Zechen Sun,Fei Zhao,Kang Peng,Bin Liang,Huayu Deng,Yao Hu,Kam-Fai Wong,Mu Chuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TGOPD improves on-policy distillation by adding prompt-level teacher reliability checks before applying dense supervision, outperforming vanilla OPD across multiple domains and scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vanilla On-policy Distillation (OPD) applies uniform token-level supervision from a frozen teacher without checking teacher reliability per prompt. Since reverse KL is mode-seeking, a confidently wrong teacher can give misleading updates. Existing distributional proxies don't directly verify outcome correctness.

Method: TGOPD introduces prompt-level teacher reliability verification: estimates reliability from small set of verifier-scored teacher probes, then routes each prompt exclusively to dense OPD when reliability check passes, or to verifier-grounded GRPO otherwise. Uses otherwise-idle teacher capacity for reliability estimation.

Result: Outperforms vanilla OPD in all six single-domain settings (mathematics, code, instruction following) for 4B and 35B students. Achieves higher seven-benchmark averages at both scales under multi-domain training. Reduces teacher-side compute waste from 9.8% to 78.9% GPU utilization in 4B single-domain run.

Conclusion: TGOPD provides effective mechanism to verify teacher reliability before applying dense supervision, improving performance across domains and scales while optimizing compute utilization by repurposing idle teacher capacity for reliability estimation.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) accelerates post-training by providing dense token-level supervision from a frozen teacher on the student's own rollouts. Vanilla OPD applies this supervision uniformly across prompts, without checking whether the teacher is reliable for each prompt. Because reverse KL is mode-seeking, a confidently wrong teacher can induce a strong yet misleading update. Distributional proxies, such as entropy or teacher-student likelihood agreement, measure uncertainty or agreement but do not directly verify outcome correctness. We introduce Teacher-Gated On-Policy Distillation (TGOPD), built on the principle that teacher reliability should be verified at the prompt level before dense supervision is admitted. TGOPD estimates reliability from a small set of verifier-scored teacher probes and routes each prompt exclusively to dense OPD when the reliability check passes or to verifier-grounded GRPO otherwise. Across 4B and 35B students in mathematics, code, and instruction following, TGOPD outperforms Vanilla OPD in all six single-domain settings and achieves higher seven-benchmark averages at both scales under multi-domain training. By using otherwise-idle teacher capacity for reliability estimation, TGOPD also reduces teacher-side compute waste in asynchronous OPD, increasing teacher-node GPU utilization from 9.8% to 78.9% in the measured 4B single-domain run.

</details>


### [80] [Causal Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03003)
*Christopher Stith,Hossein Rahmani,Jesse C. Cresswell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A practical introduction to causal foundation models (CFMs), which bring the foundation model paradigm to causal inference by enabling pretrained neural networks to estimate causal effects on new datasets using in-context learning without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional causal inference requires custom pipelines for each new problem (proposing causal mechanisms, selecting estimators, training). Meanwhile, machine learning has shifted to foundation models (pretrained once, applied to new tasks without fine-tuning). CFMs aim to bring this paradigm shift to causal inference.

Method: CFMs are pretrained neural networks that use in-context learning to estimate causal quantities (like average treatment effect) on new datasets without requiring model updates or retraining. The paper provides practical introduction with example code and Jupyter notebooks.

Result: CFMs represent an emerging approach that enables efficient causal inference across diverse settings by leveraging pretrained models that can adapt to new problems through in-context learning rather than requiring bespoke pipelines for each case.

Conclusion: CFMs bridge the gap between traditional causal inference methods and modern foundation model paradigms, offering a more scalable and flexible approach to estimating treatment effects across various contexts and datasets without the need for problem-specific model development.

Abstract: Causal inference is the practice of estimating the effect of a treatment or intervention from data. It traditionally requires a bespoke pipeline for every new problem: first proposing a causal mechanism, selecting a compatible estimator, and finally training it. Meanwhile, across diverse settings and modalities, much of machine learning has shifted to the paradigm of foundation models: networks pretrained once at scale and applied to new tasks without fine-tuning. Causal foundation models (CFMs) bring this paradigm to causal inference. CFMs are pretrained neural networks that estimate causal quantities, such as the average treatment effect, on entirely new datasets using in-context learning without requiring model updates. This work provides a practical introduction to this emerging area. We summarize the necessary background in causal inference and machine learning before discussing CFMs. Throughout, we include example code and Jupyter notebooks.

</details>


### [81] [Risk and Anomaly Identification for Distribution Network Optimal Operation Based on Reinforcement Learning and Uncertainty Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03308)
*Ziqi Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework for joint risk and anomaly identification in distribution networks, using uncertainty quantification to distinguish between inherent stochastic risks and out-of-distribution anomalies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern distribution networks face operational risks and anomalous events under pervasive uncertainty. Operators need to distinguish between: 1) inherent risks from stochastic but in-distribution conditions, and 2) anomalies from out-of-distribution behaviors like unusual load patterns, extreme weather, or cyber-physical attacks.

Method: The authors propose a deep reinforcement learning framework that integrates distributional and Bayesian deep reinforcement learning to create a second-order uncertainty quantification scheme. This decomposes total uncertainty into aleatoric (inherent randomness) and epistemic (model uncertainty) components, which are used to characterize inherent risk and out-of-distribution anomalies respectively.

Result: The framework uses epistemic uncertainty estimates for both exploration during training and out-of-distribution detection with fallback control during deployment. Aleatoric uncertainty estimates characterize intrinsic operational risk. Simulation results demonstrate the performance of the DRL agent and the effectiveness of the uncertainty quantification approach.

Conclusion: The proposed uncertainty-aware deep reinforcement learning framework successfully addresses the joint risk and anomaly identification problem for distribution network operation by leveraging decomposed uncertainty estimates, enabling better distinction between normal operational risks and anomalous events.

Abstract: Reliable operation of modern distribution networks requires timely identification of operational risks and anomalous events under pervasive uncertainty. In practice, operators must identify risks that are inherent in stochastic yet in-distribution conditions, and anomalies that correspond to out-of-distribution behaviors such as unusual load patterns, extreme weather or cyber-physical attacks. This paper addresses this joint risk and anomaly identification problem for optimal distribution network operation and proposes a deep reinforcement learning framework that is explicitly uncertainty aware. We integrate distributional and Bayesian deep reinforcement learning to realize a second- order uncertainty quantification scheme that decomposes total uncertainty into aleatoric and epistemic components, which are respectively used to characterize inherent risk and out-of- distribution anomalies. The resulting epistemic estimates drive both exploration during training and out-of-distribution detec- tion with fallback control during deployment, whereas aleatoric estimates are used to characterize intrinsic operational risk. Simulation results demonstrate the performance of our DRL agent and the effectiveness of the uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [82] [ObserverBench: Testing Mechanistic Estimates for Intervention and Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03026)
*Vijay Erramilli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ObserverBench is a benchmark framework for evaluating whether internal estimators (observers) in mechanistic interpretability are adequate for guiding interventions like activation steering, circuit removal, and safety monitoring, measuring both estimation accuracy and resulting action losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability is increasingly used to guide interventions, but an internal estimate that is accurate on average can still lead to poor actions. There's a need to test whether internal estimators are actually adequate for the specific intervention, control, or safety tasks they're meant to direct.

Method: ObserverBench provides a benchmark framework with fixed task contracts that define: the model, information boundary, allowed actions, decision rule, held-out cases, and loss. The benchmark separately reports estimation accuracy from the loss caused by chosen actions, allowing evaluation of interpretability methods through the actions they enable.

Result: Experiments show that in closed-loop control, observer errors matter at starting points and directions interventions can reach. On circuit-intervention tasks in GPT-2-small and Qwen2.5-7B, pairwise observers predict unseen effects more accurately without always choosing better actions, while observers trained on action loss choose lower-loss actions. In safety triage, AUROC can rank monitors differently from deployment loss, and the best information source varies across models. Sparse SAE readouts trail dense controls under certain conditions.

Conclusion: Both estimation accuracy and action loss are needed to evaluate internal estimators for interpretability-guided interventions. ObserverBench provides fixed task contracts, runnable baselines, and table-based submissions for proper evaluation of interpretability methods through their practical utility in enabling better actions.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability is increasingly used to guide interventions such as activation steering, circuit removal, and safety monitoring. Yet an internal estimate that is accurate on average can still choose a poor action.
  We present ObserverBench, a benchmark framework for testing whether an internal estimator---an observer---is adequate for the intervention, control, or safety task it directs. Each task fixes the model, information boundary, allowed actions, decision rule, held-out cases, and loss. The benchmark reports estimation accuracy separately from the loss caused by the chosen action.
  Theory and experiments show why both are needed. In closed-loop control, observer errors matter at the starting point and along directions the allowed intervention can reach. On circuit-intervention tasks in GPT-2-small and Qwen2.5-7B, pairwise observers predict unseen effects more accurately without always choosing better actions; observers trained on action loss choose lower-loss actions. In safety triage, a score that perfectly separates violations can allocate a fixed intervention budget poorly when violations have different costs. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma-2-9B-it, and prospectively frozen Qwen3.5-9B APPS tasks, AUROC can rank monitors differently from deployment loss, and the best information source changes across models. Sparse SAE readouts also trail their layer-matched dense controls on the reported Qwen panels, under disclosed activation-density or checkpoint mismatches.
  ObserverBench provides fixed task contracts, runnable baselines, and table-based submissions for evaluating interpretability methods through the actions they enable.

</details>


### [83] [Inferred Generative-Process Diversity Predicts Correlated Failure Across Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03422)
*Ross Tieman,Evan Markou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "generative-process diversity" as a more fundamental measure than semantic diversity for language model populations, showing it predicts reduced correlated failures in multi-model systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current assessments of language model diversity focus on semantic similarity of outputs, which only captures differences in meaning but misses fundamental differences in how models generate outputs. This is important for building resilient multi-model systems since models may appear different semantically but still have correlated failures if their underlying generative processes are similar.

Method: Using Algorithmic Information Theory, the authors measure generative-process diversity using Normalised Compression Distance between raw model outputs, residualised against a permutation control. They apply this to 38 language models and analyze its relationship with correlated failures across ten disjoint benchmark families.

Result: The measure identifies population structure missed by semantic similarity and predicts cross-task variation in chance-corrected correlated failure among model pairs. The cross-benchmark partial rank association is -0.216 (95% CI: [-0.309, -0.122]), with negative estimates on all ten benchmarks, showing increased generative-process diversity is associated with reduced correlated failure independent of semantic similarity and model capability.

Conclusion: Generative-process diversity offers a novel and practical approach for investigating diversity in multi-model systems, particularly for safety-relevant contexts where reducing correlated failures is crucial for system resilience.

Abstract: Diversity is a widely observed factor in the resilient function of collective systems, yet the type of diversity that matters depends on the properties and failure modes of the system. This distinction is important for systems composed of multiple language models. Different models may be treated as independent components even when their behaviour and failures remain strongly correlated. Assessments of language-model populations using semantic similarity demonstrate limited semantic diversity, but this captures only differences in the meaning of observed outputs. We argue that a more fundamental notion of model diversity is generative-process diversity, the differences between processes capable of generating the observed outputs. Drawing from Algorithmic Information Theory, we use Normalised Compression Distance between raw model outputs, residualised against a permutation control, as a measure of inferred generative-process diversity. Across 38 language models, this measure identifies population structure missed by semantic similarity and predicts cross-task variation in chance-corrected correlated failure among model pairs across ten disjoint benchmark families, beyond semantic similarity and model-pair capability. The cross-benchmark partial rank association is $-0.216$ with a 95% interval of $[-0.309,-0.122]$, and the estimate is negative on all ten benchmarks. These results indicate that increased generative-process diversity is associated with reduced correlated failure in model pairs that is not attributable to semantic similarity or capability. Inferred generative-process diversity offers a novel and practical approach for investigating diversity of multi-model systems in safety-relevant contexts.

</details>


### [84] [Learnable composition for neural operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03069)
*Zituo Chen,Baiming Zhang,Sili Deng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentDDM is a neural operator framework that co-designs local pretraining on small subdomains with lightweight adaptation to new physical settings, reducing deployment costs by requiring fewer high-fidelity target simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural operators degrade in accuracy when domain geometry, size, or operating conditions differ from training. Supervised adaptation requires costly high-fidelity simulations for even small target sets, creating a need for more efficient transfer learning approaches in physical simulation.

Method: LatentDDM pretrains a neural operator on small subdomains to predict local fields. For new settings, it freezes this operator and trains only a lightweight composition module that assembles local predictions into global solutions. This enables transfer with minimal target data.

Result: The method achieves 36-56% lower error on larger Darcy domains after adaptation with only 16 target simulations compared to capacity-matched full-domain models. It also improves 20-step field rollouts in fast-pitching airfoil flow, both zero-shot and after few-shot calibration.

Conclusion: Co-designed local pretraining and composition-level transfer represents a promising design principle for physical foundation models, enabling efficient adaptation to new settings with reduced computational cost.

Abstract: Neural operators are fast, differentiable surrogates for physical simulation, but their accuracy often degrades when domain geometry, size, or operating conditions differ from training. Supervised adaptation can recover accuracy, but even a small target set requires costly high-fidelity simulations. We therefore ask how pretraining and transfer can be designed together to reduce this deployment cost. LatentDDM first pretrains a neural operator to predict fields on small subdomains. For a new setting, it freezes this operator and trains only a lightweight module that composes the local predictions. We evaluate our method on two complementary problems: steady Darcy flow, where long-range pressure coupling must extend across increasingly large porous domains, and unsteady incompressible flow around a pitching airfoil, where rollout errors compound as target pitching frequencies exceed the training range. Compared with the capacity-matched models that process the full domain at once, LatentDDM's error is 36-56% lower on larger Darcy domains after adaptation with 16 target simulations. It also improves 20-step field rollouts in fast-pitching airfoil flow, both zero-shot and after few-shot calibration. These results identify the co-designed local pretraining and composition-level transfer as a promising design principle for physical foundation models.

</details>


### [85] [LeanStream: A Speculate-and-Refine Streaming Framework for Efficient on-Device LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03079)
*Renyuan Liu,Yuyang Leng,Kaiyan Liu,Yuzhou Zhong,Shaohan Hu,Chun-Fu,Chen,Peijun Zhao,Heechul Yun,Shuochao Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeanStream is a streaming speculate-and-refine framework for efficient on-device LLM inference that enables fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O, significantly reducing memory usage while improving throughput.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference is desirable for privacy and responsiveness but faces challenges due to model weights exceeding available DRAM on mobile/embedded devices. Prior systems using activation sparsity and weight offloading face a fundamental trade-off between accurate sparse execution decisions (requiring latest context) and efficient computation-I/O overlap (requiring early prediction), resulting in serialized execution or redundant fetches/computation.

Method: LeanStream uses a streaming speculate-and-refine framework that progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results. This enables fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O by making execution decisions based on partial results rather than waiting for complete computations.

Result: On both mobile and embedded platforms, LeanStream reduces memory usage by 4.8× to 7.5× at the best throughput achieved by prior work, while further improving token generation throughput by 1.6× to 2.1× compared to prior on-device LLM inference systems.

Conclusion: LeanStream effectively addresses the fundamental systems trade-off in on-device LLM inference by enabling efficient computation-I/O overlap through progressive refinement of priorities using partial results, achieving significant memory reduction and throughput improvements simultaneously.

Abstract: On-device LLM inference is attractive for privacy and responsiveness, but remains challenging on mobile and embedded devices because model weights far exceed available DRAM. Prior systems exploit activation sparsity and offload weights to SSD or flash storage, but face a fundamental systems trade-off: accurate sparse execution decisions require the latest context, whereas efficient computation-I/O overlap requires early prediction. As a result, existing designs either serialize execution or incur redundant weight fetches, extra computation, and large cache overheads. We present LeanStream, a streaming speculate-and-refine framework for efficient on-device LLM inference. LeanStream progressively refines computation, loading, and cache-retention priorities using partial GPU results, enabling fine-grained overlap between GPU execution and storage I/O. We implement LeanStream on both mobile and embedded platforms. Compared with prior on-device LLM inference systems, LeanStream reduces memory usage by 4.8$\times$ to 7.5$\times$ at the best throughput achieved by prior work, while further improving token generation throughput by 1.6$\times$ to 2.1$\times$.

</details>


### [86] [The Gradient Does Not See Rank: Rank-Indifference in Matrix-CODI on ProsQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03090)
*Samuel Larson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Matrix-valued chain-of-thought models show flat accuracy curves when their latent matrices are truncated to low rank, suggesting rank doesn't track parallel reasoning paths as expected, with multiple experiments confirming rank-blindness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether rank in matrix-valued chain-of-thought models serves as a structural observable that tracks parallel reasoning paths through superposition in latent matrices, and whether truncating to low rank would hurt accuracy on multi-component reasoning tasks.

Method: Tested matrix-CODI models across four training regimes (three on ProsQA, one on GSM8K-Aug). Conducted rank-k projection ablation studies, trained models with different readouts (bilinear, bilinear-plus-GELU, SVD-augmented MLP, quadratic), used linear probes on latent matrices, and ran negative controls on vanilla GPT-2 SFT.

Result: Flat rank-k ablation curves within 0.6 percentage points across all experiments; three-seed replication shows 81.0 ± 2.0% accuracy while effective rank varies widely (4, 12, 13); rank-k curves remain flat for all four nonlinear readouts; linear probe on latent matrix underperforms pretrained hidden state; negative control shows flat curves and conflates rank-blindness with position-irrelevance.

Conclusion: Matrix-valued chain-of-thought models are rank-blind - rank doesn't track parallel reasoning paths as hypothesized. The rank-k ablation alone cannot distinguish between rank-blindness and position-irrelevance, challenging the assumption that matrix rank serves as a structural observable of reasoning complexity in these models.

Abstract: Continuous chain-of-thought models compress reasoning into latent tokens. Matrix-valued variants, which route each latent token through a d x d matrix bottleneck, introduce rank as a single-sample structural observable on the latent matrix Z. If matrix latents carry parallel reasoning paths via superposition, rank should track them, and truncating Z to low rank should hurt accuracy on tasks whose solutions plausibly require multiple components. Across four training regimes of a matrix-CODI model (three on ProsQA, one on GSM8K-Aug below the learning threshold), the rank-k projection ablation curve is flat to within 0.6 percentage points. A three-seed replication yields 81.0 +/- 2.0 percentage points accuracy while the final effective rank of Z spans {4, 12, 13}; the loss does not reward any particular rank. To test whether rank-blindness arises from the flatten-then-project readout alone, we trained four readouts: a bilinear reparametrization, a bilinear-plus-GELU readout nonlinear in Z, an SVD-augmented readout feeding singular values through an MLP, and a quadratic readout in Z Z^T. All four rank-k curves remain flat (Spearman p-values 0.63, 0.14, 0.82, 0.46). The flat curves persist for readouts nonlinear in Z. A linear probe on Z underperforms a raw pretrained hidden state at target prediction (AUC 0.673 vs. 0.846). A negative control on vanilla GPT-2 SFT (no matrix bottleneck, no Z, three seeds, n=500) reproduces a flat rank-k curve under the same intervention paradigm with pooled-mean range 0.20pp, and a random-h sensitivity floor lands at the same accuracy: the rank-k ablation alone conflates rank-blindness with position-irrelevance.

</details>


### [87] [Robust PAC Learning of Concurrent Stochastic Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04189)
*Angel Y. He,David Parker*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First PAC learning framework for general-sum concurrent stochastic games with transition uncertainty that handles Nash equilibrium existence problems through a robust approach with certificates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning frameworks for concurrent stochastic games don't properly address the challenge of Nash equilibrium existence when there's transition uncertainty, creating theoretical and practical gaps.

Method: Uses data-driven L1 confidence sets over transition kernels, solves robust CSG for social-welfare optimal ε-NE, employs robust MDP-based exploration for joint state-action coverage, and introduces Nash margin characterization for equilibrium existence reasoning.

Result: Algorithm either returns an ε-approximate NE with social-welfare ε-close to optimal, or provides sound certificate that no exact NE exists; achieves polynomial sample complexity Õ(R²maxH⁴|S|²|A|/(p_reachε²)) under minimum reachability condition.

Conclusion: First PAC framework successfully handles both learning in CSGs with transition uncertainty and the fundamental NE existence problem through a principled robust approach with certificates, demonstrated by theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.

Abstract: We introduce the first Probably Approximately Correct (PAC) learning framework for general-sum concurrent stochastic games (CSGs) with transition uncertainty, while addressing the challenge of Nash equilibrium (NE) existence. Our algorithm maintains data-driven $L^1$ confidence sets over transition kernels and solves a robust CSG to compute a social-welfare optimal $\varepsilon$-NE, using a robust MDP-based exploration mechanism to drive joint state-action coverage. Crucially, we introduce a Nash margin characterisation that enables principled reasoning about equilibrium existence: the framework either returns an $\varepsilon$-approximate NE whose social-welfare value is $\varepsilon$-close to optimal, or provides a sound certificate that no exact NE exists. Under a minimum reachability condition $p_{\mathrm{reach}}>0$ over relevant state-action pairs, the algorithm terminates after a polynomial number of trajectory samples, with sample complexity $\widetilde{O}\left( {R_{\max}^2 H^4 |S|^2 |A| / (p_{\mathrm{reach}} \varepsilon^2)} \right)$. Empirical results on benchmark CSGs demonstrate near-optimal performance, correct handling of equilibrium (non-)existence, and sample complexity consistent with theory.

</details>


### [88] [Distilling deep optical flow stereo methods to retrieve dense three-dimensional wind fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03100)
*Thomas J. Vandal,Dong L. Wu,James L. Carr,Derek J. Posselt,Elise Penn,Tristan Ballard,August Posch,Kate Duffy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes using deep optical flow for stereo matching in atmospheric motion vectors to eliminate NWP dependency, then distills this into a single-satellite student model for global coverage with quality assurance emulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional AMVs have circular dependencies on NWP background states leading to inaccurate heights, high computational costs, and sparse retrievals. Stereo winds improve accuracy but remain computationally heavy with limited coverage requiring multi-satellite overlap.

Method: 1) Replace window-based tracking with deep optical flow for efficient stereo matching, 2) Fine-tune using self-supervised geometric residual loss plus supervised radiosonde reconstruction, 3) Distill stereo teacher into single-satellite student model to eliminate multi-satellite requirements, 4) Student emulates teacher's chi-square and height uncertainties for quality assurance.

Result: Validation shows stereo winds improve performance beyond operational AMVs for water vapor bands (6.2, 6.9, 7.3 μm) with some degradation in long-wave infrared (11.2 μm) band. Student model generates winds across full-disk GEO imagery globally with quality assurance.

Conclusion: Deep optical flow enables efficient stereo wind retrieval that eliminates NWP dependency, and knowledge distillation allows extension to single-satellite operations with maintained quality assurance, providing global coverage improvements over traditional AMVs.

Abstract: Geostationary atmospheric motion vectors (AMVs) provide the dense horizontal wind vectors (u,v) and heights ingested into data assimilation systems. Traditional AMVs track features using window-based cross-correlation and estimate heights via infrared brightness temperatures paired with numerical weather prediction (NWP) background states, creating a circular dependency that yields inaccurate heights, high computational cost, and sparse retrievals. Stereo winds from GEO-GEO and GEO-LEO geometrically resolve heights from parallax shifts across different poses, eliminating NWP dependence and improving accuracy, but they remain computationally heavy with limited coverage. In this work, we replace window-based tracking in stereo matching with deep optical flow for efficient, improved retrieval. Fine-tuning balances a self-supervised geometric residual loss with supervised radiosonde reconstruction. To eliminate multi-satellite overlap requirements, we distill the stereo teacher into a single-satellite student model. Chi-square and height uncertainties from the teacher are emulated by the student for quality assurance. The student generates winds across full-disk GEO imagery globally. Validation compares stereo and student models against radiosondes, operational AMVs, ERA5 reanalysis, and EarthCARE cloud profiles. Results through triple collocation show that stereo winds improve performance beyond operational AMVs for water vapor bands (6.2, 6.9, and 7.3 μm), wit degradation in the long-wave infrared (11.2 μm) band.

</details>


### [89] [Scaling Laws, Tabular Data and Actuarial Ratemaking Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03106)
*Ronald Richman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling laws in actuarial ratemaking show TabM has stronger data scaling than Transformers and MLPs, with Transformers needing inductive biases to scale effectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether scaling laws similar to those in deep learning (where performance improves with model size, data, and compute) also apply to actuarial ratemaking, where data are tabular, noisy, and classical models like GLMs remain competitive.

Method: Used a real-world motor insurance portfolio to train various model families across increasing fractions of training data with multiple random seeds. Evaluated out-of-sample Poisson deviance (a likelihood-based loss for Poisson count predictions where lower values indicate better fit).

Result: All models improve with more data, but scaling exponents differ: TabM shows strongest data scaling. Transformers exhibit weak parameter scaling unless augmented with TabM-style adaptations or self-supervision. Simple increases in Transformer size provide limited gains.

Conclusion: Effective scaling in actuarial tabular tasks depends on architecture and loss function design, not just increasing model size. These scaling regularities provide quantitative guidance for model selection based on available data.

Abstract: Scaling laws in modern deep learning describe how held-out loss improves as model capacity, training data, and compute increase, often following power-law trends. We investigate whether analogous scaling regularities arise in actuarial ratemaking, where data are tabular, heterogeneous, and noisy, and where classical models such as GLMs remain strong baselines. Using a real-world motor insurance portfolio, we train models from different families across increasing fractions of the training data and multiple random seeds, evaluating out-of-sample Poisson deviance, a likelihood-based loss for Poisson count predictions in which lower values indicate better held-out fit. We find that all model families improve with additional data, but scaling exponents differ substantially: TabM exhibits markedly stronger data scaling than purely supervised tabular Transformers and standard MLP baselines. Transformer variants show weak parameter scaling unless augmented with additional inductive biases (TabM-style adaptation or self-supervision). These results provide quantitative guidance on model selection by data regime and suggest that effective scaling on actuarial tabular tasks depends on architecture and loss function objective design, with simple increases in Transformer size providing limited gains.

</details>


### [90] [Kernel Reboot: Breaking the Boundaries of Neural Tangent Kernels for Neural Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03117)
*Amir Mallak,Alaa Maalouf,Lior Wolf,Daniela Rus,Dan Rosenbaum*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NTK-driven neural field methods enable fast, high-quality reconstruction from sparse observations through distilled support sets, meta-learned initialization, and hybrid approaches that bridge analytic kernels with practical few-shot learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural fields struggle with fast high-quality reconstruction from sparse observations. Classical NTK regression provides closed-form fits but is fundamentally linear and cannot accumulate reusable task priors, creating a gap between analytic methods and practical few-shot reconstruction needs.

Method: Three algorithms: 1) NTK-KIP learns a distilled support set of coordinates (and optional labels) for non-linear representation; 2) MetaQuill meta-learns shared initialization for INR with small task-specific weight offsets; 3) MetaQuill-KIP fuses both approaches - seeds with KIP-style warm start then refines small offset around meta-learned initialization.

Result: MetaQuill-KIP achieves high-PSNR reconstructions and semantically plausible inpainting under very sparse observations with lightweight per-instance adaptation, outperforming diffusion-style baselines that require large pretrained generative priors and costly per-image tuning.

Conclusion: NTK-driven neural fields can be made both non-linear and meta-learnable, narrowing the gap between analytic kernels and practical few-shot reconstruction while providing feature learning and reusable priors without heavy computational overhead.

Abstract: Neural fields (NFs) map continuous coordinates to signals such as color or density, but fast high-quality reconstruction from sparse observations remains difficult. Classical Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) regression gives closed-form fits, yet it is fundamentally linear and cannot accumulate reusable task priors. We develop three algorithms that address these gaps. NTK-KIP learns a distilled support set of coordinates (and optional labels) so that a finite NTK can inpaint large missing regions from little observed data, yielding a compact non-linear representation instead of a raw kernel solve. MetaQuill meta-learns a shared initialization for an INR so that new scenes can be adapted by updating only a small task-specific weight offset, which provides true feature learning and a reusable prior. Finally, MetaQuill-KIP fuses both ideas: it seeds the task with a KIP-style non-linear warm start, then refines only that small offset around the meta-learned initialization. MetaQuill-KIP achieves high-PSNR reconstructions and semantically plausible inpainting under very sparse observations, while requiring only lightweight per-instance adaptation, whereas diffusion-style baselines typically depend on large pretrained generative priors and costly per-image tuning. This shows that NTK-driven neural fields can be made both non-linear and meta-learnable, narrowing the gap between analytic kernels and practical few-shot reconstruction.

</details>


### [91] [Routing Is Not Enough: Diagnosing Intra-Adapter Subspace Contention in MoE+LoRA Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03150)
*Mehreen Hossain Chowdhury,Nowshin Mahjabin,Ahmed Shafin Ruhan,Md Azam Hossain,Abu Raihan Mostofa Kamal,Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MoE+LoRA fine-tuning assumes token-level routing prevents negative transfer, but this fails due to gradient interference within adapters. SpawnLoRA dynamically adds sub-adapters inside experts to reduce interference, outperforming standard and rank-adaptive LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-domain fine-tuning with MoE+LoRA assumes token-level routing separates domain-specific updates, preventing negative transfer. The paper aims to test this assumption and address failures when routing separation alone proves insufficient.

Method: Introduced Jaccard routing overlap and adapter-gradient cosine similarity to diagnose interference. Proposed SpawnLoRA, which dynamically adds gated sub-adapters inside MoE experts when adapter-level contention is detected while keeping the router fixed.

Result: Found that even with near-disjoint expert routing (e.g., Python code vs biomedical text), adding biomedical data substantially increases code perplexity. SpawnLoRA effectively reduces negative transfer compared to standard and rank-adaptive LoRA across multiple mixture settings on Phi-tiny-MoE-instruct and OLMoE-1B-7B.

Conclusion: Routing separation alone doesn't prevent negative transfer; interference arises from orthogonal domain gradients competing within the same low-rank adapter subspace. Structural separation inside experts (via SpawnLoRA) provides benefits beyond routing or rank expansion alone.

Abstract: Multi-domain fine-tuning often combines MoE routing with LoRA, assuming that token-level routing separates domain-specific updates. We test this assumption in MoE+LoRA using Python code paired with biomedical text and mathematical reasoning. Although these domains show near-disjoint expert routing, adding biomedical data substantially increases code perplexity, indicating that routing separation alone may not prevent negative transfer. To localize the failure, we introduce Jaccard routing overlap and adapter-gradient cosine similarity, which measure expert sharing and update compatibility, respectively. These diagnostics indicate that interference arises mostly from nearly orthogonal domain gradients competing within the same low-rank adapter subspace. We address this issue with SpawnLoRA, which dynamically adds gated sub-adapters inside MoE experts when adapter-level contention is detected, while keeping the router fixed. We evaluate SpawnLoRA on Phi-tiny-MoE-instruct and OLMoE-1B-7B across multiple mixture settings and find that it effectively reduces negative transfer compared with standard and rank-adaptive LoRA. These results demonstrate that structural separation inside experts provides benefits beyond routing or rank expansion alone.

</details>


### [92] [Frontier LLMs are effective batch optimizers: Assessing reasoning models in continuous and discrete settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03177)
*Frank Hu,Shriram Chennakesavalu,David Graff*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLMs show promise as batch optimizers but perform best in semantic-rich discrete spaces rather than purely numerical optimization tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs have become attractive priors for optimization due to their large-scale pretraining, but their effectiveness as batch optimizers across different optimization settings remains underexplored.

Method: Investigated the performance of current generation frontier LLMs as batch optimizers in both continuous and discrete settings, comparing them against classical non-LLM optimization approaches.

Result: LLMs are competitive zero-shot batch optimizers for numerical test functions but are brittle compared to classical methods. However, they significantly outperform in semantically rich settings where the discrete spaces resemble their pretraining data structure.

Conclusion: LLMs show strong potential as batch optimizers, particularly in semantically rich discrete optimization problems where their pretraining knowledge can be effectively leveraged, though they remain less robust than classical methods for purely numerical optimization tasks.

Abstract: Frontier large language models (LLMs) have become attractive priors for optimization due to their large-scale pretraining that enables them to navigate a variety of optimization settings. However, the effectiveness of modern reasoning LLMs in batch optimization settings remains underexplored. Here we investigate the performance of the current generation of frontier LLMs as batch optimizers in both continuous and discrete settings. We find that while LLMs are competitive zero-shot batch optimizers for numerical test functions, their performance is brittle compared to classical non-LLM optimization approaches. However, LLM priors are significantly better in semantically rich settings, indicating that their batch optimization behavior is highly effective when navigating and reasoning over the discrete spaces most similar in structure to their pretraining data.

</details>


### [93] [Portable Causal Fairness Across Synthetic Data Generator Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03180)
*Steven Golob,Sikha Pentyala,Martine De Cock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Three fairness definitions can be implemented as edge cuts on a generator's causal graph, and this mechanism transfers effectively across nine different generator families (including marginals-based, GANs, and diffusion models) with differential privacy guarantees, with minimal impact on data fidelity and classifier performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Statistical agencies need to release synthetic data instead of sensitive records while ensuring unfair pathways are absent from the generated data. Previous work (DECAF) showed how three fairness definitions can be implemented as edge cuts on a generator's causal graph, but it was unclear whether this mechanism was specific to DECAF or generalizes to other generation methods.

Method: The study ports all three fairness definitions to nine different generators from three unrelated families: marginals-based, GANs, and diffusion models, each with differentially private variants. Experiments cover three levels of formal privacy guarantees, with 2,520 matched-pair runs on Adult and COMPAS datasets to test generalization across methods.

Result: The fairness mechanism transfers effectively everywhere across all generator families. The new causal diffusion backbone yields the fairest release among tested families, with fidelity close to marginals-based methods. Applying the edge cuts barely moves data fidelity, only costs downstream classifiers about 0.07 to 0.15 AUC on average, and adding privacy guarantees doesn't make the data less fair.

Conclusion: The fairness-through-causal-graph-cutting mechanism generalizes beyond DECAF to diverse generator families, works well with differential privacy, and has minimal impact on data utility. Causal diffusion models offer the best fairness-performance tradeoff among the tested approaches.

Abstract: When a statistical agency or regulator releases synthetic data in place of sensitive records, it chooses the generator that produces the table, and can shape that generator so unfair pathways are absent. DECAF made this concrete on one non-private GAN: three fairness definitions become three sets of edge cuts on the generator's causal graph. Whether the mechanism belongs to DECAF, or to causal factorisation itself, was untested. We port all three definitions to nine generators from three unrelated families (marginals-based, GAN, and diffusion, each with differentially private variants), across three levels of formal privacy guarantee, over 2,520 matched-pair runs on Adult and COMPAS datasets. The mechanism transfers everywhere, and our new causal diffusion backbone yields the fairest release of any family we tested, at fidelity close to the marginals tier. Applying the cut barely moves fidelity, only costs a downstream classifier about $0.07$ to $0.15$ AUC on average, and adding privacy guarantees don't make the data less fair.

</details>


### [94] [Language-encoded network topology enables large language models to reason about complex networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03229)
*Ucchwas Talukder Utsha,Sakib Mostafa,James Zou,Md Tauhidul Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioGlyph is a method that converts network topology into an interpretable language of structural roles to help LLMs better reason about network structure, achieving up to 26% accuracy improvements across diverse domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs struggle with network reasoning when networks are presented as edge lists or measurement tables because structural meaning must be inferred. Networks are fundamental across many domains (biology, social systems, infrastructure), and reasoning requires understanding structural elements like centrality, community bridges, and effects of element removal.

Method: BioGlyph combines graph partitioning and structural measurements to identify structural roles (hubs, community cores, cross-community connectors), then uses fixed rules to translate these into a universal vocabulary. The representation describes each element through its structural role, supporting evidence, and semantic consequences without changing the network or LLM.

Result: Across 20 networks spanning 5 domains, BioGlyph improves LLMs' structural reasoning by up to 26 percentage points in system accuracy compared to edge-based, numerical, and learned representations. Gains are more prominent in dense, community-structured networks. In yeast protein-interaction networks, it reveals biological organization: cross-community connectors are enriched for essential genes while peripheral proteins are depleted.

Conclusion: BioGlyph provides an interpretable representation that enhances both language models' and scientists' ability to reason about network structure by explicitly encoding structural roles in semantically interpretable terms.

Abstract: Networks describe systems in biology and beyond, from protein interactions and social relationships to power grids and citation records. Reasoning about such systems requires understanding their structure: which elements are central, which connections bridge separate communities, and how it changes when elements are removed. Although large language models (LLMs) excel at natural language, they struggle with such questions when networks are given as edge lists, sentences or measurement tables, because their structural meaning must be inferred. Here we introduce BioGlyph, which compiles network topology into an interpretable and transferable language of structural roles. BioGlyph combines graph partitioning and structural measurements to identify roles such as hubs, community cores and cross-community connectors, and fixed rules to translate them into a universal vocabulary. The representation describes each element through its structural role, supporting evidence and semantic consequences, leaving both the network and the LLM unchanged. Across twenty networks spanning five domains, BioGlyph substantially improves open LLMs' ability to answer structural reasoning questions, outperforming edge-based, numerical and learned representations by up to 26 percentage points in system accuracy. Ablations show that the gain comes from explicitly encoding structural roles in semantically interpretable terms. The gain is more prominent in dense, community-structured networks and diminishes in sparse networks whose topology is more readily inferred from text. In a budding-yeast protein-interaction network, BioGlyph exposes biological organization: cross-community connectors are enriched for essential genes, whereas peripheral proteins are depleted. BioGlyph thus provides an interpretable representation for both language models and scientists to reason about network structure.

</details>


### [95] [The 2026 PNPL Competition: Word Classification and Efficient Cross-Subject Generalisation in LibriBrain100](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03231)
*Francesco Mantegna,Gereon Elvers,Dulhan Jayalath,Gilad Landau,Tasha Kim,Miran Özdogan,Luisa Kurth,Teyun Kwon,SungJun Cho,Benjamin Ballyk,Alex Fung,Anna Greer,Pratik Somaiya,Christian Herff,Yorguin Mantilla Ramos,Hamza Abdelhedi,Karim Jerbi,Greg Farquhar,Brendan Shillingford,Mark Woolrich,Oiwi Parker Jones*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents the 2026 PNPL competition's approach to advancing non-invasive speech BCI through LibriBrain100 dataset with two tracks: Deep track for within-subject word classification and Broad track for cross-subject generalization with limited fine-tuning data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While previous competition (2025) achieved strong within-subject speech decoding performance with extensive data (~50 hours per subject), practical brain-computer interfaces (BCIs) must generalize to new users from minutes of data, not hours, to be clinically feasible for restoring communication to people with paralysis.

Method: The 2026 competition introduces LibriBrain100 dataset with 32 additional subjects (~40 minutes each) plus more within-subject data (~80 hours). It advances to word classification tasks with two complementary tracks: 1) Deep track for optimal within-subject performance, and 2) Broad track for cross-subject generalization, progressively reducing fine-tuning data from ~40 to ~20 to ~10 minutes.

Result: The competition builds on previous success where winning submissions achieved F1-macro scores of 95.6% on speech detection and 73.6% on phoneme classification using the LibriBrain dataset. The new competition aims to address the cross-subject generalization challenge that was identified as a key limitation for practical BCI deployment.

Conclusion: The 2026 PNPL competition represents a strategic advancement toward clinically viable non-invasive speech BCIs by shifting focus from within-subject optimization to cross-subject generalization with minimal fine-tuning data, bringing the field closer to practical applications for communication restoration in paralysis patients.

Abstract: The ambition of the 2025 PNPL competition (Landau et al., 2025) was to launch a multi-year curriculum for non-invasive speech decoding. Designed to progress from foundational tasks toward the linguistic complexity required for a practical brain-computer interface (BCI), it set the stage with speech detection and phoneme classification tasks. Winning submissions reached F1-macro scores of 95.6% and 73.6% on the respective tasks (Elvers et al., 2026), highly significant advances. This success was built on the LibriBrain dataset (Özdogan et al., 2025), the largest within-subject MEG dataset recorded at the time with ${\sim}50$ hours of data for one subject. However, while within-subject scale drives strong decoding performance, a practical BCI must generalise to new users from minutes of data, not hours.
  The 2026 PNPL competition responds to this challenge with LibriBrain100 (Mantegna et al., 2026), an extended LibriBrain dataset with 32 additional subjects (${\sim}40$ minutes each) plus even more within-subject data (${\sim}80$ hours). Advancing the curriculum of tasks to focus on word classification, two complementary tracks are presented in this competition: the Deep track targets within-subject word classification at scale, aiming at the best possible performance; the Broad track targets cross-subject generalisation, progressively reducing the amount of subject-specific fine-tuning data from ${\sim}40$ to ${\sim}20$ to ${\sim}10$ minutes, a duration that falls within a clinically feasible range and brings us a step closer to a non-invasive BCI capable of restoring communication to people living with profound paralysis.

</details>


### [96] [B2B Customer Conversion Prediction: A Document Representation, Graph Theory, and CatBoost Driven Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03239)
*Tianqi Wang,Sheikh Shams Azam,Wan Eih Huang,Anton Wiranata,Christopher G. Brinton,Jan P. Allebach*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for B2B customer conversion prediction that aggregates contact data, cleans company names, generates features, and applies CatBoost to predict purchase interest with 91% accuracy, enabling personalized campaign recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In B2B contexts with long sales cycles (months to years), identifying high-potential customers and recommending personalized campaigns is crucial for effective marketing. The paper addresses challenges in B2B customer data aggregation, feature generation, and predicting whether customers will show purchase interest.

Method: 1) Proposes an algorithm to aggregate individual contacts to B2B customer level using multiple keys; 2) Develops novel architecture to cluster non-standardized company names, handling irregularities like spelling mistakes and variants; 3) Defines and generates a feature set; 4) Applies CatBoost model for customer conversion prediction.

Result: The framework achieves 91% prediction accuracy for B2B customer conversion prediction. Based on prediction results and model analysis, the paper discusses personalized campaign recommendations to foster conversion.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively addresses B2B marketing challenges by combining data aggregation, name normalization, feature engineering, and machine learning to predict customer conversion with high accuracy, enabling targeted personalized campaigns in long B2B sales cycles.

Abstract: In the one-time selling B2B context, the buying cycle may last months or even years. During the long process, targeting customers that have a high potential to make purchases and recommending personalized campaigns accordingly are important for effective marketing. For this goal, we study the following problems, B2B customer data aggregation, customer feature generation, and prediction of whether a B2B customer would show interest in making a purchase (i.e., prediction of conversion into sales funnel). We propose an algorithm to aggregate individual contacts to the B2B customer level based on multiple keys. For non-standardized keys such as company names, we propose a novel architecture to cluster them in a domain encompassing irregularities such as spelling mistakes and spelling variants. We then define and generate a set of features and apply the CatBoost model for customer conversion prediction. Our framework achieves 91\% prediction accuracy. Based on the prediction results and analysis of the model, we then discuss personalized campaign recommendations to foster conversion.

</details>


### [97] [FlowBalance: Verifier-Grounded Self-Improvement from On-Policy Reasoning Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03241)
*Zixun Huang,Kishan Panaganti,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowBalance is a new self-improvement method for reasoning models that combines dense same-model guidance with terminal verifier supervision via trajectory balance, achieving better performance, training stability, and solution diversity than previous methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reasoning models struggle with fragile inner-loop self-improvement: terminal verifiers provide reliable but sparse supervision, while dense same-model guidance can reinforce false confidence or focus too narrowly on limited solution modes, creating a need for better self-improvement methods.

Method: FlowBalance learns a normalized distribution over complete responses by combining on-policy trajectories with frozen policy guidance. It produces token-level log-probability gains aggregated into trajectory-level scores, then calibrates these with verifier-derived group advantages (retained on positive trajectories, reversed on negative ones, disabled when no preference). This creates an energy that exponentially reweights a reference policy, fitted via trajectory balance with one log-partition estimate per rollout group.

Result: On mathematical reasoning tasks, FlowBalance improves average performance over FlowRL on both Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B models. It also shows better training speed and stability, avoids the response-length collapse of direct OPSD, and exhibits higher correct-strategy diversity in AIME24 diagnostics.

Conclusion: FlowBalance successfully addresses the limitations of existing self-improvement methods by achieving outcome-calibrated self-guidance through trajectory balance without separate token-level imitation loss, with theoretical guarantees including contrast preservation, minimum-change reverse-KL characterization, verifier control of target reward, and correction against false-positive self-guidance.

Abstract: A reasoning model can improve from its own on-policy experience, but this inner loop is fragile: terminal verifiers provide reliable yet sparse supervision, while dense same-model guidance can reinforce false confidence or overconcentrate learning on a narrow solution mode. We introduce FlowBalance, a verifier-grounded self-improvement method that learns a normalized distribution over complete responses. For each on-policy trajectory, a frozen training-time view of the same policy uses privileged context to produce token-level log-probability gains, which are aggregated into a trajectory-level self-guidance score. FlowBalance calibrates this score with the verifier-derived group advantage: guidance is retained on positive-advantage trajectories, reversed on negative-advantage trajectories, and disabled when the rollout group provides no outcome preference. The resulting energy exponentially reweights a reference policy, and profiled trajectory balance fits the normalized target with one log-partition estimate per rollout group. This realizes outcome-calibrated self-guidance via trajectory balance, without a separate token-level imitation loss. Our analysis establishes within-group contrast preservation, a minimum-change reverse-KL characterization, monotonic verifier control of target reward, and an exact correction against false-positive self-guidance on rejected responses. On mathematical reasoning, FlowBalance improves average performance over FlowRL on both Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, while also improving training speed and stability, avoiding direct OPSD's response-length collapse, and exhibiting higher correct-strategy diversity in a controlled AIME24 diagnostic.

</details>


### [98] [Selective Hypergraph Refinement for Frozen Graph Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03265)
*Zimo Si*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SHR is a post-processing method that improves frozen graph clustering by selectively updating assignments using hypergraph refinement without changing model parameters or representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph clustering methods lack effective post-processing techniques for already trained and frozen models. There's a need to improve clustering results without modifying the original model, parameters, or graph structure after training completion.

Method: The proposed Selective Hypergraph Refinement (SHR) uses an attribute hypergraph to capture higher-order relations that ordinary graphs cannot express. It generates candidate residual directions from the hypergraph and evaluates their reliability using graph structure, node attributes, and matched-null evidence. The method selectively updates only nodes with sufficient support while retaining original assignments for uncertain cases.

Result: In evaluations: 13/15 backbone-dataset cells showed positive mean macro gain (0.066 pp, 95% CI [0.030, 0.107] pp), with only 0.209% of hard assignments changing on average. A broader 15-combination evaluation yielded 0.137 pp macro gain at 0.375% mean change ratio. Performance gains are heterogeneous across backbone-dataset pairs.

Conclusion: Frozen clustering outputs retain limited but measurable refinement space after training. Selective hypergraph-based refinement can improve results without model modification, but effects are dataset/backbone dependent and broader coverage increases exposure to negative transfer risks.

Abstract: Existing graph-clustering methods typically improve clustering performance by optimizing model parameters and node representations. Effective means of further improving the clustering results of an already trained and frozen model, however, remain limited. We study post-processing for frozen graph clustering. After checkpoint fixation, the procedure uses no labels and updates neither model parameters, node representations, nor the original graph structure. Instead, it exploits an attribute hypergraph to supplement higher-order relations that ordinary graphs cannot readily express, thereby refining existing cluster assignments. Because global hypergraph refinement can yield both performance gains and erroneous updates, we propose Selective Hypergraph Refinement (SHR). The method generates candidate residual directions from the hypergraph and evaluates their reliability using graph structure, node attributes, and matched-null evidence. It updates only nodes with sufficient support and otherwise retains their original assignments. Further analysis shows that whether a node changes cluster is jointly governed by its native assignment gap and the directional strength of the refinement. In a controlled common-suite evaluation, 13 of 15 backbone-dataset cells had a positive mean macro gain, one produced exact no-action, and one was negative. The cell-equal macro gain was 0.066 pp (95% bootstrap CI, [0.030, 0.107] pp), while only 0.209% of hard assignments changed on average. A broader 15-combination native-interface evaluation yielded a macro gain of 0.137 pp at a mean change ratio of 0.375%. These results indicate that frozen clustering outputs retain a limited but measurable refinement space after training. The effect is heterogeneous across backbone-dataset pairs, and broader coverage also increases exposure to negative transfer.

</details>


### [99] [Latent Energy Action Planning with World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03294)
*Phu Pham,Aniket Bera*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEAP improves model predictive control by treating the action horizon as differentiable variable and optimizing it through a frozen world model, combining terminal latent goal matching with state energy minimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing latent world models can optimize single learned latent objectives that sometimes produce action sequences whose predicted terminal state doesn't match the actual goal descriptor, limiting control performance.

Method: LEAP treats the complete action horizon as differentiable variable and optimizes it through frozen LeWorldModel. It combines terminal latent goal matching with terminal-window state energy, uses frozen goal-conditioned proposal for initialization, quasi-Newton solver for refinement, and post-optimization projection for action constraints.

Result: LEAP raises mean success rate from 77.5% for LeWM+CEM to 94.8% across four control domains using official LeWM checkpoints, a 17.3 percentage point improvement while maintaining frozen model representation.

Conclusion: LEAP provides an effective planning system that significantly improves control performance in latent world models by better aligning terminal predictions with goal specifications through differentiable action horizon optimization.

Abstract: Latent world models support efficient model predictive control from high-dimensional observations, yet optimizing a single learned latent objective can favor action sequences whose decoder-predicted terminal descriptor does not match the goal descriptor. We introduce Latent Energy Action Planning (LEAP), which treats the complete action horizon as a differentiable variable and optimizes it through a frozen LeWorldModel (LeWM). LEAP couples terminal latent goal matching with a terminal-window state energy. Low energy requires the predicted terminal latent to agree with the goal latent and the decoder-predicted terminal descriptor to agree with the goal descriptor. A frozen goal-conditioned proposal initializes the search, a quasi-Newton solver refines actions through the autoregressive rollout, and post-optimization projection enforces the admissible action range. Across four control domains using the officially released LeWM checkpoints, the complete LEAP planning system raises mean success from 77.5% for LeWM planned with the cross-entropy method (LeWM+CEM) to 94.8% under a matched protocol, a 17.3-percentage-point improvement, while retaining the frozen LeWM representation and predictor.

</details>


### [100] [Geometry-Aware Graph Construction via Adaptive Spectral Bandwidth Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03306)
*Ecem Bozkurt,Antonio Ortega*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a method to automatically select optimal Gaussian bandwidth for kernelized graph methods by matching kernel spectral complexity to intrinsic manifold complexity, improving semi-supervised learning accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kernelized graph methods using Gaussian kernels critically depend on the bandwidth parameter sigma. Choosing sigma is challenging: too small leads to overestimating local complexity, too large causes geometric discrimination loss. Existing methods lack principled ways to set sigma consistently with intrinsic manifold structure.

Method: Proposes a per-node bandwidth criterion that matches the kernel's effective rank to the local intrinsic dimension estimated via minimum spanning tree. This approach anchors the search in the manifold-consistent log-log scaling regime to make kernel spectral complexity consistent with underlying manifold complexity.

Result: Evaluated semi-supervised learning embeddings from six encoders on CIFAR-100. Adaptive bandwidth consistently improves leave-one-out classification and label propagation accuracy over fixed-bandwidth methods and competing adaptive methods.

Conclusion: Matching kernel spectral complexity to intrinsic manifold complexity through adaptive bandwidth selection provides superior performance for kernelized graph methods, offering a principled solution to the bandwidth selection problem.

Abstract: Kernelized graph methods - spectral clustering, diffusion maps, and sparse kernel -regression graphs - that use Gaussian kernels depend on the choice of Gaussian bandwidth sigma, which governs the spectral character of the local kernel operator. When sigma is too small, the kernel overestimates local complexity and treats each sample as an independent direction; when sigma is too large, the kernel collapses multiple directions together, the condition number diverges, and all geometric discrimination is lost. We propose a choice of scale to make the spectral complexity of the kernel consistent with the intrinsic complexity of the underlying manifold. We propose a per-node bandwidth criterion that operationalizes this principle by jointly matching the kernel's effective rank to the local intrinsic dimension estimated via minimum spanning tree, anchoring the search in the manifold-consistent log-log scaling regime. We evaluate SSL embeddings from six encoders on CIFAR-100, showing that adaptive bandwidth consistently improves leave-one-out (LOO) classification and label propagation (LP) accuracy over fixed-bandwidth methods and competing adaptive methods.

</details>


### [101] [DE-Venus: A Data-Efficient RLVR Framework for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03324)
*Shenzhi Yang,Guangcheng Zhu,Kai Tang,Zhengqing Zang,Xing Zheng,Haobo Wang,Yingfan Ma,Bowen Song,Bo Han,Bo An,Lei Feng,Weiqiang Wang,Junbo Zhao,Gang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG
