TL;DR: DE-Venus is a unified framework for data-efficient reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards that reduces annotation and training costs by organizing supervision as evolving state across data preparation and policy optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves LLM reasoning but faces practical scaling constraints due to expensive on-policy rollouts and high costs of obtaining reliable targets at scale. Existing methods address sample selection, incomplete supervision, or noisy labels separately, often entangling supervision logic with distributed training, hindering controlled comparison and reuse.

Method: DE-Venus treats supervision as evolving state across data preparation and policy optimization, organizing the lifecycle into three modules: 1) Active Data Selection allocates training/annotation budgets, 2) Weak Supervision Construction derives learning signals from unlabeled examples, and 3) Training-Time Supervision Refinement filters/corrects unreliable supervision. The framework supports seven representative methods and a data-selection pipeline by expressing method-specific decisions as offline dataset transitions or online transformations of targets, rewards, batches, and advantages while preserving RL's distributed execution contracts.

Result: Across public benchmarks and three business scenarios, separate configurations preserve or improve model quality with only 10% of labels or as little as 13% of relevant data. Selected business configurations also reduce observed convergence steps by 63%-75%, demonstrating significant efficiency improvements.

Conclusion: DE-Venus reduces annotation and training costs without sacrificing scalable RL execution, providing a unified framework for data-efficient RLVR that addresses multiple supervision challenges while maintaining performance.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) improves large language model reasoning, but its practical scaling is constrained by expensive on-policy rollouts and the cost of obtaining reliable targets at scale. Existing methods address sample selection, incomplete supervision, or noisy labels separately, often entangling supervision logic with distributed training and hindering controlled comparison and reuse. We present DE-Venus, a unified framework for data-efficient RLVR that treats supervision as evolving state across data preparation and policy optimization. It organizes this lifecycle into three modules: Active Data Selection allocates training and annotation budgets; Weak Supervision Construction derives learning signals from unlabeled examples; and Training-Time Supervision Refinement filters or corrects unreliable supervision. DE-Venus supports seven representative methods and a data-selection pipeline by expressing method-specific decisions as offline dataset transitions or online transformations of targets, rewards, batches, and advantages while preserving verl's distributed execution contracts. Across public benchmarks and three business scenarios, separate configurations preserve or improve model quality with only 10% of labels or as little as 13% of relevant data; selected business configurations also reduce observed convergence steps by 63%--75%. DE-Venus thus reduces annotation and training costs without sacrificing scalable RL execution.

</details>


### [102] [A Large Open Multi-Energy Corpus of Soil Compaction Tests, with Machine-Learning Baselines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03337)
*Sompote Youwai,Chana Phutthananon,Warat Kongkitkul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive study analyzing 2,854 Proctor compaction tests from multiple sources reveals that significant portions of published compaction data are physically impossible, establishes baseline saturation characteristics, and develops predictive models for density and moisture content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Proctor tests are time-consuming and expensive. Existing correlations are limited by small datasets, single-laboratory bias, restricted compactive energy levels, and lack of public availability, making reliable prediction of compaction properties challenging.

Method: Created a large corpus of 2,854 laboratory compaction tests from 6 public sources, 162 provenance groups, and 4 Proctor energy levels. Data was audited against Proctor methods and screened using zero-air-voids condition. Developed tabular foundation models and symbolic regression approaches to predict density and water content.

Result: Found that 11.8% of harmonized records and 5.7% with measured specific gravity violate physical impossibility. Established optimum degree of saturation baseline at 0.815 with 11% CV. Models achieved R² up to 0.824 for density and 0.784 for water content. Compactive energy was conditionally decisive, with modified-Proctor predictions dropping from R² 0.740 to -0.651 when energy was ignored.

Conclusion: Significant portions of published compaction data are physically invalid. Developed predictive models with satisfactory accuracy for screening purposes, though not for acceptance testing. The optimum saturation baseline and energy-sensitive models provide valuable tools for preliminary assessment in geotechnical engineering.

Abstract: Every engineered fill is specified by a maximum dry density and an optimum moisture content. Each determination needs a full Proctor test. Published correlations rest on one to four hundred specimens, usually from one laboratory at one compactive energy, and are seldom released. This paper releases a corpus without those limits. It holds 2,854 laboratory compaction tests from six public sources, across 162 provenance groups and four Proctor energy levels, with fines from 1.5 to 100%. Every record is audited to the Proctor method its source names, and no energy is inferred. Screening on the zero-air-voids condition removed 11.8% of harmonised records, and 5.7% of those with a measured specific gravity. A material share of published compaction data is physically impossible. The optimum degree of saturation over the corpus is 0.815 at a coefficient of variation of 11%. That is a baseline, not a constant. Both parameters are then estimated from one classification suite and the compaction standard. A tabular foundation model reaches R2 0.824 for density and 0.784 for water content under random folds. It reaches 0.727 and 0.696 with folds drawn around provenance, and 0.520 and 0.614 with a whole source held out. Compactive energy is negligible marginally yet decisive conditionally. Density on the 66 modified-Proctor records is predicted at R2 0.740 with it and -0.651 without. Symbolic regression yields closed forms coupled through a phase relation. No predicted pair can then exceed the zero-air-voids line. The predictions are for screening, not acceptance.

</details>


### [103] [Gradients Know What Outcomes Don't: Unlocking Reinforcement Learning for LLM Reasoning with Gradient-Aligned Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03342)
*Leqi Zheng,Jinbo Su,Fang Niu,Chaokun Wang,Weiping Wang,Jiajun Zhang,Shannan Yan,Jie Wu,Zhaolu Kang,Rong Fu,Hang Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GAR (Gradient-Aligned Reward) is a new RL reward function that uses gradient alignment instead of binary outcomes, providing dense reasoning-aware rewards with minimal computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current reinforcement learning approaches for chain-of-thought reasoning either use binary rewards that can't distinguish among correct trajectories, or use dense rewards that either ignore expert solutions in training data or require expensive offline annotation.

Method: GAR operates in the policy's gradient space: it uses truncated backpropagation through the output projection layer to extract gradient vectors for each rollout, then computes cosine similarity with an expert-anchor gradient to produce dense rewards with less than 9% wall-clock overhead.

Result: The method consistently improves over GRPO and other baselines on competition-level math benchmarks, and transfers effectively to GPQA Diamond and MMLU-Pro without domain-specific data.

Conclusion: GAR provides an efficient, reasoning-aware reward mechanism that leverages existing expert solutions in training data, avoiding expensive annotation while offering interpretable gradient alignment signals.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) drives chain-of-thought reasoning in large language models, yet its binary outcome reward cannot distinguish among correct trajectories. Existing dense reward alternatives, from surface heuristics to process reward models, either ignore the expert solutions already present in training corpora or require expensive offline annotation. We propose Gradient-Aligned Reward (GAR), which operates in the policy's own gradient space: truncated backpropagation through the output projection layer extracts a compact gradient vector for each rollout, and cosine similarity with an expert-anchor gradient yields a dense, reasoning-aware reward with less than 9% wall-clock overhead. We prove that this cosine admits a multiplicative decomposition into prediction-error and activation-pattern factors, providing a concrete characterization of what the alignment signal measures. On Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, GAR consistently improves over GRPO and other baselines on competition-level math benchmarks and transfers to GPQA Diamond and MMLU-Pro without domain-specific data. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LQgdwind/GAR.

</details>


### [104] [From Zero to Hero: An Open LLM Ecosystem for Armenian](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03350)
*Erik Arakelyan,Khatun Avetisyan,Meri Davtyan,Heghine Grigoryan,Nane Khachatryan,Hayk Shahsuvaryan,Henrik Sergoyan,Vahan Martirosyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the scarcity of pretraining data for Armenian by curating and releasing two datasets (ArmWeb and ArmSTEM), then creates an improved Armenian LLM (arm-gemma-e4b) through continued pretraining that outperforms existing models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Armenian is a morphologically rich, low-resource language with scarce pretraining data, and there are no open Armenian LLMs with reproducible training data and recipes.

Method: 1) Curated two datasets: ArmWeb (4.37M validated Armenian news documents) and ArmSTEM (373K parallel English-Armenian math/science problems with step-by-step solutions, verified by LLM judgment and human evaluation. 2) Performed continued pretraining of Gemma-4-E4B on these datasets. 3) Conducted ablation studies to analyze effects of different data types.

Result: arm-gemma-e4b outperforms all existing open Armenian models and its unadapted base. News-only pretraining improves fluency but erodes knowledge, while verified STEM data reverses this loss. Also found significant train/test overlap in existing public Armenian corpora.

Conclusion: The work successfully addresses the Armenian LLM gap by providing reproducible datasets and models. The combination of news and verified STEM data is crucial for balanced performance in low-resource languages, and transparency about data contamination is important for reliable evaluation.

Abstract: Pretraining data for Armenian, a morphologically rich and low-resource language, is scarce, and no open Armenian LLM has been released with the data and recipe needed to reproduce it. To address this gap, we curate and release two datasets. ArmWeb is an extensively validated corpus of 4.37M Armenian news documents. ArmSTEM is a parallel English-Armenian collection of 373K math and science problems with step-by-step solutions, translated into Armenian and verified through both answer-preserving LLM judgment and human evaluation. Continued pretraining of Gemma-4-E4B on these datasets yields arm-gemma-e4b, which outperforms every existing open Armenian model as well as its unadapted base, and is the first open Armenian LLM with complete training data and recipe. Our ablations show that news-only continued pretraining improves fluency while eroding knowledge, a pattern we also observe in existing Armenian models, and that a small share of verified translated STEM data reverses the loss. We further find that the largest public Armenian corpora overlap web-derived evaluation panels heavily, including a train/test self-overlap inside FineWeb-2. We openly release all data, models, and code.

</details>


### [105] [Time Without Timesteps: Simulating Coupled Dynamical Systems via Self-Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03358)
*Liyu Zerihun,Mark Shinyoung Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training neural surrogates that map full driving trajectories to full output trajectories, transforming dynamical system simulation from stepwise time integration to fixed-point problems over complete trajectories, enabling faster convergence with fewer iterations and memory-efficient gradient computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional numerical simulation of dynamical systems uses causal time-stepping (each state computed from previous one), which can be computationally expensive for coupled systems. The authors want to explore an alternative approach that avoids sequential time recursion.

Method: Train neural surrogate models for each subsystem that map full driving trajectories and initial conditions directly to full output trajectories. For coupled systems, enforce self-consistency among trajectories using waveform relaxation principles, transforming simulation into a fixed-point problem over complete trajectories. Use Newton iterations (4-10) instead of stepwise integration (1500 steps). Compute gradients as linear systems solved by GMRES with memory independent of solver depth.

Result: Achieves faster convergence: 4-10 Newton iterations vs. 1500 steps for reference integrator. Gradient computation loses time recursion, enabling memory-efficient GMRES solving. Spectral radius of learned operator's Jacobian predicts convergence boundaries. The implicit gradient remains accurate (0.04%) even when unrolled backpropagation diverges. Demonstrated on coupled van der Pol oscillators and Hodgkin-Huxley neuron networks.

Conclusion: The waveform-based neural surrogate approach successfully transforms coupled system simulation into fixed-point problems, achieving faster convergence with fewer iterations and memory-efficient gradient computation. The spectral radius of the learned operator's Jacobian provides predictive power for convergence. While successful in many cases, surrogate error can degrade performance, indicating areas for future improvement.

Abstract: Numerical simulation of dynamical systems is usually organized as a causal march through time: each state is computed from the previous one. We explore a different formulation for coupled systems. For each subsystem type we train a neural surrogate mapping a full driving trajectory and initial condition directly to a full output trajectory; following classical waveform relaxation, coupled systems are assembled by enforcing self-consistency among these trajectories: simulation becomes a fixed-point problem over complete trajectories rather than a stepwise rollout. On coupled van der Pol oscillators and Hodgkin-Huxley neuron networks, sequential depth becomes the number of solver iterations: 4-10 Newton iterations where the reference integrator takes 1500 steps. The gradient likewise loses its time recursion: it becomes a linear system solved by GMRES at memory independent of solver depth. A single scalar measured from the learned operator, the spectral radius of its Jacobian, predicts in advance where the coupled solve will converge; past that boundary, unrolled backpropagation diverges and a Neumann adjoint fails, while the implicit gradient remains correct to 0.04%. We report where the approach succeeds and where surrogate error degrades it.

</details>


### [106] [SimpleDesign: A Joint Model for Protein Sequence and Structure Codesign](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03377)
*Jiarui Lu,Yuyang Wang,Yizhe Zhang,Jiatao Gu,Navdeep Jaitly,Joshua M. Susskind,Miguel Ángel Bautista*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SimpleDesign: A single-stage end-to-end multi-modal protein design model that directly operates in data space without multi-stage latent training, using a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Proteins function through complex interplay between amino acid sequences and 3D structures. Existing generative models for protein co-design use multi-stage training with autoencoder latent spaces, but the authors hypothesize this isn't necessary for performant models.

Method: SimpleDesign uses single-stage end-to-end training with combined discrete cross-entropy for sequences and regression for structures. Features a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture for modality-specific processing while maintaining global self-attention across both modalities.

Result: Trained on over 2M sequence-structure pairs, achieves strong performance across co-design and unconditional sequence/structure generation benchmarks.

Conclusion: SimpleDesign demonstrates that effective multi-modal protein design models can be trained directly in data space without complex multi-stage latent training processes, offering a simpler yet performant alternative.

Abstract: Proteins are fundamental to biological processes, with their function determined by the complex interplay between the amino acid sequence and the three-dimensional structure. Developing generative models capable of understanding this intrinsically multi-modal relationship is crucial for fields like drug discovery and protein engineering. Existing models often rely on a multi-stage training process where autoencoders that tokenize data into latent representations are trained in a first stage. Secondly, a generative model is trained on the latent representation of the autoencoder(s), i.e., generative modeling in a latent space. We hypothesize that this multi-stage training is not necessary to obtain performant co-design models and thus present SimpleDesign, an effective multi-modal protein design model trained directly in the data space. SimpleDesign leverages a single-stage end-to-end objective that combines discrete cross-entropy for sequences and a regression objective for structures. In order to effectively model the difference in sequence and structure modalities, we develop a Mixture-of-Transformer architecture that allows modality-specific processing while keeping global self-attention over both modalities. We train SimpleDesign on over 2M sequence-structure pairs achieving strong performance across co-design and unconditional sequence/structure generation benchmarks.

</details>


### [107] [RecurTrace: Adaptive Latent Reasoning with Loop-Time Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03379)
*Yuxiang Wang,Kunyu Feng,Yingda Shen,Haoning Xu,Junyu Wang,Zhizheng Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RecurTrace improves latent recurrence in language models by adding loop memory attention (to access earlier computations) and adaptive halting (to allocate more depth to harder inputs), achieving better reasoning with fewer loops than fixed-depth or existing adaptive methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current latent recurrence approaches have two key limitations: 1) Each iteration only sees the previous output, losing access to earlier computations, and 2) Fixed loop counts waste computation on easy inputs while giving insufficient depth to hard inputs.

Method: RecurTrace introduces two components: 1) Loop Memory Attention that allows looped layers to attend to their own previous states along the loop-time axis, and 2) A halting head that predicts when to continue looping, supervised by an oracle that identifies when additional depth still reduces loss.

Result: On MathQA, RecurTrace achieves 56.9% accuracy with average 2.0 loops, beating fixed loop depth by 2.2 points at matched compute. It outperforms ACT, PonderNet, CALM, LoopUS-Conf, and TaH-Mismatch baselines. Gains scale with model size from 0.6 to 3.4 points improvement at 0.6B to 8B parameter models.

Conclusion: RecurTrace effectively addresses limitations of latent recurrence by enabling access to earlier loop states and adaptive computation allocation, resulting in more efficient and effective reasoning that scales well with model size.

Abstract: Repeating a small block of middle layers increases a language model's effective inference depth without adding parameters or generating extra tokens, and recent work shows that this latent recurrence improves reasoning. However, two design choices limit these gains. Each iteration sees only the previous output and cannot directly access earlier computations. Moreover, a fixed loop count wastes depth on easy inputs while leaving hard ones with too little computation. We introduce RecurTrace, which addresses both limitations using the loop's own trajectory. Specifically, Loop Memory Attention lets each looped layer attend to its own states from previous iterations along the loop-time axis, so the model can revisit earlier computations instead of relying on the latest state alone. A halting head then reads the loop state and predicts whether to continue, with supervision from an oracle that identifies when additional depth still reduces loss. In a controlled MathQA comparison on the same looped backbone, RecurTrace achieves 56.9% accuracy with an average of 2.0 loops, exceeding the best fixed loop depth by 2.2 points at matched compute. By comparison, ACT and PonderNet collapse to one loop, and CALM reaches only 54.1% with 5.6 loops, while the stronger LoopUS-Conf and TaH-Mismatch baselines reach 55.3% at 3.2 loops and 55.7% at 2.1 loops. Finally, RecurTrace improves generation accuracy over same-budget fine-tuned baselines at 0.6B, 1.7B, 4B, and 8B, with the gain growing with model size from 0.6 to 3.4 points.

</details>


### [108] [TIGPO: Temporal Instance-Graph Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03383)
*Jinwei Gan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TIGPO extends graph-based policy optimization by maintaining persistent transition graphs across policy updates, allowing historical transitions to inform current credit assignment while preventing policy loss replay, improving long-horizon LLM agent performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based policy optimization methods construct graphs independently within each policy update, discarding previously discovered transitions and limiting advantage estimation to small, batch-local rollout groups, which hampers effective credit assignment for long-horizon tasks.

Method: TIGPO maintains persistent transition graphs for each task across policy updates, uses Exploration slots for new task sampling and Revisit slots for reattempting previously explored tasks, constructs cross-temporal references by pairing current rollout groups with earlier Exploration groups, and uses historical transitions only as structural references without replay in policy loss.

Result: Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks show that TIGPO consistently outperforms prior group-based and graph-based policy optimization methods.

Conclusion: TIGPO successfully extends graph-based credit assignment across policy updates, enabling more stable advantage estimation and better policy improvement tracking by leveraging historical experience while avoiding policy loss replay, demonstrating superior performance for long-horizon LLM agents.

Abstract: Graph-based policy optimization improves credit assignment for long-horizon LLM agents by organizing rollout trajectories into state-transition graphs. However, existing methods construct graphs independently within each policy update, discarding transitions discovered by earlier policies and limiting advantage estimation to small, batch-local rollout groups. We propose \emph{Temporal Instance-Graph Policy Optimization} (TIGPO), which extends graph-based credit assignment across policy updates. TIGPO maintains a persistent transition graph for each task, allowing valid transitions discovered by different policy versions to jointly determine credit for current rollouts. To actively reconnect current exploration with historical experience, TIGPO allocates a fixed rollout budget between Exploration slots for ordinary task sampling and Revisit slots for delayed reattempts of previously explored tasks. For each revisit, TIGPO pairs the current rollout group with its corresponding earlier Exploration group to construct a cross-temporal reference. The enlarged reference is designed to stabilize relative advantage estimation under small rollout groups, while comparison on the same task directly captures policy improvement across training stages. Historical transitions and scores serve only as structural and detached statistical references and are never replayed in the policy loss. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop demonstrate that TIGPO consistently outperforms prior group-based and graph-based policy optimization methods.

</details>


### [109] [TraveL: Transformer-based Multi-view Path Distributional Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03427)
*Fang He,Tao-yang Fu,Wang-chien Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TraveL: A Transformer-based framework that learns distributional path representations capturing traveler behavior variability and regional correlations, outperforming SOTA methods on travel time estimation, path similarity, and destination prediction tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing path representation learning methods use co-occurrence relationships among road segments/paths but fail to capture varied traveler behaviors and regional correlations, limiting their usefulness for path-related applications.

Method: Proposes TraveL framework using Transformer architecture with: 1) distributional representations encoding paths and travel times, 2) regional attention to capture road segment dependencies, 3) Kolmogorov-Smirnov test for training by comparing sampled behavior with ground truth.

Result: Outperforms state-of-the-art methods on synthetic and real-world datasets: 14.7% improvement in Mean K-S distance for travel time distribution estimation, 16.7% in MAE for path similarity prediction, and 3.97% in MAE for destination prediction.

Conclusion: TraveL effectively learns distributional path representations that capture traveler behavior variability and regional correlations, demonstrating superior performance across multiple path-related tasks compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Path representation learning (PRL) for road networks has received increasing research attention, due to various path-related applications. Existing works on PRL typically exploit the co-occurrence relationship among road segments and paths to learn a vector as the path representation, without exploring the varied traveler behaviors and the regional correlation on the path. In this work, we propose to learn distributional representations, which provide valuable information for use in path-related applications, by capturing the varied traveler behaviors as well as the various dependencies within regions of road segments. We propose a novel Transformer-based Multi-view Distributional Representation Learning (TraveL) framework to encode a path along with a travel starting time to a distributional representation, which can be used to decode possible samples of on-path traveler behavior. Moreover, by analyzing the regional correlation which reveals various road segment relationships, we propose a regional attention to encode these correlations in a path. Also, we explore the idea of Kolmogorov-Smirnov (K-S) test to compare the sampled traveler behavior against the collected ground truth to facilitate training. Experimental results show that the proposed TraveL model outperforms the state-of-the-art methods on both synthetic and real-world datasets, by 14.7% in Mean K-S distance for travel time distribution estimation, 16.7% in Mean Absolute Error (MAE) for path similarity prediction, and 3.97% in MAE for destination prediction.

</details>


### [110] [It's the Problem, Not the Path: Budget and Difficulty Confounds in LLM Reasoning Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03436)
*Yigit Utku Bulut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces two controlled methods to challenge existing interpretations of LLM reasoning traces, finding that: 1) model prefixes rarely provide unique value beyond what fresh computation could achieve, and 2) early-window internal signals contain little outcome information beyond baseline difficulty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interpretations of LLM reasoning traces are problematic because they lack proper counterfactual controls. Researchers often read reasoning traces as containing "breakthrough" moments or early-legible fates, but these readings rely on measurements missing essential controls at the claim level.

Method: 1) Restart-controlled truncation probe: Compares per-anchor continuation solve rates against from-scratch restart curves at matched token budgets to separate when solutions fit continuation budgets from when prefixes provide unique value. Applied to 178 problem-model cells.
2) Pre-registered difficulty-controlled test: Evaluates early-window internal signals against problem-difficulty baseline.
3) Two generation-free corpus analyses: Uses trace-blind difficulty proxy and reconstruction of published early-window positive for validation.

Result: 1) Only 1 of 178 cells shows prefix-limited capability; restart analysis separates compute-starved from capability-limited models.
2) Continuing model's own prefix beats restarting 9 of 9 times where budget lies inside restart grid, primarily due to compute compression.
3) No detectable outcome information in early-window signals beyond difficulty baseline.
4) Trace-blind difficulty proxy achieves AUROC 0.873, within published probe range.
5) Reconstructed early-window probe shows pooled AUROC 0.849, but within-problem performance is statistically indistinguishable from chance (0.496 at t=4).

Conclusion: High pooled AUROCs alone cannot establish within-attempt information. Proper controls like question-only baselines or within-problem evaluation are essential. Most perceived "breakthrough" moments and early-legible fates in reasoning traces are artifacts of measurement without counterfactual controls.

Abstract: Reasoning traces of large language models are widely read as containing "breakthrough" moments and early-legible fates. Both readings rest on measurements missing a counterfactual control at the level of the claim; we supply both controls. First, a restart-controlled truncation probe separates when a solution fits the continuation budget from when a prefix carries value that fresh computation cannot buy, comparing per-anchor continuation solve rates against from-scratch restart curves at matched total generated-token budget. Applied to 178 problem-model cells (89 MATH problems x two small open models, an outcome-blind but difficulty-targeted cohort), exactly 1 of 178 cells survives as prefix-limited; restart dose-response separates a compute-starved model from a capability-limited one; and wherever the matched budget lies inside the restart grid, continuing the model's own prefix beats restarting (9 of 9) -- predominantly compute compression rather than expanded reachability. Second, a pre-registered, difficulty-controlled test finds no detectable outcome information in early-window internal signals beyond a problem-difficulty baseline, and two generation-free analyses of public corpora show why this control is needed: a trace-blind difficulty proxy reaches AUROC 0.873 on 192K DeepSeek-R1 generations -- inside the published probe range -- and a closely matched reconstruction of the closest published early-window positive recovers a comparable pooled result (0.849) while within problem it is statistically indistinguishable from chance at all ten anchors (0.496 at t=4); a post-hoc within-targeted probe finds only a small average residual, concentrated in three low-failure problems. High pooled probe AUROCs cannot by themselves establish within-attempt information; a question-only baseline or within-problem evaluation is required.

</details>


### [111] [Guide, Not Bind: Why Defeasible Priors Fail in Augmented Lagrangian Causal Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03442)
*Sairam Sundararaman,Sara Girdhar,Manit Narasimha Murthy,Samrudh N,Bhaskarjyoti Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAG discovery methods using expert priors with ALM penalty fail due to premature suppression of true edges and objective function symmetry issues; both problems have theoretical solutions but practical limitations remain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current differentiable causal discovery methods use expert priors as forbidden-edge constraints with ALM penalty, assuming data-adaptive relaxation will override incorrect priors when contradicted by data.

Method: Analyzes the "guide, not bind" design with theoretical characterization: 1) Shows sequential penalty-ramping ALM suppresses wrong priors before detection, with three necessary conditions for adaptive relaxation; 2) Proves standard correlation-matching objective creates symmetry between true edges and reverses; introduces covariance matching alternative.

Result: 1) DADU relaxation rule violates all three necessary conditions, causing suppression of true edges in 87-97% of trials across 3,072 runs; 2) Correlation matching ties edge directions identically at 2r² cost while covariance matching provides provable separation by at least w₀⁴.

Conclusion: The "guide, not bind" design fails for two independent reasons: premature suppression of true edges and objective function symmetry. While fixes exist (conditions for relaxation and covariance matching), complete restoration of the approach is only partial.

Abstract: Differentiable causal discovery methods increasingly encode expert priors as forbidden-edge constraints enforced by an Augmented Lagrangian (ALM) penalty, on the assumption that a data-adaptive relaxation mechanism will discount and eventually override a rule the data consistently contradicts. We show this design, which we call \emph{guide, not bind}, fails for two independent, precisely characterized reasons, and that directly repairing both restores it only partially. First, sequential penalty-ramping ALM suppresses a wrongly-forbidden true edge before any counterfactual check can detect it: we give three necessary conditions any adaptive relaxation must satisfy to avoid this (Proposition~\ref{prop:conditions}), prove that DADU---the natural relaxation rule this paper introduces as the object of study---violates all three (Corollary~\ref{cor:dadu_failure}), and confirm the failure across 3{,}072 training runs spanning graphs from 4 to 32 nodes, where a single wrong prior suppresses a true edge in 87--97\% of trials under DADU. Second, and independent of any fix to the mechanism, we prove in closed form that the standard correlation-matching objective ties a true edge and its reverse to an identical cost of exactly $2r^2$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:tie}), not because the underlying equal-variance model is unidentifiable, but because normalizing to correlation discards exactly the variance information that would make it identifiable; covariance matching instead separates the two directions by a provable margin of at least $w_0^4$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:separation}).

</details>


### [112] [Beyond Straightness: Non-Crossing Flow Matching via Quantile AlignTree Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03443)
*Junyi Lin,Mengyu Li,Jingxuan Hu,Kejun He,Cheng Meng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QAT-FM is an efficient Flow Matching method that uses quantile-aligned tree structures for structured source-target coupling, achieving O(Nd log N) construction cost with improved path separation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flow Matching performance depends on source-target coupling quality. Independent coupling causes path crossings and local velocity ambiguity, while optimal transport (OT) couplings have high construction costs.

Method: Proposes Quantile AlignTree Flow Matching (QAT-FM), which constructs hierarchical coupling between Gaussian prior and target distribution via quantile-aligned tree structure. Achieves O(Nd log N) construction time with O(d) per-pair sampling complexity.

Result: QAT-FM satisfies marginal consistency, induces non-crossing linear interpolation paths, improves path separation at intermediate times compared to independent coupling, alleviates local velocity ambiguity. Achieves competitive generative performance while substantially reducing coupling construction cost across diverse benchmark datasets. Extends naturally to conditional generation with structured conditional coupling.

Conclusion: QAT-FM provides an efficient structured coupling strategy for Flow Matching that balances computational efficiency with improved path separation, enabling scalable training for large-scale high-dimensional generative tasks.

Abstract: The performance of Flow Matching largely depends on the quality of the coupling between the source and target distributions. However, independent coupling often leads to path crossings and local velocity ambiguity, while OT-based couplings typically incur high construction costs. To address this challenge, we propose Quantile AlignTree Flow Matching (QAT-FM), an efficient structured coupling strategy that constructs a hierarchical coupling between a Gaussian prior and the target data distribution via a quantile-aligned tree structure. QAT-FM constructs the coupling in $\mathcal{O}(Nd\log N)$ time and supports per-pair source sampling with $\mathcal{O}(d)$ complexity, enabling scalable training for large-scale high-dimensional generative tasks. Theoretically, we prove that the QAT coupling satisfies marginal consistency, induces non-crossing linear interpolation paths, and consistently improves path separation at intermediate times compared with independent coupling, thereby alleviating local velocity ambiguity. QAT-FM further extends naturally to conditional generation, enabling structured conditional coupling while preserving global Gaussian alignment. Experiments across diverse benchmark datasets demonstrate that QAT-FM achieves competitive generative performance while substantially reducing coupling construction cost.

</details>


### [113] [A Two-Stage Forecasting System for CPU Workload Prediction in Private Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03457)
*Ashir Javeed,Anton Borg,Håkan Grahn,Lars Lundberg,Dhyey Patel,Sogand Shirinbab*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage cloud resource forecasting model that first predicts service requests (TPS), then estimates CPU workload from TPS, using XGBoost with adaptive online retraining to handle concept drift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cloud resource forecasting approaches directly estimate CPU workload from historical traces, overlooking the relationship between customer service demand and subsequent resource consumption. This leads to suboptimal forecasting for proactive resource provisioning, QoS maintenance, and cost reduction in dynamic cloud environments.

Method: Proposes a two-stage integrated forecasting model: (1) forecast customer service requests (Transactions Per Second - TPS), (2) estimate future CPU workload from TPS forecast. Uses XGBoost for both components in a cascaded learning architecture. Incorporates adaptive online retraining with expanding-window strategy to address concept drift in evolving cloud workloads.

Result: Evaluation on real-world traces from 10 private cloud applications shows robust performance: SMAPE below 7% for most applications, with best application achieving MAE=0.7372, RMSE=1.1866, SMAPE=3.57%, R2=0.9185. Horizon-wise drift analysis confirmed stable recursive forecasting across 60-step prediction horizon with controlled error accumulation.

Conclusion: The proposed two-stage model outperforms conventional direct CPU forecasting by providing improved forecasting robustness, computational efficiency, and interpretability. This makes it well-suited for proactive resource management and intelligent auto-scaling in cloud computing environments.

Abstract: Accurate cloud resource forecasting is essential for proactive resource provisioning, maintaining Quality of Service (QoS), and reducing operational costs in dynamic cloud environments. The existing forecasting approaches predominantly estimate future CPU workload directly from historical resource traces, which often overlook the relationship between customer service demand and subsequent resource consumption. This study proposes a two-stage integrated forecasting model that explicitly models this dependency by first forecasting customer service requests, expressed as Transactions Per Second (TPS), and subsequently estimating future CPU workload from the TPS forecast. Both the forecasting component and resource prediction component employed the XGBoost model within a cascaded learning architecture, complemented by adaptive online retraining using an expanding-window strategy to address concept drift in continuously evolving cloud workloads. The proposed work was evaluated using real-world traces collected from a private cloud environment comprising ten applications. Experimental results demonstrate robust forecasting performance by achieving Symmetric Mean Absolute Percentage Error (SMAPE) below $7\%$ for most applications, with the best-performing application achieving an MAE of $0.7372$, RMSE of $1.1866$, SMAPE of $3.57\%$, and an R2 of $0.9185$. Horizon-wise drift analysis confirmed stable recursive forecasting behavior with controlled error accumulation across a 60-step prediction horizon. Compared with the conventional direct CPU forecasting method, the proposed two-stage integrated model gives improved forecasting robustness, computational efficiency, and interpretability, making it well-suited for proactive resource management and intelligent auto-scaling in cloud computing environments.

</details>


### [114] [Mind the Gap: Robustness Risks in PII Detection Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03464)
*Adeel Zafar,Slawomir Nowaczyk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A study revealing that current PII detection systems suffer significant performance degradation under realistic distribution shifts, despite strong benchmark scores, with different architectures showing complementary failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern PII detection systems report strong benchmark performance, but these evaluations mask substantial robustness failures under realistic distribution shifts encountered in real deployment scenarios, where missed entities constitute direct privacy and security risks.

Method: Constructed a stress test benchmark spanning seven categories of natural distribution shift and evaluated representative systems from three widely deployed architectural families: encoder-based NER (SpaCy), rule-based hybrid detection (Presidio), and generative LLM extraction (Qwen2.5-3B).

Result: All three architectures exhibited significant degradation on out-of-distribution inputs with distinct failure modes: encoder models failed on unseen surface forms and boundary detection, rule-based systems failed on non-standard formats, and LLMs showed entity-type confusion and generation instability.

Conclusion: Aggregate benchmark scores obscure deployment-critical weaknesses, and no single architecture is uniformly reliable across PII categories. The authors propose a hybrid detection pipeline with QA-driven feedback loop for iterative risk mitigation and release their benchmark for OOD-aware evaluation.

Abstract: Personally Identifiable Information (PII) detection is a foundational component of data protection infrastructure where missed entities constitute direct privacy and security risks. Although modern PII systems report strong performance on standard benchmarks, we show that these evaluations mask substantial robustness failures under realistic distribution shifts encountered in deployment. Rather than comparing state-of-the-art accuracy, we study how different PII detection paradigms fail under noisy, unstructured, and informal inputs. We construct a stress test benchmark spanning seven categories of natural distribution shift and evaluate representative systems from three widely deployed architectural families: encoder-based NER (SpaCy), rule-based hybrid detection (Presidio), and generative LLM extraction (Qwen2.5-3B).
  All three exhibit significant degradation on out-of-distribution inputs, but with distinct and complementary failure modes. Encoder models primarily fail on unseen surface forms and boundary detection, rule-based systems fail on non-standard formats, and LLMs exhibit entity-type confusion and generation instability. These results show that aggregate benchmark scores obscure deployment-critical weaknesses and that no single architecture is uniformly reliable across PII categories. Motivated by these findings, we propose a hybrid detection pipeline with a QA-driven feedback loop for iterative risk mitigation, and release our benchmark to support OOD-aware evaluation of PII systems.

</details>


### [115] [Spectral characteristics of autoencoder parameters as a vector representation of data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03495)
*Maria Nikitina,Anton Bishuk,Oleg Bakhteev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Autoencoder parameters can serve as dense vector representations of training samples, with spectral characteristics revealing statistical relationships between model parameters and data properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the relationship between autoencoder model parameters and the statistical properties of the training data, and to test the hypothesis that model parameters can be viewed as vector representations of corresponding samples.

Method: Theoretical analysis connecting singular values of autoencoder parameter matrices to eigenvalues of the data covariance matrix, plus experimental validation on CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST datasets using spectral characteristics of parameter matrices to create vector representations.

Result: Vector representations derived from autoencoder parameters allow high-accuracy distinction between models trained on different data subsets without complex algorithms or original samples, confirming parameters capture data statistical properties.

Conclusion: Trained autoencoder parameters can indeed be viewed as sample representations, with spectral analysis revealing transfer of information between data space and parameter space.

Abstract: This paper examines the relationship between the parameters of autoencoder models and the statistical properties of the data on which they are trained. Autoencoders are defined as models with an encoder-decoder architecture, trained to reconstruct input data through a compressed latent representation. It is proposed that the model parameters can be viewed as a dense vector representation of the corresponding sample. To test this hypothesis, a theoretical and experimental study is conducted in which a vector representation is formed based on the spectral characteristics of the autoencoder parameter matrices. Theoretical analysis shows that the singular values of the model parameter matrices are related to the eigenvalues of the covariance matrix of the training data, ensuring the transfer of information between the data space and the parameter space. Experimental results on the CIFAR-10 and FashionMNIST datasets confirm that the resulting vector representations allow for a high degree of accuracy in distinguishing between models trained on different data subsets, without resorting to complex vector generation algorithms or using the original samples. These results suggest that the parameters of trained autoencoders can be viewed as sample representations.

</details>


### [116] [Restricted Eigenvalues Beyond Gaussian Width: Threshold Occupancy under Heavy Tails](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03504)
*Shi Fu,Huibo Xu,Qixin Zhang,Dacheng Tao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that heavy-tailed measurement designs can fail to satisfy the restricted eigenvalue (RE) conditions needed for stable recovery, even when they satisfy the same small-ball condition as sub-Gaussian designs, providing a negative answer to an open problem from COLT 2015.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses an open problem from COLT 2015 about whether the same sample complexity laws that hold for sub-Gaussian measurements (governed by Gaussian width) also apply to heavy-tailed designs satisfying a uniform small-ball condition alone. This is important for understanding the theoretical foundations of norm-regularized estimators like Lasso in heavy-tailed settings.

Method: The authors construct counterexamples using polyhedral descent cones to demonstrate failures of RE conditions. They show that constant-width polyhedral descent cones can have zero empirical RE for heavy-tailed designs. They also develop threshold encoding techniques to generalize results to finite range spaces and use Gaussian smoothing to create distributions with arbitrarily poor RE despite having nice properties.

Result: The paper proves that the proposed sample complexity law from the sub-Gaussian case fails for heavy-tailed designs: 1) Constant-width polyhedral descent cones can have zero empirical RE for heavy-tailed measurements; 2) The sharp worst-case sample complexity for fixed threshold VC dimension d is Θ(β⁻¹[d log(1/β) + log(1/δ)]); 3) Heavy-tailed designs can fail pathwise with n ≲ √(p/log p) samples while Gaussian measurements succeed with O(1 + log(1/δ)) samples on the same cone; 4) Distribution-free fallback governed by affine dimension times squared enclosing radius is sharp.

Conclusion: The separation between sub-Gaussian and heavy-tailed designs is fundamental and persists under exact isotropy and all finite moments. The paper provides a systematic negative answer to the COLT 2015 open problem, showing that additional assumptions beyond the small-ball condition are needed for heavy-tailed designs to achieve the same sample complexity guarantees as sub-Gaussian measurements.

Abstract: Restricted eigenvalue (RE) bounds govern stable recovery by norm-regularized estimators. For isotropic sub-Gaussian measurements, the benchmark sample size is $1+w(A)^2$, where $w(A)$ is the Gaussian width of the normalized descent cone. The COLT 2015 open-problem note (Banerjee et al., 2015) asked whether the same law follows for heavy-tailed designs from a uniform small-ball condition alone. We give an explicit and systematic negative answer to the general question as formulated there: the proposed law fails in its full dimension-free, arbitrary-set form, and the missing obstruction is simultaneous threshold occupancy. A constant-width polyhedral descent cone with fixed small-ball constants has zero empirical RE on every sample path up to half the ambient dimension. More generally, every finite range space admits exact threshold encoding in an arbitrarily narrow spherical cap and a lift to a full polyhedral descent-cone section. For every fixed threshold VC dimension $d$, as $β\downarrow0$, the sharp worst-case sample complexity is $Θ(β^{-1}[d\log(1/β)+\log(1/δ)])$. The separation persists under exact isotropy and all finite moments: on the same constant-width cone, Gaussian measurements succeed with $O(1+\log(1/δ))$ samples, whereas an isotropic heavy-tailed design fails pathwise for $n\lesssim\sqrt{p/\log p}$. Gaussian smoothing yields an everywhere-positive $C^\infty$ density while retaining arbitrarily poor RE. Under isotropy, a distribution-free fallback governed by affine dimension times squared enclosing radius is sharp on this family.

</details>


### [117] [An Adversarial Zero-Shot Learning Approach for Anomaly Detection in Multivariate IoT Traffic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03505)
*Mahshid Rezakhani,Tolunay Seyfi,Fatemeh Afghah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel zero-shot domain adaptation framework for IoT anomaly detection using adversarial learning and contrastive loss with sequence-based VAE architecture, enabling cross-domain generalization without labeled data or feature transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IoT networks present unique challenges for anomaly detection due to device diversity, lack of labeled data, and domain variability across different environments and deployments.

Method: Proposes a multivariate time-series anomaly detection framework combining adversarial learning and contrastive loss within a sequence-based VAE architecture. Uses encoder/decoder adaptor layers for domain alignment and destination-based segmentation for IoT traffic modeling.

Result: Comprehensive evaluation on six distinct IoT datasets across 44 transfer scenarios shows strong zero-shot generalization in cross-domain settings and competitive performance against contrastive domain-adaptation baselines.

Conclusion: The framework effectively addresses heterogeneity in IoT deployments and enables privacy-preserving, zero-shot domain adaptation without requiring labeled data or raw feature transfer.

Abstract: Anomaly detection in Internet of Things (IoT) networks presents unique challenges due to the diversity of devices, lack of labeled data, and domain variability across environments. In this paper, we propose a novel framework for multivariate time-series anomaly detection that leverages adversarial learning and contrastive loss within a sequence-based Variational Autoencoder (VAE) architecture. Our method enables zero-shot domain adaptation by jointly optimizing domain-invariant latent representations and semantically structured embedding spaces, without requiring labeled data or raw feature transfer. To address the heterogeneity of IoT deployments, we introduce encoder and decoder adaptor layers that align feature distributions across domains while preserving contextual semantics. Additionally, we propose a destination-based segmentation strategy to better model real-world communication structures in IoT traffic. Our framework is comprehensively evaluated on six distinct datasets spanning industrial, enterprise, general-purpose, smart home, and military automation domains across 44 transfer scenarios. Experimental results demonstrate strong zero-shot generalization in several cross-domain settings and competitive performance against a contrastive domain-adaptation baseline under realistic, heterogeneous, and privacy-constrained IoT conditions.

</details>


### [118] [LongCounsel-8: A Benchmark Suite for Longitudinal Depression Tracking from Multi-Session Counseling Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03507)
*Jiayi Li,Zhaomin Wu,Bingsheng He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LongCounsel-8 is a benchmark suite for tracking depression across multiple counseling sessions, addressing limitations of existing single-session datasets by providing 7,749 five-session trajectories with controlled depression states and realistic counseling patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current depression tracking research is constrained by scarce longitudinal counseling data with standardized session-level labels. Existing resources provide either multi-session conversations without depression labels or single-session labeled interviews, creating a gap for studying symptom progression across sessions.

Method: Combines three techniques: 1) Profile-grounded simulation using real-world client profiles, 2) Empirically informed state construction based on depression trajectories and symptom compositions, and 3) Indirect behavioral realization to express depression states naturally without exposing target labels. Created three independently generated datasets totaling 7,749 five-session counseling trajectories.

Result: Simulated self-reports closely recover the controlled states, confirming label fidelity. Experiments revealed that: 1) Lower single-session score error doesn't guarantee accurate trend identification (improvement/worsening), 2) Existing methods are consistently less reliable on worsening trajectories, and 3) Additional session history may reduce trend prediction accuracy.

Conclusion: LongCounsel-8 establishes a foundation for advancing depression assessment from static, single-session prediction toward reliable longitudinal tracking of mental health changes, enabling better research on how depression evolves across counseling sessions.

Abstract: Tracking depression from multi-session counseling dialogues requires estimating both current symptom severity and how it changes across sessions. Yet progress on this task is constrained by the scarcity of longitudinal counseling data with standardized session-level depression labels. Existing resources typically provide either multi-session conversations without depression labels or labeled interviews in a single session. Building such a benchmark poses three challenges: maintaining longitudinal consistency and diversity, grounding symptom progression in empirical patterns, and expressing controlled depression states naturally without exposing target labels. To address these challenges, we introduce LongCounsel-8, a benchmark suite of three independently generated datasets totaling 7,749 five-session counseling trajectories, grounded in real-world client profiles, depression trajectories, symptom compositions, and counseling patterns. We combine profile-grounded simulation, empirically informed state construction, and indirect behavioral realization to address these challenges. Across the benchmark, simulated self-reports closely recover the controlled states, supporting label fidelity. Experiments on existing depression tracking methods reveal three key findings: (1) lower single-session score error does not guarantee accurate identification of trend, i.e., improvement or worsening; (2) existing methods are consistently less reliable on worsening trajectories; and (3) additional session history may reduce the accuracy of trend prediction. Together, these findings establish LongCounsel-8 as a foundation for advancing depression assessment from static, single-session prediction toward reliable longitudinal tracking of mental-health change.

</details>


### [119] [LeanGRPO: Eliminating Redundant Recomputation in Diffusion RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03528)
*Sijie Wang,Zhiqiang Tan,Xinrui Yang,Shaohuai Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LeanGRPO introduces recompute-free training schedules for diffusion RL, achieving up to 1.83x speedup by eliminating redundant gradient computations in methods like DanceGRPO and FlowGRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion RL methods recompute selected timesteps with gradient tracking after rollout, which is mathematically redundant when using the same backend for both rollout and update. While reusing the same feed-forward backbone could avoid this redundancy, it incurs large memory overhead during rollout.

Method: LeanGRPO restructures the data-parallel layout and introduces two recompute-free training schedules: 1) LeanGRPO-Retain enables gradient tracking during rollout and directly reuses computation graphs and saved activations for backward pass, and 2) LeanGRPO-Reweight enables gradients during rollout but immediately backpropagates each step with provisional advantage, then corrects gradients with true advantage after trajectory completion.

Result: Across FlowGRPO/DanceGRPO with FLUX.1-dev and Wan models, LeanGRPO achieves up to 1.83x end-to-end speedup while preserving the original optimization objective.

Conclusion: LeanGRPO successfully eliminates redundant recomputation in diffusion RL, providing efficient training schedules that maintain optimization objectives while significantly accelerating training speed across different model scales and input sizes.

Abstract: Diffusion reinforcement learning (RL) has recently achieved significant success in post-training image and video generative models. However, most diffusion RL methods, including DanceGRPO and FlowGRPO, recompute selected timesteps with gradient tracking after rollout. Under on-policy training with the same backend for rollout and update, this recomputation is mathematically redundant. Intuitively, the rollout and policy update steps can reuse the same feed-forward backbone to avoid redundant computation, but doing so can incur a large memory overhead during rollout. To address the issue, we present LeanGRPO by restructuring the data-parallel layout and introducing two recompute-free training schedules for trajectory-logprob diffusion RL: (1) LeanGRPO-Retain enables gradient tracking during rollout and directly reuses the resulting computation graphs and saved activations for backward during update, requiring no recomputation; and (2) LeanGRPO-Reweight also enables gradients during rollout, but immediately backpropagates each selected step using a provisional advantage and delays gradient synchronization, then corrects the provisional gradients with the true advantage after the trajectory is completed. These schedules target different model scales and input sizes. Across FlowGRPO/DanceGRPO with FLUX.1-dev and Wan, LeanGRPO achieves up to 1.83x end-to-end speedup while preserving the original optimization objective.

</details>


### [120] [Coupled Scaling: A Representational Accessibility Framework for Neural Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03533)
*Jie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A task-conditioned scaling framework explains neural scaling differences through the interaction between task structure and the geometry accessible to different architecture-optimization systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural scaling theories focus on data geometry or data-model spectra, but fail to explain why systems trained on the same data scale differently with architectural or optimization changes.

Method: Introduces Coupled Scaling framework with a solvable mode-truncation model where loss separates into target energy outside architectural support and unresolved supported tail. Derives residual exponent bounds and specializes to fixed-kernel case for training-time exponent prediction.

Result: Residual exponent lies between best-N supported tail and tail beyond largest completed high-value prefix, with bounds [ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}, γ_{A,T}]. Under bounded off-prefix gain, completed prefix determines rate with α_{A,O,T}=ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}. Framework provides tests: static geometry tracks loss at common budget, multiscale geometry tracks exponent ordering including reversal across tasks.

Conclusion: The framework separates architectural support from finite-budget acquisition, motivates empirical tests for scaling behaviors, and enables factorial testing to measure geometry separately from scaling fits using released emergence trajectories.

Abstract: Existing theories derive neural scaling from data geometry or a specified data-model spectrum, but systems trained on the same data can scale differently when architecture or optimization changes the representations they can efficiently reach. We introduce Coupled Scaling, a task-conditioned framework in which finite-budget scaling depends on the relation between task structure and the geometry accessible to an architecture-optimization system. In a solvable mode-truncation model, loss separates into target energy outside architectural support and an unresolved supported tail. For an arbitrary priority order, the residual lies between the best-N supported tail and the tail beyond the largest completed high-value prefix. If the cumulative-tail and coverage log-rates are $γ_{A,T}$ and $ρ_{A,O,T}$, the residual exponent lies in $[ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T},γ_{A,T}]$. Under bounded off-prefix gain, the completed prefix is rate-determining and $α_{A,O,T}=ρ_{A,O,T}γ_{A,T}$; for $a_{A,T,j}\asymp j^{-b_{A,T}}$, this gives $α_{A,O,T}=ρ_{A,O,T}(b_{A,T}-1)$. A fixed-kernel specialization derives the training-time exponent from the near-zero tail of a task-weighted spectral measure defined independently of the loss fit. The framework separates architectural support from finite-budget acquisition and motivates two tests: static task-relevant geometry should track loss at a common budget, while multiscale geometry should track coupling-specific exponent ordering, including reversal across contrasting tasks. An audit of released emergence trajectories identifies the controls needed for a direct factorial test that measures geometry separately from the scaling fit.

</details>


### [121] [WeatherNext 3: Increasing resolution and performance of global weather models with raw observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03582)
*Stephan Rasp,Boris Babenko,Dominic Masters,Andrew El-Kadi,Samier Merchant,Guy Shalev,Ilan Price,Fred Zyda,Remi Lam,Sasha Shysheya,Matthew Willson,Stratis Markou,Shreya Agrawal,Suhani Vora,Mohammed Alewi Hassen,Sunny Mak,Tom R. Andersson,Megan Bela,Akib Uddin,Nofar Peled Levi,Ben Gaiarin,Ferran Alet,Aaron Bell,Peter Battaglia,Alvaro Sanchez-Gonzalez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WeatherNext 3 is an AI weather model that addresses limitations of current AI weather forecasting by providing higher resolution forecasts, hourly updates using satellite data, and directly incorporating observations rather than just analysis data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI weather models have two key shortcomings: lower spatial/temporal resolution than physics-based models, and exclusive reliance on analysis data which prevents direct use of observations and inherits analysis biases.

Method: WeatherNext 3 uses geostationary satellite data for hourly initialization, achieves 0.1 degree spatial resolution and hourly temporal resolution, learns to predict satellite-derived precipitation and cyclone data, and models sparse station data to make localized predictions conditioned on geography.

Result: Establishes new state-of-the-art for probabilistic medium-range forecasting skill, achieves resolution parity with physics-based models, provides significantly lower error for localized predictions than competing global models, and integrates traditionally separate forecasting stages.

Conclusion: WeatherNext 3 moves operational AI weather forecasting beyond emulating traditional separate stages of data assimilation, forecasting, and post-processing, advancing both performance and granularity for global weather prediction.

Abstract: State-of-the-art AI weather models have shown impressive medium-range forecast skill and computational efficiency, but suffer two key shortcomings: their forecasts have lower spatial and temporal resolution than the best physics-based models and they are exclusively initialized with and trained on analysis data. As a result, they cannot directly make use of observations, and any biases in the analysis are inherited by the forecast. WeatherNext 3 addresses these shortcomings and establishes a new state-of-the-art for probabilistic medium-range forecasting skill. First, WeatherNext 3 generates new forecasts every hour (rather than every 6 hours like traditional global models) by ingesting low-latency geostationary satellite data. Second, WeatherNext 3's temporal and spatial resolution are on par with physics-based global models, with hourly time steps and 0.1 degree resolution for single-level variables, including solar radiation and cloud cover. Third, WeatherNext 3 moves beyond traditional analysis variables by learning to predict satellite-derived precipitation estimates, as well as tropical cyclone and station observations. Modelling sparse station data allows WeatherNext 3 to make 2m temperature and dewpoint predictions at any location and time, conditioned on local geographical features, with substantially lower error than competing global models, even when evaluated against unseen stations. Together, WeatherNext 3's capabilities move operational AI-based weather forecasting beyond emulating the traditionally distinct stages of data assimilation, forecasting and post-processing, which helps to further push the frontier of performance and granularity for global weather prediction.

</details>


### [122] [Neural-Network Maxent: a general extension with learned nonlinearity, applied to time-series for Desert Locust distribution modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03603)
*Alessandro Grassi,Edoardo Kimani Bellotto,Wassim El Azami,Sabrina Outmani,Maximilien Houel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RNN Maxent extends the Maxent framework by replacing hand-crafted feature transforms with a GRU neural network, enabling better capture of temporal dependencies in time-series environmental data for improved species distribution forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Maxent methods flatten time-series covariates into independent features, discarding sequential structure critical for modeling species like Desert Locust whose breeding dynamics depend on rapidly evolving environmental conditions.

Method: RNN Maxent replaces Maxent's fixed feature dictionary with a Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU) neural network trained end-to-end via backpropagation while preserving Maxent's presence-only statistical foundations, background normalization, and probability calibration.

Result: Applied to Desert Locust habitat mapping using 50-day environmental time series with 7-day forecasting gap, RNN Maxent outperformed standard Maxent: ROC AUC 0.862 (std 0.036) vs. 0.792, and F1 score 0.671 (std 0.056) vs. 0.590.

Conclusion: RNN Maxent successfully integrates deep learning with Maxent's statistical framework to better capture temporal relationships in ecological monitoring, offering improved forecasting capability for species distribution modeling with time-series data.

Abstract: Species Distribution Modelling (SDM) is essential for understanding how environmental conditions shape biodiversity, particularly for destructive pests such as the Desert Locust (Schistocerca gregaria), whose breeding dynamics are tightly coupled to rapidly evolving environmental conditions. Maxent has become the dominant method for presence-only data, but its reliance on a linear combination of hand chosen feature transforms limits its ability to capture the nonlinear, temporal relationships common in ecological monitoring, where covariates such as precipitation, soil moisture, and vegetation indices evolve meaningfully over time. Standard implementations flatten time-series covariates into independent features, discarding sequential structure that carries critical signal. We introduce RNN Maxent, an extension of the Maxent framework that replaces the fixed feature dictionary with a neural network, specifically a Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU), trained end to end via backpropagation. The approach preserves Maxent's presence only statistical foundations, background normalization, and probability calibration, differing only in that the nonlinearity is learned from data rather than fixed in advance. We apply RNN Maxent to map suitable habitat for the Desert Locust using 50 day environmental time series derived from ERA5 Land, MODIS, and Sentinel 3, maintaining a 7 day gap between covariates and presence records to yield forecasting behavior. Compared against standard Maxent, RNN Maxent improves performance across metrics (ROC AUC 0.862 std 0.036 vs. 0.792; F1 0.671 std 0.056 vs. 0.590).

</details>


### [123] [On the Interaction Between Model Compression and Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03604)
*Francesco Corti,Dong Wang,Young D. Kwon,Cecilia Mascolo,Olga Saukh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Compressed neural networks maintain accuracy with supervised adaptation but suffer significant performance degradation during test-time adaptation under distribution shift, with the gap increasing with compression level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks need both efficiency (through compression) and adaptability (through test-time adaptation) for real-world deployment. While both compression and TTA are studied separately, their interaction and how compression affects a model's ability to adapt during deployment is poorly understood.

Method: Systematically analyze structured compression's impact on model adaptability under distribution shift. Use ResNet-18 and ViT-Base models on CIFAR-10-C and ImageNet-C datasets. Evaluate multiple compression methods combined with standard TTA techniques. Introduce a diagnostic framework examining representational expressivity and adaptation subspace compatibility.

Result: There's a consistent performance gap: compressed models retain high accuracy with supervised adaptation, but their TTA performance degrades significantly with increasing compression. The degradation stems from reduced representational diversity and structural constraints limiting recoverability. The effects strongly depend on the specific compression method used.

Conclusion: Compression strategies need to be designed with adaptability preservation in mind, as current methods that work well for supervised adaptation don't maintain good test-time adaptation capabilities. The research highlights the importance of considering compression-TTA interaction for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Deep neural networks deployed in the wild must be both efficient and adaptable, requiring model compression and test-time adaptation (TTA). While both are well studied in isolation, their interaction remains poorly understood. We systematically analyze how structured compression affects a model's ability to adapt under distribution shift. Using ResNet-18 and ViT-Base on CIFAR-10-C and ImageNet-C, we evaluate multiple compression methods combined with standard TTA techniques. We introduce a diagnostic framework that examines representational expressivity and adaptation subspace compatibility. Our results reveal a consistent gap: although compressed models retain high accuracy under supervised adaptation, their TTA performance degrades significantly with increasing compression. We show that this stems from reduced representational diversity and structural constraints that limit recoverability. These effects strongly depend on the compression method, highlighting the need to design compression strategies that preserve adaptability.

</details>


### [124] [Local Updates, Global Learning (LUGL): Playing Games with non-incremental Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03660)
*David Milec,Spyridon Samothrakis,Michael Fairbank,Dennis J. N. J. Soemers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LUGL framework enables gradient-boosted trees (LightGBM) to work in RL by decoupling data collection from model fitting, achieving competitive performance with neural networks in game-playing benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks dominate RL despite game states being inherently tabular (discrete actions, categorical identities, structured positions), which should favor tree-based methods like LightGBM that excel in supervised learning for tabular data.

Method: LUGL framework alternates between: 1) Local updates phase - agent plays self-play games and accumulates tabular updates (Q-values, V-values, policies, or regret) in a finite table, 2) Global learning phase - uses the table to train a function approximator (LightGBM) that generalizes to unseen states before resetting the table.

Result: Competitive or superior performance to DQN and DeepCFR across four perfect-information games (Tic-tac-toe, Connect-4, Othello, Hex) and five imperfect-information games (Kuhn's poker, Leduc Hold'em, Liar's Dice, Goofspiel, Flop5 Hold'em). LightGBM-based agents achieve comparable or better results than neural networks.

Conclusion: The RL community's bias toward neural networks may be unwarranted; tree-based methods like LightGBM can achieve competitive performance in game-playing when using the LUGL framework to handle distributional shift issues in online RL.

Abstract: The dominance of Neural Networks (NNs) in RL is partially due to their incremental learning capability, which naturally suits the online, non-stationary nature of self-play training. However, gradient-boosted trees like LightGBM are widely recognised as the state of the art for tabular data in supervised learning, often outperforming NNs in accuracy and efficiency. Game states are inherently tabular---discrete actions, categorical card identities, structured board positions---which makes them an ideal candidate for tree-based methods. We introduce LUGL (Local Updates, Global Learning), a framework that decouples data collection from model fitting, enabling non-incremental learners such as GBTs to operate in RL settings where they would otherwise fail due to distributional shift. LUGL alternates between a local updates phase, where the agent plays self-play games and accumulates tabular updates (Q-values, V-values, policies, or regret values) in a finite table, and a global learning phase, where the table is used to train a function approximator that generalises to unseen states before the table is reset. We test our approach in four standard perfect-information games (Tic-tac-toe, Connect-4, Othello, and Hex) and five imperfect-information games (Kuhn's poker, Leduc Hold'em, Liar's Dice, Goofspiel, and Flop5 Hold'em), and show that our results are competitive with or superior to DQN and DeepCFR. Our experiments demonstrate that the community's strong bias towards NNs in game-playing may be unwarranted, since LightGBM-based agents achieve competitive or superior performance across all tested benchmarks.

</details>


### [125] [Extracting Forgotten Prompts from Targeted Unlearned Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03662)
*Au Ashley Hoi-Ting,Meghdad Kurmanji,William F. Shen,Nicholas D. Lane,Ligang He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new vulnerability in machine unlearning methods where forgotten prompts can be extracted using black-box access to the model, with Targeted Active Search (TAS) achieving high recovery rates using far fewer queries than naive approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unlearning methods use refusal alignment to suppress forgotten data, but refusal responses leave traces that can be exploited. Previous attacks focused on recovering forgotten answers assuming known prompts, but this paper identifies that forgotten prompts themselves can be extracted.

Method: Proposed Targeted Active Search (TAS): 1) Identifies forgotten entities by constructing canonical templates and entity pool, selectively querying the model with the most informative template-entity pairs under limited query budget. 2) Once entities are identified, instantiates prompt templates with those entities to probe the unlearned model and reconstruct forgotten prompts.

Result: Experiments across three unlearning methods with three datasets and three LLMs show TAS recovers forgotten entities with 100% accuracy and reconstructs up to 95% of forgotten prompts, while using up to 99.7% fewer queries than naive probing.
